L-function genera and applications
David Kazhdan and Andrei Okounkov

Abstract




To Enrico Arbarello

1
Overview

1.1

Our goal in these notes is to produce an accesible, leisurely and, hopefully,
engaging introduction to the ideas, objects, and techniques that are used in our papers

[
27
,
28
]
. The problem studied in these papers has to do with
spectral analysis of automorphic forms, and thus fundamentally
a problem in analysis. Using well-established techniques in spectral
theory,
see Appendix
7
for an elementary discussion,
the sought spectral decomposition
may be reduced to the study of certain contour integrals involving

\zeta
-functions and more general L-functions. These special
functions of number theory appear in what may be described as the
Eisenstein series analog of the reflection coefficients from the theory
of
1
-dimensional many-body systems. Only very basic properties of L-functions play a role in what
happens, thus reducing the problem further to an essentially combinatorial
problem of dealing with Weyl groups, roots, and many residues in
contour integrals.

This sounds like a great progress until one
realizes how much complexity the intermediate stages of
this analysis involve. Langlands devotes pages 181–195 of his
monumental treatise
[
L
]
to the analysis of the basic contour
integral for the group
G_{2}
. In this case, the integral is
2-dimensional, corresponding to the rank of the group, the integrand
is a sum of 12
terms indexed by the elements of the Weyl group, and singularities of
the integrand are
on 6 hyperplanes where the positive roots take value 1. The reader
will be no doubt dazzled by Langlands ability organize the computation
using carefully chosen notation, tables, etc., and to identify one
unexpected point in the spectrum. In light of subsequent insights by
Langlands, Arthur, and many other prominent mathematicians, this
unexpected point is explained by the existence of an
interesting nilpotent conjugacy class for type
G_{2}
, which is its own
Langlands dual
G_{2}^{\vee}=G_{2}
.

At this point,
it may be worth mentioning, that the group
E_{8}
has Weyl group of
order 696729600 and 120 polar hyperplanes, which intersect in
basically all possible ways inside an 8-dimensional space. By
contrast, it has only 70 nilpotent conjugacy classes
in total. It may be therefore highly desirable to have a more direct
link between spectral decomposition and nilpotent elements in the
Langlands dual Lie algebra. The first objective of this paper is
to explain in elementary terms such direct link found by us in

[
27
]
.

1.2

One can interpret the contour integral in question as the pairing of a
certain distribution
\Psi_{\textup{Eis}}
with a pair of holomorphic test
function. In this way, the spectral decomposition may be recast into
a problem of finding a special additive decomposition of the
distribution

\Psi_{\textup{Eis}}
. We solve this problem with geometric tools.

First, we interpret the distribution
\Psi_{\textup{Eis}}
as a certain
nonproper pushforward in equivariant K-theory, or cohomology,
depending on the characteristic of the global field
\mathbb{F}
.
Recall that for a group
\mathsf{H}
acting on a space
\mathsf{X}
,
the equivariant K-theory
K_{\mathsf{H}}(\mathsf{X})=K(\mathsf{X}/\mathsf{H})
is a ring over

K(\pt/\mathsf{H})=\textup{conjugation-invariant functions on
$\mathsf{H}$}\,,

(1)

and proper pushforwards
\mathsf{X}/\mathsf{H}\to\pt/\mathsf{H}
produce elements of
(
1
). It should be considered well-known, see e.g.
[
Atiyah
]
, but is nonetheless explained at length in Section

2
, that some nonproper
pushforwards are well-defined as linear functionals on
(
1
), that is, as conjugation-invariant
distributions
on
\mathsf{H}
.
In particular,
\Psi_{\textup{Eis}}
was identified in
[
27
]
as a certain
equivariant
genus
of the quotient stack
\mathscr{T}
from
(
44
) below.

Since it is a genus, any decomposition of
\mathscr{T}
in a suitable
equivariant cobordism group results in an additive decomposition
of
\Psi_{\textup{Eis}}
. By construction, the stack
\mathscr{T}
maps to the stack of nilpotent
elements in
\Lie(\mathsf{G}^{\vee})
, where the group
\mathsf{G}^{\vee}
is Langlands
dual to the reductive group
\mathsf{G}
for which the original
spectral problem was posed. We show in
[
27
]
that the
additive decomposition of
\mathscr{T}
and
\Psi_{\textup{Eis}}
induced by the decomposition of

\Lie(\mathsf{G}^{\vee})
into conjugacy classes
is precisely the right one for the spectral decomposition.

1.3

The analysis in
[
27
]
was about Eisenstein series induced from the trivial
automorphic form on the minimal parabolic
\mathsf{B}\subset\mathsf{G}
.
The genus that appears in
\Psi_{\textup{Eis}}
in this special case encodes the

\zeta
-function of the field
\mathbb{F}
. In the more general situation
[
28
]
,

\zeta
-functions are replaced by L-functions.

The second objective of this note is to introduce and explain the notion of
L-genera associated to algebraic actions of Galois groups
\Gamma
on smooth
algebraic varieties, or, more generally, virtually smooth algebraic stacks.
This occupies the bulk of Section
3
.
While L-genera may be given a number of definitions applicable in
slightly different contexts, the following one may have the
largest geometric appeal.

Let a group
\Gamma
act on a smooth manifold
M
. By passing to
cotangent bundles if necessary, it is convenient to assume that

\Gamma
preserves a sympletic form on
M
.
The
\Gamma
-equivariant cobordism class of

M
stores a lot of data. For every closed subgroup
\Gamma^{\prime}\subset\Gamma
, it remembers the cobordism class of the fixed
locus
M^{\Gamma^{\prime}}
, together with the restriction of the tangent
bundle
TM\big|_{M^{\Gamma^{\prime}}}
and the action of the normalizer of
\Gamma^{\prime}
on
both. The groups
\Gamma
considered in this paper have a
distinguished normal subgroup
\Gamma^{\prime}\triangleleft\Gamma
, called
the inertia subgroup. We denote the
\Gamma^{\prime}
-fixed locus by
\mathsf{Fix}\subset M
. On this fixed locus, we may consider the vector bundle formed by

T^{1}_{\textup{vir}}\mathsf{Fix}\overset{\textup{\tiny def}}{=}H^{1}(\Gamma^{\prime},TM\big|_{\mathsf{Fix}})\,.

As the notation indicates, this should be viewed as a part of the
virtual tangent bundle to the fixed locus. In fact, by duality (
35
)
for
H^{i}(\Gamma^{\prime},\textup{---})
, this is the only interesting part of the
virtual tangent bundle.

Multiplication in cohomology and the
symplectic form combine to give
T^{1}_{\textup{vir}}\mathsf{Fix}
a nondegenerate
symmetric pairing, which we can use to define a bundle
\Cliff
of
Clifford algebras on
\mathsf{Fix}
. Assuming the existence of the
corresponding self-dual spinor bundle
\Spin
, we define

\widehat{\mathsf{L}}_{\frac{1}{2}}(M)=\textup{index of $\Spin$}=\chi(\mathsf{Fix},\Spin_{0}-\Spin_{1})\,,

where we have explicitly separated the
\mathbb{Z}/2
-graded bundle
\Spin

into its even and odd parts. The index above is equivariant with
respect to the action of
\Gamma/\Gamma^{\prime}
and whatever commutes
with the action of
\Gamma
, hence defines a distribution on the
corresponding groups.

It is curious to note that even for the trivial action of
\Gamma
on

M
this procedure outputs a
nontrivial genus of
M
. For instance, for a trivial action of

\Gamma=\pi_{1,\textup{alg}}(C)
, where
C
is curve over a finite
field, this gives the genus corresponding to the completed

\zeta
-function of the curve

C
.

We believe L-genera is a useful notion beyond their use in spectral
analysis of Eisenstein series. Some potential applications are briefly
discussed in Section
4
.

1.4

Theorem
1
in Section
4
identifies the distribution

\Psi_{\textup{Eis}}
with a certain L-genus for general split unramified Eisenstein
series over a function field. It has a formal analog of number fields
which may be proven and applied without referring to the action of
\Gamma
.
While, fundamentally, it is little more
than a repackaging of classical formulas, see
[
L
,
Labesse
,
MW
]
, it certainly provides a very useful angle of approach
to spectral analysis.

It is natural to explore the deeper meaning of this
identification, including its possible categorification, by which we
mean identifying the role played by the bundle
\Spin
itself, without
passing to its K-theory class.

We explore a basic example of
such categorification in Section
5
, where we deal with
Eisenstein series for the group
\mathsf{G}=PSL(2)
over the field

\mathbb{F}=\Bbbk(C)
of functions on an algebraic curve
C
. This theory
is ultimately about special divisors on
C
. While it gets
wrapped in many layers of reformulations in the process, we hope that both
its geometric origins and the geometric output contained in
Theorem
2
will appeal to Enrico’s taste in mathematics,
and his passion for everything concerning
the geometry of algebraic
curves.

Theorem
2
categorifies Theorem
1
for
PSL(2,\Bbbk(C))

and may be also viewed as the first baby step in broadening the
fruitful interactions of between classical enumerative geometry and modern
high-energy physics in the direction of arithmetic enumeration.
A very brief and superficial discussion of
these ideas may be found in Section
4.3
.

In principle, the ideas presented in Section
5
apply to
the Eisenstein series itself, not just its inner product with another
Eisenstein series (which is what is captured by the distribution

\Psi_{\textup{Eis}}
). We find, however, that the moduli spaces that enter the
categorification of
\Psi_{\textup{Eis}}
, constant terms, and related
quantities are better-behaved than their fibers responsible for the values
of the
Eisenstein series at points. This may be the geometric explanation of
why it is easier to access the Eisenstein series via harmonic analysis
than directly from definitions.

1.5

To our surprize and delight, following the logic of Theorem
2
,
we came into a very close proximity of several classical geometric
representations theory results related to Eisenstein series. In particular, the conjecture of
[
Semiinf2
]
, proven in
[
Gaits
]
, expresses
the stalks of Eisenstein series for the curve
C=\mathbb{P}^{1}
in terms of
semi-infinite cohomology of tilting modules for quantum groups at
roots of unity. The latter can also be described as local cohomology of an irreducible perverse coherent sheaf on the nilpotent cone by
[
Bez
,
BezPos
]
. It seems clear that the
powerful techniques developed in these and related papers
may turn out to be very handy for the eventual
generalization of our Theorem
2
to groups
\mathsf{G}
other
than
PGL(2)
.

While a technical investigation of these connections is perhaps best
left for future research, it may be worthwhile to highlight some aspects in
which our line of approach may be shifting the center of mass of the argument
from representation theory to geometry. Since we deal with the curve

C
of arbitrary genus, the group
H^{1}(C)
, with its Frobenius action,
has to play the central role for us. The descendants of

H^{1}(C)
and
H^{2}(C)
generate a Clifford algebra
correspondences, which we denote by
\mathscr{A}^{\geq 1}(C)
in this paper.
The algebra
\mathscr{A}^{\geq 1}(C)
, on the
one hand, uniquely determines the modules in questions, while on
the other hand, is as close to commutative as one can get in
representation theory. In other words, one can work with
\mathscr{A}^{\geq 1}(C)
and its modules as if they were usual objects of algebraic
geometry.

1.6

In this text, we try to intertwine several related threads on
very different levels of abstraction. While some sections of this narrative
may be rather heavy on technicalities, we hope to have provided a
sufficient number of easy reading interludes and examples to keep the
reader engaged. We have even written a completely elementary
introductory discussion of some aspects of automorphic forms for
readers lacking such background. See Appendix
7
for that.

1.7

We would like to dedicate these modest notes to Enrico Arbarello as a
token of our great appreciation for the influence he had on algebraic
geometry, as well as on the people who work in this field and its
applications. We imagine Enrico would agree with the point of view
that a good way to solve
a mathematical problem, be it purely theoretical or applied, is to interpret and
solve it geometrically. This is precisely what we tried to do in the context
of spectral analysis of Eisenstein series.

1.8

We would like to thank many people for very interesting and enlightening
conversations about the topics discussed here. Among them, Roman
Bezrukavnikov, Alexander Braverman, Misha Finkelberg, Johan de Jong,
Mikhail Kapranov, Davesh Maulik,
Hiraku Nakajima, Nikita Nekrasov,
Martin Olsson, Alexander Polishchuk, Will Sawin, and Leon Takhtajan.
A.O. thanks
the Simons Foundation for being supported as Simons Investigator.

We
think about Igor Krichever, who’s untimely passing left an enormous void
in mathematics and life for us. We cherish the memory of many related
and unrelated discussions with Igor in both recent and distant past.

2
Distributions from geometry

2.1
Characters as distributions

2.1.1

Let
V
be a finite-dimensional complex representation of a group
\mathsf{G}
. Its
character

\chi_{V}(g)=\tr_{V}g

is a conjugation-invariant function on
\mathsf{G}
, which satisfies

\chi_{V}=\chi_{V_{1}}+\chi_{V_{2}}\,,

(2)

for any exact sequence

0\to V_{1}\to V\to V_{2}\to 0

of
\mathsf{G}
-modules.

2.1.2

If
\mathsf{G}
is a complex reductive group, equivalently, the complexification of
a compact Lie group
\mathsf{K}
, then
\chi_{V}
determines
V

completely. Indeed, the multiplicity of an irreducible representation

V^{\mu}
in
V
is given by

[V:V^{\mu}]=\int_{\mathsf{K}}\chi_{V}(k)\,\overline{\chi_{\mu}(k)}\,d_{\textup{Haar}}k\,,

(3)

where
\chi_{\mu}=\chi_{V^{\mu}}
and the Haar measure on
\mathsf{K}
is normalized to have total mass
1
.

2.1.3

Fundamental to study of infinite-dimensional representations is the
idea that the character of a infinite-dimensional representation
V
,
while not defined as a function, may be well-defined as a
distribution so that, in particular, the equality (
3
) is
preserved as a pairing

\left\langle\chi_{V},\chi_{\mu}\right\rangle=\left[V:\left(V^{\mu}\right)^{*}\right]\,,

(4)

of a test function
\chi_{\mu}

with the distribution
\chi_{V}
.

To avoid a possible confusion, we should stress that
in this paper we are not interested in traces of unitary and
other truly infinite-dimensional representations of reductive
groups. Representations that we consider are direct sums of
finite-dimensional irreducible representations with finite
multiplicities. In particular, test functions
\chi_{\mu}
are
analytic functions on
\mathsf{G}
.

2.1.4

For example, if
V
is the regular representation
\mathbb{C}[\mathsf{G}]
of a complex
reductive group
\mathsf{G}
then the decomposition

\mathbb{C}[\mathsf{G}]=\bigoplus_{\textup{irreducible $V^{\mu}$}}\End(V^{\mu})

gives
[\mathbb{C}[\mathsf{G}]:V^{\mu}]=\dim V^{\mu}=\chi_{\mu}(1)
. Thus

\chi_{\mathbb{C}[\mathsf{G}]}=\delta_{1}\,,

(5)

where
\delta_{1}
is the
\delta
-function at the identity
1\in\mathsf{G}
.

2.1.5

More generally, the multiplicity

[V:V^{\mu}]=\dim\Hom_{\mathsf{G}}(V^{\mu},V)\,,

which we assume to be finite,
may be upgraded to a representation of the centralizer of
\mathsf{G}
in

GL(V)
. If
\mathsf{G}\times\mathsf{H}
acts on
V
and

(g,h)\in\mathsf{G}\times\mathsf{H}
then

\left\langle\chi_{V}(gh),\chi_{\mu^{*}}\right\rangle=\tr_{\Hom_{\mathsf{G}}(V^{\mu},V)}h\,,

(6)

defines a linear functional on
\mathbb{C}[\mathsf{G}]^{\Ad}
with values in
\mathbb{C}[\mathsf{H}]^{\Ad}
.

For example, the group
\mathsf{G}\times\mathsf{G}
acts in
\mathbb{C}[\mathsf{G}]
by left
and right shifts and

\left\langle\tr_{\mathbb{C}[\mathsf{G}]}g\times h,\chi_{\mu}(g)\right\rangle=\chi_{\mu}(h)\,.

Since we pair only with conjugation-invariant functions of
g
, we get

\tr_{\mathbb{C}[\mathsf{G}]}g\times h=\delta_{g\sim h}\,,

(7)

where
g\sim h
means that
g
and
h
have the same
eigenvalues, that is, the same image in
\Spec\mathbb{C}[\mathsf{G}]^{\Ad}
. In
other words,
\delta_{g\sim h}
is the delta-function at the point

h
in
\Spec\mathbb{C}[\mathsf{G}]^{\Ad}
.
This generalizes (
5
).

2.1.6

In the example (
7
),
one can observe the following general phenomenon. If
h
lies in a
fixed neighborhood
\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}
of a maximal compact subgroup of
\mathsf{H}
then
the traces (
6
) grow with a fixed exponential rate as
function of the highest weight
\mu
. Therefore,
\chi_{V}(gh)
may be
paired with test functions
f(g)
analytic in a sufficiently large
neighborhood
\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{G}}
of
\mathsf{K}\subset\mathsf{G}
. The sizes of
\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}

and
\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{G}}

are correlated in general and simply equal in the particular
example (
7
).

In number-theoretic
applications,
\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{H}}
is bounded in terms of the data like the
cardinality
q
of a finite field etc. We will thus use the word

distributions
to describe linear functionals on functions
analytic in a certain fixed neighborhood
\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{G}}
of
\mathsf{K}\subset\mathsf{G}
.

A typical example of such linear functional for
\mathsf{G}=(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{r}\owns z
is

f(z)\mapsto\int_{\gamma}f(z)\,w(z,h)\,\prod\frac{dz_{j}}{2\pi iz_{j}}

(8)

where
w
is meromorphic in
z
and

\gamma\in H_{r}(\mathsf{G}\setminus\{\textup{poles of $w$}\})
. In
particular,
\delta
-functions and their derivatives can be written in
this form by the Cauchy integral formula.
Clearly, in this example,
\mathscr{U}_{\mathsf{G}}
has to be large enough to contain a
representative of
\gamma
.

2.1.7

It is possible and important to also consider the generalizations in
which the trace (
6
), while being infinite,
converges for a given
h\in\mathsf{H}
. This means that the multiplicities
of irreducibles for
\mathsf{G}\times\mathsf{H}
are finite, and furthermore
satisfy certain bounds in the
\mathsf{H}
-direction.

2.2
Euler characteristics as distributions

2.2.1

The regular representation of
\mathsf{G}
in

\mathbb{C}[\mathsf{G}]=H^{0}(\mathscr{O}_{\mathsf{G}})={H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(\mathscr{O}_{\mathsf{G}})\,,

where
\mathscr{O}_{\mathsf{G}}
is the sheaf of functions on the algebraic variety
\mathsf{G}
,
belongs to the very broad class of representations arising in the
cohomology groups

H^{i}(\mathsf{X},\mathscr{F})
, where
\mathsf{X}
is a scheme or an algebraic stack with the
action of
\mathsf{G}
and
\mathscr{F}
is a
\mathsf{G}
-equivariant coherent sheaf on

\mathsf{X}
.

Informally, one can say that we replace a finite-dimensional
representation of
\mathsf{G}
by a family
\mathscr{F}_{x}
of vector spaces (in generally, of
varying dimension) parametrized by the points of
\mathsf{X}
, on which the
group
\mathsf{G}
acts by linear isomorphisms
\mathscr{F}_{x}\xrightarrow{\,\sim\,}\mathscr{F}_{gx}
. Taking
\mathsf{X}
to be a point recovers the previous
setup.

2.2.2

In order to maintain the basic additivity (
2
) over short
exact sequences

0\to\mathscr{F}_{1}\to\mathscr{F}\to\mathscr{F}_{2}\to 0\,,

(9)

one has to combine the individual cohomology groups into the Euler characteristic

\chi(\mathscr{F})=\sum_{i}(-1)^{i}H^{i}(\mathscr{F})\,,

(10)

which is a virtual representation of
\mathsf{G}
. We will identify this
representation with its characters, viewed as a distribution on

\mathsf{G}
. Here we assume that the multiplicities of irreducibles in
\chi(\mathscr{F})

are finite.

2.2.3

Despite the fact that we are only interested in
the Euler characteristic
\chi(\mathscr{F})
, for the technical development of
the subject it is convenient to assume that all groups
H^{i}(\mathsf{X},\mathscr{F})

have finite multiplicities of irreducibles for all
\mathsf{G}
-equivariant
coherent sheaves
\mathscr{F}
. Since we can always replace
\mathscr{F}
by
a tensor product
\mathscr{F}\otimes V^{\mu}
, this is equivalent to
requiring that

\dim H^{i}(\mathsf{X},\mathscr{F})^{\mathsf{G}}<\infty

for all
i
and all
\mathscr{F}\in\Coh_{\mathsf{G}}(\mathsf{X})
. This, in turn, can be
phrased as saying the the pushforward map

\mathsf{X}/\mathsf{G}\to\pt\,,

(11)

from the quotient stack
\mathsf{X}/\mathsf{G}
to the point takes complexes of
coherent sheaves to complexes with coherent cohomology. By definition,
this means that the map (
11
), equivalently the stack

\mathsf{X}/\mathsf{G}
itself, is
cohomologically
proper

[
12
]
.

Cohomological properness is a very convenient technical assumption satisfied by
many practically important examples. It may be thought as an algebraic
extension of the notion of
transversally elliptic operators

introduced and studied by Atiyah
[
Atiyah
]
and many authors since.
As already observed by Atiyah, the index of a transversally elliptic
operator is naturally a distribution, of which

\chi(\mathscr{F})
is a direct algebraic analog.

2.2.4

Evidently, the map
\mathscr{F}\to\chi(\mathscr{F})
factors through
the K-theory
K_{\mathsf{G}}(\mathsf{X})
of
\mathsf{G}
-equivariant coherent sheaves on

\mathsf{X}
, which is a module over

K_{\mathsf{G}}(\pt)=\mathbb{Z}[\mathsf{G}]^{\Ad}\,.

For concrete spaces
\mathsf{X}
that we have in mind, there is no
difference between algebraic and topological K-theory. In any
case, one can always pass to the
numerical
K-theory, in which
one quotients out by classes
\mathscr{F}
such that
\chi(\mathscr{F}\otimes\mathscr{V})=0

for any locally free sheaf
\mathscr{V}
.

2.2.5

The pairing of
\chi(\mathscr{F})
with the test functions

\chi_{\mu}\in K_{\mathsf{G}}(\pt)
may be phrased in terms of the diagram

\textstyle{\mathsf{X}/\mathsf{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{2}}
\scriptstyle{p_{1}}
\textstyle{\pt/\mathsf{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pt}

(12)

as follows

\left\langle\chi(\mathscr{F}),\chi_{\mu}\right\rangle=p_{2*}(\mathscr{F}\otimes p_{1}^{*}(\chi_{\mu}))\,.

(13)

In this formulation,
\mathsf{X}/\mathsf{G}
may be replaced by any other stack
mapping to
\pt/\mathsf{G}
so that the composed map
p_{2}
is
cohomologically proper. In the presense of a commuting action of
a group
\mathsf{H}
, the pairing (
13
) takes values in

K_{\mathsf{H}}(\pt)
.

2.2.6

Note that the pairing (
13
) is defined over
\mathbb{Z}
regardless
of the field of definition of
\mathsf{X}
. To apply continuity and other
analytic arguments to distributions, it is convenient to extend
the scalars to

K_{\mathsf{H}}(\pt)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{C}=\mathbb{C}[\mathsf{H}]^{\Ad}\,.

As to the field of definition of
\mathsf{X}
, it is convenient to assume
it has characteristic zero,
but it may very well be the algebraic closure
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}
of
the field

\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}
of
\ell
-adic rationals, the typical ground field in the study of Galois
representations.

We also assume that

\mathsf{G}
is a split reductive group over the same ground field, although the
reader should bear in mind that
\mathsf{G}
in the ongoing discussion is
different from reductive groups considered in the automorphic and
Galois representation contexts. For our concrete applications, the we will have

\mathsf{G}=\mathsf{A}\times\mathsf{A}
, where
\mathsf{A}
is a maximal torus is the
centralizer of a Galois representation,
\mathsf{X}
will be the fixed locus
of the inertia subgroup, and

\mathsf{H}
will be the reductive group
generated topologically by the Frobenius automorphism.

2.3
Scissor relations

2.3.1

In addition to being additive over short exact sequences
(
9
),
Euler characteristics
\chi(\mathsf{X},\mathscr{F})
are also additive over
\mathsf{X}
in
the following sense.
Given a
\mathsf{G}
-stable closed
\mathsf{Y}\subset\mathsf{X}
, there is
a long exact sequence

\dots\to H^{i}_{\mathsf{Y}}(\mathsf{X},\mathscr{F})\to H^{i}(\mathsf{X},\mathscr{F})\to H^{i}(\mathsf{U},\mathscr{F})\to H^{i+1}_{\mathsf{Y}}(\mathsf{X},\mathscr{F})\to\dots\,,

(14)

in which
\mathsf{U}=\mathsf{X}\setminus\mathsf{Y}
and the groups
H^{i}_{\mathsf{Y}}(\mathsf{X},\mathscr{F})
are
local cohomology

groups. These are algebraic analog of the relative cohomology and
one should view the long exact sequence (
14
) as coming from
a distinguished triangle of spaces

\textstyle{\mathsf{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathsf{U}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{\mathsf{U}}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{X}/\mathsf{U}=\Cone(\iota_{\mathsf{U}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\,.}
\scriptstyle{[1]}

(15)

Local cohomology is easiest to compute when
\mathsf{X}
and
\mathsf{Y}
are
smooth, in which case
\Cone(\iota_{\mathsf{U}})
may be identified with the Thom space

\Thom(\mathsf{Y}\to\mathsf{X})
of the embedding of
\mathsf{Y}
into its normal
bundle.

2.3.2

It is convenient to write, by definition,

\chi(\Thom(\mathsf{Y}\to\mathsf{X}),\mathscr{F})=\sum(-1)^{i}H^{i}_{\mathsf{Y}}(\mathsf{X},\mathscr{F})\,,

and one can, in principle, declare the Cech complex that computes
the local cohomology of
\mathscr{O}_{\mathsf{X}}
to be the structure sheaf of
\Thom(\mathsf{Y}\to\mathsf{X})
.
It follows from (
14
) that if any two among
\mathsf{X}/\mathsf{G}
,

\mathsf{U}/\mathsf{G}
, and
\Thom(\mathsf{Y}\to\mathsf{X})/\mathsf{G}
are cohomologically proper
then so it the third.

Tautologically, we have

\chi(\mathsf{X},\mathscr{F})=\chi(\mathsf{U},\mathscr{F})+\chi(\Thom(\mathsf{Y}\to\mathsf{X}),\mathscr{F})\,,

which means that from the point of view of Euler characteristics,

\mathsf{X}
is the sum of
\mathsf{X}\setminus\mathsf{Y}
and
\Thom(\mathsf{Y}\to\mathsf{X})
. It
is
therefore convenient
to introduce equivalence classes
[\mathsf{X}]
of schemes or stacks that satisfy

\left[\mathsf{X}\right]=\left[\mathsf{X}\setminus\mathsf{Y}\right]+\left[\Thom(\mathsf{Y}\to\mathsf{X})\right]\,.

2.3.3
Example

Many key features of the general story are already clearly visible in
the following simplest example. Let
z\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}=\mathsf{G}
act on

\mathsf{X}=\mathbb{C}^{n}
by multiplication by
z
. Then
z
acts by multiplication
by
z^{-d}
on homogeneous polynomials of degree
d
, and therefore

\chi(\mathscr{O}_{\mathsf{X}})=1+nz^{-1}+\binom{n+1}{2}z^{-2}+\dots=(1-z^{-1})^{-n}\,,\quad|z|>1\,.

(16)

Equivalently,

\left\langle\chi(\mathscr{O}_{\mathsf{X}}),\phi(z)\right\rangle=\oint_{|z|=c>1}\frac{\phi(z)}{(1-z^{-1})^{n}}\,\frac{dz}{2\pi iz}\,.

(17)

The fact that (
16
) converges for
|z|>1
and that,
correspondingly, the integral in (
17
) goes outside of the
pole at
z=1
can be traced to the fact that
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
contracts
\mathsf{X}
to
a proper variety (here, the point at the origin) as
z\to 0
.

2.3.4
Example, continued

Now take
\mathsf{Y}=\{0\}\subset\mathsf{X}
. The Cech complex computing the
local cohomology is the product of the elementary complexes

\mathbb{C}[x_{i}]\to\mathbb{C}[x_{i}^{\pm 1}]

(18)

over all coordinates
i=1,\dots,n
. The map in (
18
) is the
obvious embedding. We conclude

H^{i}_{\mathsf{Y}}(\mathscr{O}_{\mathsf{X}})=\begin{cases}\left(\prod x_{i}^{-1}\right)\mathbb{C}[x_{1}^{-1},\dots,x_{n}^{-1}]\,,\quad&i=n\,,\\
0\,,\quad&\textup{otherwise}\,.\end{cases}

Therefore

\displaystyle\chi(\Thom(0\to\mathbb{C}^{n}),\mathscr{O})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n}\cdot\left(z^{n}+nz^{n+1}+\binom{n+1}{2}z^{n+2}+\dots\right)

\displaystyle=(1-z^{-1})^{-n}\,,

\displaystyle\quad|z|<1\,.

(19)

The equality of (
16
) and (
19
) as
rational functions follows from localization theorem in equivariant
K-theory. The fact that now the trace converges for
|z|<1
can
again be traced to the fact that the normal bundle
N_{\mathsf{X}/\mathsf{Y}}
is
has positive
z
-weights.

2.3.5
Example, continued

The action of
\mathsf{G}=\mathbb{C}^{\times}
on
\mathsf{U}=\mathbb{C}^{n}\setminus\{0\}
is free and

\mathsf{U}/\mathsf{G}=\mathbb{P}^{n-1}\,.

From definitions, we have

\langle\chi(\mathbb{C}^{n}\setminus\{0\},\mathscr{O}),z^{m}\rangle=\chi(\mathbb{P}^{n-1},\mathscr{O}(m))\,,

(20)

which we can rephrase as

\langle\chi(\mathbb{C}^{n}\setminus\{0\},\mathscr{O}),\phi(z)\rangle=\chi(\mathbb{P}^{n-1},\phi(\mathscr{O}(1)))\,.

(21)

In (
21
),
\phi(\mathscr{O}(1))
is computed from a
Taylor series of
\phi
at
z=1
. This is well-defined
due to the relation
(\mathscr{O}(1)-1)^{n}=0
in
K(\mathbb{P}^{n-1})
and
makes (
21
) a distribution supported at
z=1
.

2.3.6
Example, summarized

We see that the decomposition

[\mathbb{C}^{n}/\mathbb{C}^{\times}]=[\mathbb{P}^{n-1}]+[(\Thom(0\to\mathbb{C}^{n})/\mathbb{C}^{\times}]\,.

(22)

amounts to the
equality

\oint_{|z|=c>1}\frac{\phi(z)}{(1-z^{-1})^{n}}\,\frac{dz}{2\pi iz}=\chi(\mathbb{P}^{n-1},\phi(\mathscr{O}(1)))+\oint_{|z|=c^{\prime}<1}(\textup{same as in LHS})\,.

(23)

This means that the scissor relation (
22
) replaces
picking up residue while moving the contour of
integration.

2.3.7

Of course, the operation of picking up residues while moving the contour of
integration appears all over mathematics and natural sciences. In
particular, a very popular approach to spectral decompositions,
including spectral decomposition of Eisenstein series, uses this step
in an essential way. See, for instance, Appendix
7
for an elementary
example.

The advantages of lifting an additive relation between distributions
to an additive relation between spaces becomes
particularly apparent for groups of rank more than one.
Distributions of the form (
8
) coming from

\chi(\mathsf{X},\mathscr{F})
have the schematic form

w(z,h)\,dz=\frac{p(z,h)}{\prod_{i}(1-h^{\alpha_{i}}z^{\beta_{i}})}\prod\frac{dz_{j}}{2\pi iz_{j}}\,,

(24)

where
p(z,h)
is a polynomial and
z^{\beta_{i}}

and

h^{\alpha_{i}}
are weights of
\mathsf{G}
and
\mathsf{H}
, that is,
characters of their maximal tori. Each
exponent in (
24
) is a integer vector of dimension
equal to the rank of the corresponding group.

The monomials
z^{\beta_{i}}h^{\alpha_{i}}

are the weights of the
maximal torus of
\mathsf{G}\times\mathsf{H}
acting in the tangent
space to the fixed locus
\mathsf{X}^{\mathsf{G}\times\mathsf{H}}
. This fixed locus is
often 0-dimensional, which means that is has

\chi_{\textup{top}}(\mathsf{X})

many components and each contributes
\dim\mathsf{X}

terms to the denominator. The weight
w(z,h)
has
usually a much simpler conceptual form when written as a sum over
fixed loci, but this sum can very large indeed (think order of the
Weyl
group many terms any time we see a flag variety) and have many more
poles than the collected expression.
All this leads to a combinatorial explosion in the
group
H_{\textup{middle}}(\mathsf{G}\setminus\{\textup{poles of $w$}\})
, which in turn
results in unwieldy outputs from the
direct application of the residue formula. The eventual dramatic
simplification of these outputs appears miraculous in such
approach.

Scissor relations, in general, cross many poles at once and
automatically account for a multitude of geometric
relations satisfied by distributions of the form
\chi(\mathsf{X},\mathscr{F})
.
In addition to the additivity in both
\mathscr{F}
and
\mathsf{X}
,
these relations include the basic principle that

\textup{$\supp\mathscr{F}$ is proper}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\textup{$\chi(\mathscr{F})$ has no singularities}\,,

(25)

which in practical terms amounts to cancellation of all poles in
rather complex rational functions that give an equivariant
localization computation of
\chi(\mathscr{F})
.

2.4
Remarks

2.4.1

A given
\mathsf{X}
can be cut in pieces in many different ways and
it is natural to ask if there is some guiding principle to finding
good decompositions.

If
\mathsf{G}
preserves an ample line bundle
\mathscr{L}
on
\mathsf{X}
then one
can use geometric invariant theory to partition
\mathsf{X}
into
the unstable and the semistable locus (which is what we did
in Example (
2.3.4
)), and then further
partition the unstable locus into various strata according
to their degree of instability. Such stratification has been
introduced and used by many authors in slightly different
settings, see Chapter 5 in
[
VinPop
]
for a textbook
exposition.

Different choices of
\mathscr{L}
produce interesting linear
relations between distributions. For instance, in the example
above, the unstable locus can be the origin or all of
\mathbb{C}^{n}
,
depending on the weight of
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-action on
\mathscr{L}=\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{C}^{n}}
.
However, what we have found especially illuminating, is
to compare the GIT stratifications for
\mathsf{G}
and
\mathsf{H}
.

2.4.2

One can study the diagram (
12
) in multiplicative cohomology
theories
{\mathsf{h}}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(\textup{---})
other than equivariant K-theory.
The cohomology
{\mathsf{h}}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(\pt/\mathsf{G})
becomes the
ring of test functions for the distributions
p_{1,*}\mathscr{F}
,

\mathscr{F}\in{\mathsf{h}}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(\mathsf{X}/\mathsf{G})
.

The key ingredient of
{\mathsf{h}}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(\textup{---})
is
\spec{\mathsf{h}}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(\pt/\mathbb{C}^{\times})
, which
is a commutative algebraic group since
\pt/\mathbb{C}^{\times}
is a group up to
homotopy. What we have found in
[
27
]
is that the natural choice for
this group
in the automorphic context is

\spec{\mathsf{h}}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(\pt/\mathbb{C}^{\times})=\Hom(\mathscr{Q}_{\mathbb{F}},\mathbb{C}^{\times})\,,

(26)

where
\mathscr{Q}_{\mathbb{F}}
is the image of the norm map
\|\,\cdot\,\|
on the
adeles of
\mathbb{F}
. Concretely,

\mathscr{Q}_{\mathbb{F}}=\textup{image of $\|\,\cdot\,\|$ in $\mathbb{R}_{>0}$}=\begin{cases}q^{\mathbb{Z}}\,,&\ch F>0\,,\\
\mathbb{R}_{>0}\,,&\ch\mathbb{F}=0\,.\end{cases}

(27)

Here
q
is the cardinality of the constant subfield
\Bbbk\subset\mathbb{F}=\Bbbk(C)
and the number field case may be viewed as the

q\downarrow 1
limit. Formula (
26
) means that

{\mathsf{h}}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(\textup{---})
is the equivariant K-theory for
a global function field and the equivariant cohomology
for a global number field.

2.4.3

As linear
q
-difference equations are growing in importance in
various areas of mathematics and theoretical physics, so does
the interest in integral solutions of these equations.

Formally, an integral is a pushforward of some other
q
-difference
module, and in this way one aims to describe an interesting
q
-difference
module as an image of a simpler one. The simplest possible
linear
q
-difference equation is a rank
1
equation

f(qz)=r(z)f(z)\,,

(28)

where
r(z)
is a rational function of the variable
z\in\mathbb{C}
. The
equation (
28
) may be solved explicitly in terms of
the
q
-analog

\Gamma_{q}(z)=\frac{1}{(1-z)(1-qz)(1-q^{2}z)(1-q^{3}z)\dots}\,,\quad|q|<1\,,

of the
\Gamma
-function. The product converges for all
z\neq 1,q^{-1},q^{-2},\dots
and solves

\Gamma_{q}(qz)=(1-z)\Gamma_{q}(z)\,.

One notes that

\Gamma_{q}(z)=\chi(\mathscr{O}_{\mathsf{X}})\,,\quad\mathsf{X}=\Maps(\mathbb{C}\otimes q\to\mathbb{C}\otimes z^{-1})\,,

(29)

where we consider polynomial maps (that is, just polynomials in this
case), and the group
\left(\mathbb{C}^{\times}\right)^{2}\owns(q,z)
act in the
source and target of these maps with
indicated weights. Here and in what follows we denote
by
\otimes q
,
\otimes z^{-1}
, etc., the twist of an action on a linear space by
a character of the group.

It is easy to guess from (
29
) that one can solve
interesting
q
-difference equations via K-theoretic
computations on

\mathsf{X}=\Maps(\mathbb{C}\otimes q\to\textup{some target})\,,

which is indeed the case, see for instance
[
41
]
. There are
natural stratifications of the spaces of maps coming from
a stratification of their target or from some notion of stability
for maps, and all these play a certain role in the development
of the subject.

3
\zeta
-function and
L
-function genera

3.1
Genera

3.1.1

The dependence of
\chi(\mathsf{X},\mathscr{F})
on two separate arguments is
artificial as, with minimal assumptions about
\mathsf{X}
, every such
quantity may be naturally expressed as a linear
combination of
\chi(\mathscr{O}_{\mathsf{Y}})
, where
\mathsf{Y}\subset\mathsf{X}
is an
invariant closed subvariety or a substack. These satisfy a version of scissor
relations from Section
2.3
, as well as relations
imposed by equivariant deformations and flat limits.

3.1.2

If
\mathsf{X}
is smooth, one can put
\chi(\mathscr{O}_{\mathsf{X}})
into the richer
framework of
genera
as follows. Let
\psi(x)
be a
function of one variable
x
, which we will take to be
a Laurent polynomial with coefficients in a ring
R
to start. Denoting by
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
, where

n=\dim\mathsf{X}
, the Chern roots of the tangent bunde
T\mathsf{X}
, we
can define

\psi(T\mathsf{X})=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\psi(x_{i})\in K_{\mathsf{G}}(\mathsf{X})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}R\,.

Recall that Chern roots of a bundle
\mathscr{V}

or rank
r
are the coordinates in the target of the map

\Spec K_{\mathsf{G}}(\mathsf{X})\to S^{r}\mathbb{C}^{\times}=\Spec K(\pt/GL(r))\,,

induced on K-theories by the map
\mathsf{X}\to\pt/GL(r)
that
classifies
\mathscr{V}
.

3.1.3

For cohomologically proper
\mathsf{X}
, we define

\psi(\mathsf{X})=\chi(\psi(T\mathsf{X}))\in\Hom(K_{\mathsf{G}}(\pt),R)\,.

In addition to the obvious deformation invariance,
these are preserved by an arbitrary
\mathsf{G}
-equivariant bordism.
Further, we have

\psi(\mathsf{X}_{1}\times\mathsf{X}_{2})=\psi(\mathsf{X}_{1})\times\psi(\mathsf{X}_{2})

(30)

provided one of the factors is proper, which means that in the right-hand side
of (
30
) we have a product of a function and a distribution.
We also have

\psi(\mathsf{X})=\psi(\mathsf{X}\setminus\mathsf{Y})+\psi(\Thom(\mathsf{Y}\to\mathsf{X}))\,,

(31)

when
\mathsf{Y}
is smooth and either (equivalently, both) spaces on
the right are cohomologically proper.

3.1.4

Many spaces in algebraic geometry, while not smooth, have a
virtual
1
1

1

While the literature on virtual
tangent bundles and virtual structure sheaves is really vast and
advanced, old references like
[
Fan
]
or Section 5.3 in

[
OP
]
may still be useful as
an introductory discussion of the subject.

tangent bundle
T_{\textup{vir}}\mathsf{X}\in K_{\mathsf{G}}(\mathsf{X})
. For example,

\mathsf{X}
could be cut out by a section of a vector bundle
\mathscr{V}
in
some ambient smooth space
\mathsf{X}^{\prime}
, in which case

T_{\textup{vir}}\mathsf{X}=\left(T\mathsf{X}^{\prime}-\mathscr{V}\right)\Big|_{\mathsf{X}}\,.

Since

\psi(\mathscr{V}_{1}-\mathscr{V}_{2})=\psi(\mathscr{V}_{1})\big/\psi(\mathscr{V}_{2})\,,

(32)

the quantity
\psi(T_{\textup{vir}}\mathsf{X})
is a rational function on
the spectrum of
K_{\mathsf{G}}(\mathsf{X})
. To push it forward to a distribution,
we need to make sure it is regular on a appropriate open set. It is
convenient to discuss this issue in the context of analytic K-theory
classes.

3.1.5

In general, let

\mathscr{U}\subset\Spec K_{\mathsf{G}}(\pt)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{C}=\textup{maximal torus}\big/\textup{Weyl group}

be an open neighborhood of the image of the compact torus,
and let

\AK_{\mathsf{G}}(\pt)\supset K_{\mathsf{G}}(\pt)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{C}

be some algebra of analytic
functions on
\mathscr{U}
. The notation is short for analytic K-theory
classes. The projection
K_{\mathsf{G}}(\pt)\to\mathbb{Z}

onto
\mathsf{G}
-invariants extends to the map
\AK_{\mathsf{G}}(\pt)\to\mathbb{C}
given by
the Weyl integration formula. This gives a natural embedding

\AK_{\mathsf{G}}(\pt)\hookrightarrow\Hom(\AK_{\mathsf{G}}(\pt),\mathbb{C})

of functions in distributions.

For any
\mathsf{G}
-space
\mathsf{X}
, we can define

\AK_{\mathsf{G}}(\mathsf{X})=K_{\mathsf{G}}(\mathsf{X})\otimes_{K_{\mathsf{G}}(\pt)}\AK_{\mathsf{G}}(\pt)\,.

(33)

Since push-forward is a
K_{\mathsf{G}}(\pt)
-linear operation, these push
forward just like ordinary K-theory classes. That is, if
\mathscr{F}\in\AK_{\mathsf{G}}(\mathsf{X})
then
\chi(\mathscr{F})\in\AK_{\mathsf{G}}(\pt)
if
\mathsf{X}
is proper and

\chi(\mathscr{F})\in\Hom(\AK_{\mathsf{G}}(\pt),\mathbb{C})

if
\mathsf{X}
is cohomologically proper. In particular,

\psi(\mathsf{X})=\chi(\psi(T_{\textup{vir}}\mathsf{X}))

is a well-defined linear functional on
\AK_{\mathsf{G}}(\pt)
if
\psi(T_{\textup{vir}}\mathsf{X})
is regular on the preimage of
\mathscr{U}
.

3.2
\zeta
-function genera

3.2.1

This paper is about certain topological
computations which have applications to arithmetic
questions. Interestingly, we use very little arithmetic information
as an input, making our results applicable to genera associated with
arbitrary functions
that share a few basic properties with
\zeta
-functions and
L-functions.
Since, at the moment, we don’t see any application beyond the arithmetic
situation, we will refer to a function like (
38
) below as a

\zeta
-function of some curve
C
, even though the actual proof
in
[
27
]
only uses the fact that
\alpha_{i}
come in complex
conjugate pairs and that
1<|\alpha_{i}|<q
.

3.2.2

In this paper, will
be concerned with algebraic actions of groups
\Gamma
of the
form

1\to\Gamma^{\prime}\to\Gamma\to\mathscr{Q}_{\mathbb{F}}\to 1

(34)

on algebraic varieties over
\mathbb{f}
. Here
\mathbb{f}=\mathbb{C}
in the number
field case, while in the function field case
\mathbb{f}
can be any algebraically
closed field of characteristic zero. The group
\mathscr{Q}_{\mathbb{F}}\subset\mathbb{R}_{>0}

was defined in (
27
). It is formed
by the norms of the adeles of
\mathbb{F}
.

The exact sequence (
34
) makes

\Gamma
look like a Weil group of a global field. The only
further arithmetic input that we will need to define L-genera is the duality pairing

H^{i}(\Gamma^{\prime},\pi)\otimes H^{2-i}(\Gamma^{\prime},\pi^{\vee})\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt\cup\hskip 8.19447pt}H^{2}(\Gamma^{\prime},1_{\mathbb{f}})=1_{\mathbb{f}}\otimes\hbar

(35)

of
\mathscr{Q}_{\mathbb{F}}
-modules, where
\pi
is a representation of
\Gamma
over

\mathbb{f}
,
1_{\mathbb{f}}
denotes the trivial representation, and

\hbar:\Gamma/\Gamma^{\prime}\to GL(1,\mathbb{f})

(36)

is the tautological character. Concretely

\hbar(\Fr)=q=\begin{cases}\textup{cardinality of the constant field}\,,&\ch F>0\,,\\
1\,,&\ch F=0\,,\end{cases}

(37)

for any element
\Fr\in\Gamma
that projects to the generator of

\mathscr{Q}_{F}
in the function field case and to the canonical infinitesimal
generator
1\in\Lie\mathscr{Q}_{\mathbb{F}}
in the number field. To save on
notation, after these introductory pages, we will identify the
character
\hbar
with its value
q
on the generator of
\mathscr{Q}_{F}
.
This is harmless since
all equivariant computations are in the end evaluated at an element

\Fr\in\Gamma
as above.

By definition, an action of
\Gamma
on an algebraic variety
\mathsf{X}

over
\mathbb{f}
factors through a homomorphism from
\Gamma
to
a linear algebraic group over
\mathbb{f}
acting on
\Aut(\mathsf{X})
, see the
diagram (
52
) below. Without loss of generality, we may
assume that the character
\hbar
extends to a character of the
algebraic group in question and denote it by the same letter.

3.2.3

After these preliminaries, we recall that
a
\zeta
-function of the field
\mathbb{F}=\Bbbk(C)
is obtained by
substituting
t=q^{-s}
into

\zeta_{C}(t)=\prod_{x\in C}(1-t^{\deg x})^{-1}=\frac{\prod_{i=1}^{g}(1-t\alpha_{i})(1-tq/\alpha_{i})}{(1-t)(1-tq)}\in\mathbb{Z}[[t]]\,,

(38)

where
\{\alpha_{i},q/\alpha_{i}\}
are pairs of eigenvalues of the Frobenius
action on
H^{1}(C)
. A slight modification

\xi_{C}(t)=(q^{1/2}t)^{1-g}\zeta_{C}(t)

(39)

satisfies the functional equation

\xi_{C}(q^{-1}t^{-1})=\xi_{C}(t)\,.

(40)

The two poles in (
38
) and the functional equation
(
40
) suggest that
\xi_{C}(t)
can be interpreted as an equivariant
genus of a 2-dimensional vector space on which
a group acts with weights
\{t,q^{-1}t^{-1}\}
.
Concretely, let

\mathsf{Y}=\mathbb{A}^{1}\otimes t\,,

be a one-dimensional representation of
\mathsf{G}
with
character
t
and trivial action of
\mathsf{H}=GL(1,\mathbb{f})
. Let

\hbar:\Gamma\to\mathsf{H}
be the tautological character as before.
We consider

\mathsf{X}=T^{*}\mathsf{Y}

and let the group
\mathsf{H}
act on it by scaling the cotangent fibers with
weight
-1
. The genus

\psi(\mathsf{X})\Big|_{\{\Fr\}\times\mathsf{G}}=\frac{\psi(t)\psi(q^{-1}t^{-1})}{(1-t^{-1})(1-qt)}\,,

has the same poles and the same symmetry as
\xi_{C}(t)
.
We can thus find a Laurent polynomial
\psi_{\mathbb{F}}(t)
such that

\psi_{\mathbb{F}}(\mathsf{X})\Big|_{\{\Fr\}\times\mathsf{G}}=\xi_{C}(t)\,.

(41)

Note that while the polynomial
\psi_{\mathbb{F}}(t)
involves splitting
Frobenius eigenvalues and taking
square roots, these ambiguities cancel out of
(
41
) and its generalization (
42
) below.

A parallel definition works for number fields in cohomology once
one introduces analytic equivariant cohomology classes as
in Section
3.1.5
.

3.2.4

More generally, suppose that
\mathsf{Y}
is smooth but otherwise
arbitrary and consider
\mathsf{X}=T^{*}\mathsf{Y}
. Let
\mathsf{G}
and
\mathsf{H}
act on

\mathsf{Y}
and let us twist the induced action on
\mathsf{X}
by a character

\hbar^{-1}:\mathsf{H}\to GL(1,\mathbb{f})

that scales all cotangent directions. This scales the
canonical symplectic form on
\mathsf{X}
with weight
\hbar
.

The Chern roots of
T\mathsf{X}
form pairs of the form

\{y_{i},\hbar^{-1}y_{i}^{-1}\}
, where
y_{i}
are the Chern
roots of
T\mathsf{Y}
. Clearly,
\psi(\mathsf{X})
depends only on
the combination
\psi(y)\psi(\hbar^{-1}y^{-1})
which is
a function symmetric with respect to
y\mapsto\hbar^{-1}y^{-1}
.
Identifying
\Fr
with its image in
\mathsf{H}
, let

\Fr\in\mathsf{H}
be an element such that
\hbar(\Fr)=q
.
We define

\xi_{\mathbb{F}}(\mathsf{X})=\psi_{\mathbb{F}}(\mathsf{X})\Big|_{\{\Fr\}\times\mathsf{G}}\,.

(42)

This defines a distribution on
\mathsf{G}
provided
the traces of
\Fr
in the
\mathsf{G}
-multiplicity spaces converge as
in (
6
).

3.2.5

The essense of our paper
[
27
]
may be described as
the following link
between genera and spectral problems. Let
\mathsf{G}
be a
split reductive group and let
\mathsf{G}^{\vee}
be the Langlands
dual reductive group. We have the Springer resolution

T^{*}(\mathsf{G}^{\vee}/\mathsf{B}^{\vee})\to\textup{Nilpotent elements $\Nil^{\vee}$}\subset\Lie(\mathsf{G}^{\vee})\,,

(43)

where
\mathsf{B}^{\vee}
is the Borel subgroup. One can take
the symplectic reduction of (
43
) by
\mathsf{B}^{\vee}

and arrive at the quotient stack
\mathscr{T}
with two
canonical maps

\textstyle{\mathscr{T}=T^{*}(\mathsf{B}^{\vee}\backslash\mathsf{G}^{\vee}/\mathsf{B}^{\vee})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{2}}
\scriptstyle{p_{1}}
\textstyle{\pt/(\mathsf{A}^{\vee}\times\mathsf{A}^{\vee})}
\textstyle{\Nil^{\vee}/\Ad\mathsf{G}^{\vee}\,.}

(44)

Here
\mathsf{A}^{\vee}
is the maximal torus of
\mathsf{G}^{\vee}
,
and the canonical map
\mathsf{B}^{\vee}\to\mathsf{A}^{\vee}
is the quotient by
the unipotent radical. Since

K(\pt/(\mathsf{A}^{\vee}\times\mathsf{A}^{\vee}))=\mathbb{Z}[\mathsf{A}^{\vee}\times\mathsf{A}^{\vee}]\,,

the pushforward

\Psi_{\xi_{\mathbb{F}}}=p_{1,*}\xi_{\mathbb{F}}(\mathscr{T})

defines a distribution on two copies of
\mathsf{A}^{\vee}
.

3.2.6

The torus
\mathsf{A}^{\vee}
is the space of parameters for the spherical
Eisenstein
series
\textup{Eis}(a)
induced from the trivial representation of
\mathsf{B}(\mathbb{A})\subset\mathsf{G}(\mathbb{A})
, where
\mathbb{A}
denotes the adeles of the field
\mathbb{F}
.
By
construction, spherical Eisenstein series are certain functions on

\Bun_{\mathsf{G}}=\mathsf{G}(\mathbb{F})\backslash\mathsf{G}(\mathbb{A})/\textup{maximal compact}\,,

which are always eigenfunctions of the Hecke operators (and of the
Laplace operators in the number field context). However, they
are not square-integrable and the problem of the describing
the spectrum of Hecke operators in the intersection of

L^{2}(\Bun_{\mathsf{G}})
with the closure of the span of Eisenstein series
is highly nontrivial.

In his groundbreaking work
[
L
]
, Langlands introduced integral
formulas and shift of the integration contours as tools of spectral
analysis.
the spectrum. These techniques received further important contributions in
the work of many prominent mathematicians, including the work of
C. Mœglin and J.-L. Waldspurger
[
Moe1
,
Moe2
,
MW
]
, who analyzed
the case of classical groups. Volker Heiermann, Marcelo de
Martino and Eric Opdam, starting in

[
13
]
and completing their project in the recent
paper
[
14
]
, extended
the analysis to general split groups.
Their work is focused on the number field case and the minimal
parabolic subgroup. It is computer
assisted in the exceptional cases.

3.2.7

Taking linear combinations of Eisenstein series, one can form the
function

\textup{Eis}_{\omega}=\int_{|a|=\textup{fixed}\gg 1}\textup{Eis}(a)\,\omega(a)\,d_{\textup{Haar}}(a)\,,\quad\omega\in\mathbb{C}[\mathsf{A}^{\vee}]\,,

(45)

known as the pseudo-Eisenstein series, or wave packets in other
contexts, see Appendix
7
for an elementary example.
These are always in

L^{2}(\Bun_{\mathsf{G}})
but never eigenfunctions of the Hecke operators.
Indeed, Hecke operators acts on (
45
) as multiplication by
a
W
-invariant function of
a
under the integral sign. In other
words, we have a commutative diagram

\textstyle{\mathbb{C}[\mathsf{A}^{\vee}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\textup{Eis}_{\omega}}
multiplication by
\mathbb{C}[\mathsf{A}^{\vee}]^{W}
\textstyle{L^{2}(\Bun_{\mathsf{G}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
Hecke
operators
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}[\mathsf{A}^{\vee}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\textup{Eis}_{\omega}}
\textstyle{L^{2}(\Bun_{\mathsf{G}})\,,}

(46)

in which a Hecke operator corresponds to its image in

\mathbb{C}[\mathsf{A}^{\vee}]^{W}
under the Satake isomorphism.

3.2.8

The abstract spectral theorem asserts that a self-adjoint operator
is unitarily equivalent to multiplication by a real-valued function

\lambda
in
L^{2}(\mathbf{S},\bm{\mu})
, where
(\mathbf{S},\bm{\mu})

is a measure space. The spectrum of this operator is then the support
of pushforward
\lambda_{*}(\bm{\mu})
. The joint spectrum of the
Hecke operators in the span of the Eisenstein series is easily seen
to be simple, hence we can always take
\mathbf{S}=\mathsf{A}^{\vee}/W
, with coordinate functions
\lambda
. In this case,
the joint spectrum is the support of
\bm{\mu}
.

While (
46
) conjugates Hecke operators to multiplication by
coordinate functions, it gives no immediate information
about the measure
\bm{\mu}
and, hence, about the spectrum. By
construction, this information is captured by the pullback
of the norm
\|\cdot\|_{L^{2}(\Bun_{\mathsf{G}})}
under
\textup{Eis}_{\omega}
,
and concretely by distribution
\Psi_{\textup{Eis}}

defined by

\left\langle\Psi_{\textup{Eis}},\omega_{1}\otimes\omega^{*}_{2}\right\rangle\overset{\textup{\tiny def}}{=}\left(\textup{Eis}_{\omega_{1}},\textup{Eis}_{\omega_{2}}\right)_{L^{2}(\Bun_{\mathsf{G}})},

(47)

where
\omega^{*}(a)=\bar{\omega}(a^{-1})
.

Our geometric
language really captures the full spectral decomposition, including
the spectral measure and spectral projectors. This is more information
than the spectrum, which
only remembers the measure equivalence of
\mu
.
Even on the most basic level, that of the normalization of measures,
formulas like (
48
) below contain computations equivalent
to Langlands’ computation of the Tamagawa numbers
[
Lvol
]
.

3.2.9

The main results of
[
27
]
may be summarized as follows. First

\Psi_{\textup{Eis}}=\Psi_{\xi_{\mathbb{F}}}\,.

(48)

Second, the spectral
decomposition of
\Psi_{\textup{Eis}}
is induced, via
p_{2}^{*}
and the
scissor relations, from the decomposition into conjugacy
classes of nilpotent elements in (
44
). This works equally well
for function fields and number fields.

Note that by (
46
) the distribution
\Psi_{\textup{Eis}}
descends to
an element

\Psi_{\textup{Eis}}\in\textup{linear dual}\Big(\underbrace{\mathbb{C}[\mathsf{A}^{\vee}]\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[\mathsf{A}^{\vee}]^{W}}\mathbb{C}[\mathsf{A}^{\vee}]}_{K(\mathscr{T})}\Big)\,.

(49)

One may view this as a heuristic reason why it is natural to
expect a formula of the form (
48
).

An elementary example of how (
48
) works may be found
in Appendix
7
. Of course, it may be much more interesting
to explore the deeper meaning of (
48
) and its
generalizations.

3.3
L-function genera

3.3.1

Let
\mathsf{G}
be a reductive group over a global field
\mathbb{F}
, which for
simplicity we will assume to be split.
The very general and powerful philosophy going back to Langlands
aims to describe complex or
\ell
-adic automorphic forms for
\mathsf{G}(\mathbb{A})
in term of
representations

\phi:\Gamma\to\mathsf{G}^{\vee}(\mathbb{f})\,,

(50)

where
\mathbb{f}
denotes the complex numbers
\mathbb{C}
or the
\ell
-adic
algebraic numbers
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}
.
Here
\Gamma
is a
certain enhanced version of the Galois group of
\mathbb{F}
which fits into
an exact sequence of the form (
34
).

In case of
a function field
\mathbb{F}=\Bbbk(C)
,
\Gamma^{\prime}
is the geometric
fundamental group, meaning that

\Gamma^{\prime}=\pi_{1}(C\otimes\overline{\Bbbk})

(51)

in the unramified situation. The extension (
34
) includes
the natural action of the Frobenius automorphism on
(
51
). Galois-theoretic parametrization of
cuspidal automorphic representations in the function field
case is a monumental achievement of V. Lafforgue
[
Laff
]
.
The nature of
\Gamma^{\prime}
in the
number field case is much more mysterious, see for instance
[
Lmarch
]
.

3.3.2

To associate an L-function to a homomorphism (
50
), one
normally composes
\phi
with a linear representation of

\mathsf{G}^{\vee}
. Instead, and this is an important point for us,
one can consider an action of
\mathsf{G}^{\vee}(\mathbb{f})
on an algebraic variety

\mathsf{Y}
over
\mathbb{f}
.

Let
\mathbb{f}
be
an algebraically
closed field of characteristic zero.
We call an action of an abstract group
\Gamma
on an
algebraic variety
\mathsf{Y}
over
\mathbb{f}
algebraic if there is a triangle

\textstyle{\Gamma\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\textstyle{\Aut(\mathsf{Y})\,,}
\textstyle{\mathsf{G}^{\vee}(\mathbb{f})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

(52)

in which
\mathsf{G}^{\vee}
is a linear algebraic group acting
on
\mathsf{Y}
. We denote the ambient group by
\mathsf{G}^{\vee}
because it will
be the Langlands dual group in the context of Eisenstein
series. However, the notion of an algebraic action is abstract and it
is always beneficial to choose
\mathsf{G}^{\vee}
as large as possible in
(
52
). The larger the group, the more variables will the
L-genus potentially involve. For simplicity, we will assume that
\mathsf{G}^{\vee}
and
the Zariski closure
\overline{\phi(\Gamma)}\subset\mathsf{G}^{\vee}
are
reductive.

For algebraic actions of groups of the form
(
34
) on a smooth algebraic variety
\mathsf{Y}
we will define a nonlinear
generalization
\widehat{\mathsf{L}}(\mathsf{Y})
of an L-function as a certain equivariant
genus of the fixed locus
\mathsf{Y}^{\Gamma^{\prime}}
. Note that this fixed locus depends
only on the Zariski closure

\overline{\phi(\Gamma^{\prime})}\subset\mathsf{G}^{\vee}\,.

(53)

The closure (
53
) is reductive by our assumptions, being a normal subgroup of

\overline{\phi(\Gamma)}
. It follows that
\mathsf{Y}^{\Gamma^{\prime}}
is smooth.
In the case of the trivial
action, the construction of
\widehat{\mathsf{L}}(\mathsf{Y})
will recover the
\zeta
-genus

\phi=1\quad\Rightarrow\quad\widehat{\mathsf{L}}(\mathsf{Y})=\xi(T^{*}\mathsf{Y})\,.

(54)

3.3.3

The genus
\widehat{\mathsf{L}}(\mathsf{Y})
will be defined as a distribution on a certain space
of
deformations
of the action (
52
).
Homomorphisms
\phi
in (
52
) come in families in which the restriction
of
\phi
to
\Gamma^{\prime}
stays the same, while the values of
\phi
on
the quotient
\mathscr{Q}_{\mathbb{F}}
are deformed by a map

a:\mathscr{Q}_{\mathbb{F}}\to\mathsf{G}^{\sqbullet}\,,\quad\mathsf{G}^{\sqbullet}=(\mathsf{G}^{\vee})^{\phi(\Gamma^{\prime})}_{\textup{connected}}\,.

The continuous variables
a
will correspond, in particular, to the
continuous parameters of Eisenstein series. This is why we denote them
by the same
letter
a
.

The group
\mathsf{G}^{\sqbullet}
has an action of a commutative reductive
algebraic group

\mathsf{H}^{\sqbullet}=\overline{\phi(\Gamma)}/\overline{\phi(\Gamma^{\prime})}\,,

(55)

The group
\mathsf{H}^{\sqbullet}
has a distinguished semisimple topological generator
\Fr
in
the function field case and a distinguished infinitesimal
generator in the number field case, which we will also denote also by

\Fr
. It is the
\mathsf{G}^{\sqbullet}
-conjugacy class of

\phi(\Fr)a(\Fr)
that matters in the deformation, where
\Fr
is an
arbitrary lift of the Frobenius to
\Gamma
. The element

\phi(\Fr)a(\Fr)\in\mathsf{G}^{\vee}
is understood infinitesimally in the number field
case.

The

\mathsf{G}^{\sqbullet}
-conjugacy classes of semisimple elements in

\Aut(\mathsf{G}^{\sqbullet})
have been studied classically, see Section 3.3.8 in

[
VinOnish
]
. In particular, up to
\mathsf{G}^{\sqbullet}
-conjugacy, the map
a
may
be chosen in the form

\displaystyle a:\mathscr{Q}_{\mathbb{F}}\to\mathsf{A}^{\sqbullet}

\displaystyle=\textup{maximal torus of
$(\mathsf{G}^{\sqbullet})^{\phi(\Fr)}$}

\displaystyle=\textup{maximal torus of $(\mathsf{G}^{\vee})^{\phi(\Gamma)}$}\,.

(56)

While there can be a finite group action further identifying
equivalent deformations, we will focus for now on (
56
) as the
basic space of deformations. We see from (
26
) that it is naturally identified
with
\spec{\mathsf{h}}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(\pt/\mathsf{A}^{\sqbullet})
, or more precisely with the
fiber over
\Fr
in the following diagram

deformations (
56
)
\textstyle{\spec{\mathsf{h}}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(\pt/\mathsf{H}^{\sqbullet}\times\mathsf{A}^{\sqbullet})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Fr\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\spec{\mathsf{h}}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(\pt/\mathsf{H}^{\sqbullet})\,.}

(57)

Concerning this diagram, we recall that
\spec{\mathsf{h}}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(\pt/\mathsf{H}^{\sqbullet})
is
the
group
\mathsf{H}^{\sqbullet}
itself in the function field case and its Lie algebra

\Lie\mathsf{H}^{\sqbullet}
in the number field case.

For example, if
\mathsf{Y}
is an irreducible linear representation of

\mathsf{G}^{\vee}
then
\mathsf{A}^{\sqbullet}
is reduced to the scalars and

\phi(\Fr)a(\Fr)
in the function field case is a scalar multiple of the Frobenius element
that enters familiar formulas for the L-functions.

3.3.4

Consider the fixed locus

\mathsf{Fix}=\mathsf{Y}^{\Gamma^{\prime}}=\mathsf{Y}^{\overline{\phi(\Gamma^{\prime})}}\,.

Since
\mathsf{Y}
was assumed
smooth and
\overline{\phi(\Gamma^{\prime})}
is reductive, it follows that

\mathsf{Fix}
is smooth with the tangent bundle

T\mathsf{Fix}=\left(T\mathsf{Y}\big|_{\mathsf{Fix}}\right)^{\Gamma^{\prime}}\,.

(58)

We define

T_{\textup{vir}}\mathsf{Fix}={H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}\left(\Gamma^{\prime},T\mathsf{Y}\big|_{\mathsf{Fix}}\right)\in K_{\mathsf{H}^{\sqbullet}\times\mathsf{A}^{\sqbullet}}(\mathsf{Fix})\,.

(59)

Instead of taking the K-theory class in (
59
), one can
consider the corresponding complex of
\mathsf{H}^{\sqbullet}\times\mathsf{A}^{\sqbullet}
-equivariant coherent sheaves on
\mathsf{Fix}
. It is also
easy to imagine a generalization in which
T\mathsf{Y}\big|_{\mathsf{Fix}}
is by itself a
complex of
\Gamma^{\prime}
-modules.

3.3.5

In the function field case, Galois actions on
\mathsf{Y}
correspond to local

\mathsf{Y}
-systems over
C
for which
\mathsf{Fix}\subset\mathsf{Y}
are the global flat
sections. Geometrically, global sections are obstructed by higher
cohomology groups, and the virtual tangent space in (
59
)
is the natural virtual tangent space to the space of global
sections.

In the number field case, the authors of this paper do not have
new insights to offer on the nature of the group
\Gamma^{\prime}
.
It seems to us that it is more pragmatic to define the functor

H^{1}(\Gamma^{\prime},\textup{---})
as a infinite-dimensional linear space
with the action of
\Fr
given by the zeroes of the corresponding
L-function.

It may be worth
stressing that the final formulas used in applications show
remarkable uniformity between function fields and number fields
and can be proven and used without those geometric constructions that may or may not
be available in the number field case.

3.3.6

In the function field case, we define

\widehat{\mathsf{L}}(\mathsf{Y})=\chi(\mathsf{Fix},\widehat{\mathscr{O}}_{\mathsf{Fix},\textup{vir}})\Big|_{\Fr}\,.

(60)

Here

\widehat{\mathscr{O}}_{\mathsf{Fix},\textup{vir}}
is the symmetrized virtual structure sheaf corresponding
to the virtual tangent space (
59
), about which we
will say more below. The definition is arranged
so that

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathsf{L}}(\textup{space of a representation $\pi$})

\displaystyle=\widehat{L}(\pi,a)

\displaystyle\overset{\textup{\tiny def}}{=}{\det}_{{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(\Gamma^{\prime},\pi)}\left(a^{1/2}\Fr^{1/2}-a^{-1/2}\Fr^{-1/2}\right)^{-1}\,,

(61)

where
a\in\mathsf{A}^{\sqbullet}
is a scalar operator in
\pi
.
We take formula (
61
) as the definition of the completed
L-function
\widehat{L}(\pi,a)
in the function field case. Observe that
it can be interpreted as an equivariant
\widehat{A}
-genus

\displaystyle\widehat{L}(\pi,a)=\widehat{A}\left(-{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(\Gamma^{\prime},\pi)\right)

(62)

evaluated on the automorphism
a\Fr
. The
\widehat{A}
-genus here
corresponds to the function

\psi_{\widehat{A}}(x)={x^{1/2}-x^{-1/2}}\,,

(63)

and is closely related to indices of Dirac operators, see Section

3.4.2
below. Since

{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(\Gamma^{\prime},\pi)
is even-dimensional, we conclude by Poincare
duality that

\widehat{L}(\pi,a)=\widehat{L}(\pi^{*},q^{-1}a^{-1})\,,

(64)

where
\pi
denotes the dual representation.

3.3.7

As it is always the case with
\widehat{A}
-genera and Dirac operators,
the definition requires fixing some square root, namely the square
root of the determinant line
\Lambda^{\textup{top}}H^{1}(\Gamma^{\prime},\pi)
. This determinant line is, essentially, the

\varepsilon
-factor in the functional equation for the L-functions.
One should treat this line as a line bundle on the space of all
relevant parameters, and fix a square root of this line bundle.

While there is no canonical square root, finding and
fixing a square root is aided by the fact that
\dim H^{1}(\Gamma^{\prime},\pi)
is even for any
\pi
. Indeed, taking a Jordan-Holder
filtration of
\pi
, we may assume that
\pi=\pi^{\prime}\otimes V
, where
\pi^{\prime}
is irreducible and the multiplicity space
V
is a representation of
the centralizer of
\Gamma^{\prime}
. This gives

\det H^{1}(\Gamma^{\prime},\pi)=\left(\det H^{1}(\Gamma^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})\right)^{\dim V}\otimes\left(\det V\right)^{\dim H^{1}(\Gamma^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})}\,,

and hence it suffices to fix the square roots of
\det\Fr|_{H^{1}(\pi)}
for irreducible linear representations
\pi
that
appear in the tangent bundle to
\mathsf{Y}
. From our current
viewpoint, these determinants are simply elements of the algebraically
closed field
\mathbb{f}
, and their square roots may be chosen
arbitrarily. Special considerations, for instance considerations of
self-duality, may be required
to make compatible choices when different actions of
\Gamma^{\prime}
interact.

If a compatible choice of square roots cannot be made,
one can replace the symmetrized sheaf
\widehat{\mathscr{O}}_{\mathsf{Fix},\textup{vir}}
by the
usual virtual structure sheaf
\mathscr{O}_{\mathsf{Fix},\textup{vir}}
, which means
replacing the
\widehat{A}
-genus by the Todd genus. While less
symmetric, this genus still carries an equivalent amount of information.

3.3.8

In the number field case, the requirement that

\widehat{\mathsf{L}}(\textup{space of a representation $\pi$})=\textup{completed L-function of $\pi$}

(65)

uniquely fixes an analytic characteristic class on
\mathsf{Fix}
, which we
push forward to
\pt/\mathsf{H}^{\sqbullet}\times\mathsf{A}^{\sqbullet}
to
obtain the distribution
\widehat{\mathsf{L}}(\mathsf{Y})
. Here completed means that it
includes the Archimedean L-factors and a square root of the

\varepsilon
-factor, which simplify the functional equation to

\widehat{L}(\pi,s)=\widehat{L}(\pi^{*},1-s)\,.

Here the traditional variable
s
corresponds to minus the logarithm of
the coordinate on
\mathscr{Q}_{\mathbb{F}}
.

3.3.9

The symmetrized virtual structure sheaf used in (
60
)
is defined by

\widehat{\mathscr{O}}_{\textup{vir}}=(\det T_{\textup{vir}})^{-1/2}\otimes\mathscr{O}_{\textup{vir}}\,,

(66)

provided a square root of the line bundle
\det T_{\textup{vir}}
exists. Here

\mathscr{O}_{\textup{vir}}
is the usual virtual structure sheaf.

While, in general, there are obstacles to constructing
\mathscr{O}_{\textup{vir}}

when higher obstructions are present in
the virtual tangent space, in the case at hand case the construction of
\mathscr{O}_{\textup{vir}}

readily reduces to the special case of the
\zeta
-genus. Indeed, we have
the decomposition into
\overline{\phi(\Gamma^{\prime})}
-modules

T\mathsf{Y}=T\mathsf{Fix}\oplus T_{\textup{mov}}\mathsf{Y}

(67)

where the second term contains only nontrivial
\Gamma^{\prime}
-modules.
Since nontrivial
\Gamma^{\prime}
-modules have neither
H^{0}
nor
H^{2}
, we
conclude that the contribution of
T_{\textup{mov}}\mathsf{Y}
to
T_{\textup{vir}}\mathsf{Fix}

\textup{Mov}=-{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(\Gamma^{\prime},T_{\textup{mov}}\mathsf{Y})=H^{1}(\Gamma^{\prime},T_{\textup{mov}}\mathsf{Y})

(68)

is a vector bundle of rank
(2g-2)\codim\mathsf{Fix}
. The
trivial representation
T\mathsf{Fix}
, by construction, gives the pushforward of the

\xi_{\mathbb{F}}
-genus under the projection

p:T^{*}\mathsf{Fix}\to\mathsf{Fix}\,.

(69)

We thus conclude

\widehat{\mathsf{L}}(\mathsf{Y})=\chi\left(T^{*}\mathsf{Fix},\xi_{\mathbb{F}}\otimes p^{*}\widehat{A}(\textup{Mov})\right)\,.

(70)

Separating the contribution of
H^{1}
to the
\xi_{\mathbb{F}}
-genus, we can
rewrite (
70
) as follows

Proposition 3.1
.

We have

\widehat{\mathsf{L}}(\mathsf{Y})=(-q^{\frac{1}{2}})^{\dim\mathsf{Fix}}\,\chi\left(T^{*}\mathsf{Fix},p^{*}\widehat{A}\left(H^{1}\left(\Gamma^{\prime},T\mathsf{Y}\big|_{\mathsf{Fix}}\right)\right)\right)\,.

(71)

Proof.

From the equality

\xi_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(t)=\frac{q^{1/2}t}{(1-t)(1-qt)}=\frac{(-q^{1/2})}{(1-1/t)(1-qt)}\,,

we see that the contributions of
H^{0}
and
H^{2}
to the

\xi_{\mathbb{F}}
-genus combine into
(-q^{\frac{1}{2}})^{\dim\mathsf{Fix}}\mathscr{O}_{T^{*}\mathsf{Fix}}
.
∎

3.3.10

What really helps in our situation is that Frobenius acts on the
fibers of (
69
) even if it does not act on the base
\mathsf{Fix}
.
The additional weight
q^{-1}
of the Frobenius action can make
infinite sums appearing the nonproper pushforward (
69
)
converge.

Similarly, Frobenius action on (
68
) helps in the situation
when the tangent spaces
T\mathsf{Y}
and
T_{\textup{mov}}\mathsf{Y}
are already
virtual tangent spaces. As their cohomology acquire an additional
Frobenius weight, it may be possible for
p^{*}\widehat{A}(\textup{Mov})
, which
in this case will be a ratio of two K-theory classes, to
be a well-defined analytic K-theory class in the sense of
Section
3.1.5
.

3.4
Categorification and other
generalizations

3.4.1
Manifolds with a polarization

Since the symplectic manifold
M=T^{*}\mathsf{Y}
appears very prominently in
(
71
), it is natural to extend the applicability of

\widehat{L}
-genera
to other manifolds which may share some key features of
T^{*}\mathsf{Y}

without being cotangent bundles to manifolds with
\Gamma
-action.

In one possible direction, we may define the
\widehat{L}
-genus for any
manifold

M

that admits a
\Gamma\times\mathsf{A}^{\sqbullet}
-equivariant invariant
polarization. As above,
\mathsf{A}^{\sqbullet}
is a torus that commutes with
\Gamma

and forms the arguments of the
\widehat{L}
-genus. By definition, a
polarization
T^{1/2}M
a class in

K_{\Gamma\times\mathsf{A}^{\sqbullet}}(M)
such that

T^{1/2}M+q^{-1}\left(T^{1/2}M\right)^{*}=TM\,.

(72)

For example,
p^{*}T\mathsf{Y}
is a polarization of
M=T^{*}\mathsf{Y}
. We define

\widehat{\mathsf{L}}_{\frac{1}{2}}(M)=(-q^{1/2})^{\frac{1}{2}\dim M^{\Gamma^{\prime}}}\,\chi\left(M^{\Gamma^{\prime}},\widehat{A}\left(H^{1}\left(\Gamma^{\prime},T^{1/2}M\right)\right)\right)\,.

(73)

By the functional
equation (
64
), this is independent of the choice
of the polarization.

3.4.2
Spinor bundles

Note that (
72
) implies that

TM=q^{-1}\,T^{*}M

in
K_{\textup{eq}}(M)
. In examples of interest to us, there exists
a
\Gamma
-invariant
symplectic form
\omega_{M}
on
M
of weight
q

\omega_{M}:\Lambda^{2}TM\to q^{-1}\otimes\mathscr{O}_{M}\,.

(74)

It induces
a natural
symmetric

pairing

S^{2}H^{1}\left(\Gamma^{\prime},TM\right)\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt\cup\otimes\omega_{M}\hskip 8.19447pt}q^{-1}\otimes H^{2}(\Gamma^{\prime},\mathscr{O}_{\mathsf{Fix}})=\mathscr{O}_{\mathsf{Fix}}\,,

(75)

where the cup product is as in (
35
).
In (
75
) and above
\mathscr{O}
stands for the trivial line bundle on
the corresponding spaces. The pairing (
75
) induces a Clifford algebra
structure on
{\Lambda}^{\!\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}\!\,H^{1}\left(\Gamma^{\prime},TM\right)
. We denote this sheaf of algebras by
\Cliff_{M,\Gamma}
and denote by

\Spin_{M,\Gamma}=\Spin_{0}\oplus\Spin_{1}

(76)

the corresponding
\mathbb{Z}/2
-graded self-dual spinor bundle, assuming it
exists. This is a weaker
assumption than the existence of a polarization and of the
corresponding square root in (
73
).
The self-duality of
\Spin
implies that

\Cliff\cong\left(\Spin\right)^{\otimes 2}

as
\mathbb{Z}/2
-graded
vector bundles.
With these preparations, we may define the
\widehat{L}
-genus as the
index

\widehat{\mathsf{L}}_{\frac{1}{2}}(M)=(-q^{1/2})^{\frac{1}{2}\dim M^{\Gamma^{\prime}}}\,\chi\left(M^{\Gamma^{\prime}},\Spin_{0}-\Spin_{1}\right)\,.

(77)

Clearly, this agrees with (
73
) on the common domain of
applicability.

3.4.3
Categorification

Without plunging into categorical depths, we will only discuss
the special situations of interest to us in Section
5
.
We assume that the structure sheaf
\mathscr{O}_{M}
has an even
cohomological degree grading such that the form (
74
)

\omega_{M}:\Lambda^{2}TM\to\mathscr{O}_{M}\left|\!\left[2\right]\!\right|\,,

(78)

shifts the cohomological degree by
2
with a Tate twist.
This is
the case, for instance, for Coulomb branches as constructed in

[
BFN1
,
BFN2
,
BFN3
,
NakCoul
]
, since they are defined as spectra of certain cohomology
rings, see Section
5
for an elementary example. In
(
78
) and it
what follows, we combine the degree shift and Tate twist if the
overall shift is pure and set, by definition

\left|\!\left[n\right]\!\right|=[n]\bm{(}\tfrac{n}{2}\bm{)}\,.

(79)

It follows from (
78
) that the pairing (
75
) lifts to

S^{2}H^{1}\left(\Gamma^{\prime},TM\right)\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt\cup\otimes\omega_{M}\hskip 8.19447pt}H^{2}(\Gamma^{\prime},\mathscr{O}_{\mathsf{Fix}})\left|\!\left[2\right]\!\right|=\mathscr{O}_{\mathsf{Fix}}\,.

(80)

As a result, the Clifford algebra
\Cliff_{M,\Gamma}

is still well-defined and we can ask for a self-dual
spinor bundle
\Spin
on
M^{\Gamma^{\prime}}
which is
\mathbb{Z}
-graded cohomologically.
If such bundle exists, we define

\widehat{\mathscr{O}}_{\mathsf{Fix},\textup{vir}}=\Spin\left|\!\left[-\delta\right]\!\right|\,,\quad\delta=\tfrac{1}{2}\dim M^{\Gamma^{\prime}}\,.

(81)

The cohomological shift

\left|\!\left[-\delta\right]\!\right|=\mathscr{K}^{1/2}_{\mathsf{Fix}}\,,

which appears in (
81
), is the correct categorical
lift of the
(-q^{1/2})^{\delta}
-factor in (
77
).

3.4.4

More generally,
M
can be a Poisson manifold such that

\{\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,\}:\Omega^{2}M\to\mathscr{O}_{M}\left|\!\left[-2\right]\!\right|

(82)

where
\Omega^{2}M=\Lambda^{2}\Omega^{1}M
. In this case,
\Omega^{1}M\left|\!\left[2\right]\!\right|
can be used as a replacement on the tangent bundle in the
definition of
\Cliff_{M,\Gamma}
. We define

\Cliff_{M,\Gamma}=\textup{Clifford}(H\Omega_{M}),\quad H\Omega_{M}=H^{1}(\Gamma^{\prime},\Omega^{1}M)\left|\!\left[2\right]\!\right|\,.

(83)

Note that a
\Gamma
-equivariant
Poisson map

f:M_{1}\to M_{2}

induces a homomorphism
\Cliff_{M_{2}}\xrightarrow{f^{*}}\Cliff_{M_{1}}

and, therefore, a pushforward
f_{*}
for sheaves of Clifford modules.
If
f_{*}
is a symplectic resolution of singularities then
f_{*}\widehat{\mathscr{O}}_{\mathsf{Fix}_{1},\textup{vir}}
may be used as a replacement for the virtual
structure sheaf of
\mathsf{Fix}_{2}
, see an example in Section
5
.

3.4.5
Connected components

Note that L-genera, as defined above, depend only on the first order
neighborhood of
M^{\Gamma^{\prime}}
inside the ambient
space
M
with a
\Gamma
-action. In particular, there is no
interaction
2
2

2

While one may imagine
generalizations that may involve, for instance,
\Gamma^{\prime}
-invariant
curves joining different components of
M^{\Gamma^{\prime}}
, this is not something
we consider in this paper.
between different connected components of

M^{\Gamma^{\prime}}
, beyond the fact that they may be permuted by the action
of
\Fr
. Thus, given a
\Fr
-invariant subset of component
M^{\Gamma^{\prime}}_{1}\subset M^{\Gamma^{\prime}}
we
may define L-genera of
M_{1}
by restricting any of the
definitions above to
M^{\Gamma^{\prime}}_{1}
. Here
M_{1}
may be takes as a
germ of
M
along
M^{\Gamma^{\prime}}_{1}
.

By construction, L-genera are computed on the fiber over
\Fr
in
(
57
). Therefore, any component of
M^{\Gamma^{\prime}}
which is
nontrivially permuted by
\Fr
makes no contribution to the
L-genus. In other words, we can always restrict the computation to the subset of
components that are
\Fr
-stable.

The flexibility to specify a set of components is convenient for the
following pragmatic definition of genera for quotient stacks.

3.4.6
Quotient stacks

For applications to Eisenstein series and other needs, it is
convenient to have a definition of L-genera for stacks, in
particular, for quotient stacks. For quotient stacks, we propose
the following recipe.

Suppose an algebraic action of
\Gamma
on
\mathsf{X}
considered above
normalizes an action of
an algebraic group
\mathsf{H}
on
\mathsf{X}
. In particular,
\Gamma
acts on

\mathsf{H}
by automorphisms, the fixed points of which form the
subgroup
\mathsf{H}^{\Gamma^{\prime}}\subset\mathsf{H}
. For the quotient stack
\mathsf{X}/\mathsf{H}
, we
define

\mathsf{Fix}(\mathsf{X}/\mathsf{H})=\mathsf{X}^{\Gamma^{\prime}}\big/\mathsf{H}^{\Gamma^{\prime}}\,.

(84)

From

T(\mathsf{X}/\mathsf{H})=T\mathsf{X}-\Lie\mathsf{H}

we see that
T_{\textup{vir}}\mathsf{Fix}
gets an extra piece
H^{1}(\Gamma^{\prime},(\Lie\mathsf{H})_{\textup{mov}})
.

While there may be other definitions of the fixed locus for quotient
stacks, for all of them (
84
) will form a subset of the set
of
components. This subset plays a distinguished role in the
application we have in mind.

3.4.7
Example

Let

\phi:\Gamma\to\mathsf{P}^{\vee}

(85)

be a homomorphism and consider the induces action of
\Gamma
on
the flag manifold
\textup{\large Flag}=\mathsf{G}^{\vee}/\mathsf{P}^{\vee}
, which we will view
as a quotient stack. In this example, we will investigate the
difference between the fixed locus of the quotient and the
quotient of fixed loci.

Since a parabolic subgroup is its own normalizer, we have

(\textup{\large Flag})^{\Gamma^{\prime}}=\{\textup{conjugates $\mathsf{P}^{\prime}=g\,\mathsf{P}^{\vee}\!g^{-1}$ that contain
$\phi(\Gamma^{\prime})$}\}\,.

By reductivity of
\overline{\phi(\Gamma^{\prime})}
,

T_{\mathsf{P}^{\prime}}(\textup{\large Flag})^{\Gamma^{\prime}}=\Lie\left(\mathsf{G}^{\vee}\right)^{\Gamma^{\prime}}\big/\Lie\left(\mathsf{P}^{\vee}\right)^{\Gamma^{\prime}}\,.

(86)

It follows that the connected component

\mathsf{G}^{\sqbullet}
of
(\mathsf{G}^{\vee})^{\Gamma^{\prime}}
acts transitively on each connected component of

\textup{\large Flag}^{\Gamma^{\prime}}
. For each connected component, we have a homomorphism

\phi_{g}=g^{-1}\,\phi\,g:\Gamma^{\prime}\to\mathsf{P}^{\vee}\,,

(87)

well-defined up to the conjugation by
\mathsf{P}^{\vee}
. Clearly,

\mathsf{Fix}(\textup{\large Flag})=\left(\mathsf{G}^{\vee}\right)^{\Gamma^{\prime}}\big/\left(\mathsf{P}^{\vee}\right)^{\Gamma^{\prime}}=\left\{\begin{matrix}\textup{$\Gamma^{\prime}$-fixed flags such that}\\
\textup{$\phi_{g}$ is equivalent to $\phi\big|_{\Gamma^{\prime}}$}\end{matrix}\right\}\subset\textup{\large Flag}^{\Gamma^{\prime}}\,.

(88)

Each component of this locus is isomorphic to
\mathsf{G}^{\sqbullet}/\mathsf{P}^{\sqbullet}
, where

\mathsf{P}^{\sqbullet}=\mathsf{G}^{\sqbullet}\cap\left(\mathsf{P}^{\vee}\right)^{\Gamma^{\prime}}
is a parabolic subgroup. Other
components can have different stabilizers, including stabilizers of
different dimensions.

Since only
\Fr
-stable components contribute to the genus, we may
restrict our attention to the intermediate subgroup

\mathsf{G}^{\sqbullet}\subset\mathsf{G}^{\diamond}\subset\left(\mathsf{G}^{\vee}\right)^{\Gamma^{\prime}}\,,

(89)

defined by

\displaystyle\mathsf{G}^{\diamond}

\displaystyle=\left\{\left.g\in\left(\mathsf{G}^{\vee}\right)^{\Gamma^{\prime}}\right|\,g\,\phi(\Fr)\,g^{-1}\,\phi(\Fr)^{-1}\in\mathsf{G}^{\sqbullet}\right\}

(90)

\displaystyle=\{\textup{$g$ such that $g\phi g^{-1}$ is a deformation of $\phi$}\}

\displaystyle=\textup{$\Fr$-stable components of
$\left(\mathsf{G}^{\vee}\right)^{\Gamma^{\prime}}$}\,.

4
Applications

4.1
Additive relations among L-genera

By construction, L-functions behave like a genus, that is

L(\pi_{1}\oplus\pi_{2})=L(\pi_{1})L(\pi_{2})\,,

and similarly for the symmetrized L-functions (
61
).
The genera
\widehat{\mathsf{L}}(\mathsf{Y})
satisfy scissor relations, which are

additive
in the space
\mathsf{Y}
. In particular, the space
V
of an
irreducible linear representation
\pi:\Gamma\to GL(V)
is not irreducible in the
sense of
\Gamma
-actions on algebraic varieties. For example
it always has the trivial orbit
0\in V
, and will typically have
many other
\Gamma
-invariant subvarieties.

Note that these additive relations are understood in the sense of
distributions, which concretely means that they relate different
integrals, of different dimensions, involving L-functions. An
example of such additive relation is the spectral decomposition of the
distribution
\Psi_{\textup{Eis}}
.

4.2
Eisenstein series

4.2.1

In general, Eisenstein and pseudo-Eisenstein series on
\Bun_{\mathsf{G}}

are defined by the
pull-push
\pi_{\mathsf{G},*}\pi_{\mathsf{M}}^{*}
of functions, which we will assume

\mathbb{C}
-valued, in the following
diagram
of spaces

\textstyle{\Bun_{\mathsf{P}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{\mathsf{M}}}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{\mathsf{G}}}
\textstyle{\Bun_{\mathsf{M}}}
\textstyle{\Bun_{\mathsf{G}}\,.}

(91)

Here
\mathsf{P}\subset\mathsf{G}
is a parabolic subgroup and

\mathsf{P}\to\mathsf{M}
is the quotient by the unipotent radical.
