For Eisenstein series, one starts with an automorphic form
\phi
on

\Bun_{\mathsf{M}}
, which we will assume a cuspidal Hecke eigenfunction. Since
\mathsf{M}
has a
nontrivial center and, hence, nontrivial characters, such forms come in
families of the form
\phi(m)\|\chi(m)\|^{s}
, where
\chi
is
a character of
\mathsf{M}
and
s\in\mathbb{C}
is a complex number. More
abstractly, one should view the function
a(m)=\|\chi(m)\|^{s}

as an element of

\displaystyle a\in\spec{\mathsf{h}}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(\pt/\mathsf{A}^{\sqbullet})

\displaystyle=\Chars(\mathsf{M})\otimes\Hom(\mathscr{Q}_{\mathbb{F}},\mathbb{C}^{\times})

\displaystyle=\Cochars(\mathsf{A}^{\sqbullet})\otimes\Hom(\mathscr{Q}_{\mathbb{F}},\mathbb{C}^{\times})\,,

(92)

where
\mathsf{A}^{\sqbullet}
is the center of the Langlands dual group
\mathsf{M}^{\vee}
.
We denote

\textup{Eis}(\phi,a)=\pi_{\mathsf{G},*}\pi_{\mathsf{M}}^{*}\,a\cdot\phi\,,\quad a\in\spec{\mathsf{h}}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(\pt/\mathsf{A}^{\sqbullet})\,.

(93)

In principle, there is a finite group
\pFix(\phi)
that idenfies equivalent choices
of
a
in (
93
). The quotient
{\mathsf{h}}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(\pt/\mathsf{A}^{\sqbullet})/\pFix(\phi)

is the true space of parameters for the Eisenstein series associated
with
\phi
.

The spectral analysis problem mentioned in Section (
3.2.6
)
corresponds to the trivial function
\phi=1
on the minimal parabolic
subgroup
\mathsf{P}=\mathsf{B}
. The general spectral analysis problem can
be posed in the exactly the same way and rephrased, in the
exactly the same way, as finding a certain additive decomposition of
the distribution
\Psi_{\textup{Eis}}
defined by (
47
) on two copies of the
parameter space
{\mathsf{h}}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(\pt/\mathsf{A}^{\sqbullet})
.

4.2.2

For function fields
\mathbb{F}=\Bbbk(C)
, the work of Lafforgue
[
Laff
]
relates
cuspidal Hecke eigenfunctions
\phi
to Galois representations. In the
unramified situation, these are irreducible
representations

\phi:\Gamma\to\mathsf{M}^{\vee}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell})

(94)

of the unramified Weil group

1\to\Gamma^{\prime}=\pi_{1}(C\otimes\overline{\Bbbk})\to\Gamma\xrightarrow{\,\,\|\,\cdot\,\|\,\,}\mathscr{Q}_{\mathbb{F}}\to 1\,.

(95)

The irreducibility of (
94
) means that the image of
\phi

does not lie in any smaller Levi subgroup or, equivalently, that the
center
\mathsf{A}^{\sqbullet}
of
\mathsf{M}^{\vee}
satisfies

\mathsf{A}^{\sqbullet}=\textup{maximal torus of }(\mathsf{M}^{\vee})^{\phi(\Gamma)}

(96)

as in (
56
), with the role of
\mathsf{G}^{\vee}
played by

\mathsf{M}^{\vee}
. Irreducibility of
\phi
also implies that the group

\mathsf{B}^{\sqbullet}=\mathsf{G}^{\sqbullet}\cap\mathsf{P}^{\vee}

is a Borel subgroup of the group
\mathsf{G}^{\sqbullet}
. We recall that

\mathsf{G}^{\sqbullet}\subset\left(\mathsf{G}^{\vee}\right)^{\Gamma^{\prime}}
is defined
as the connected component of the identity.

4.2.3

As in (
47
),
the
L^{2}
-norm of pseudo-Eisenstein series defines a distribution
on two copies of
\mathsf{A}^{\sqbullet}
, which we want to understand with
geometric tools. Consider the quotient stack

\mathscr{T}=T^{*}(\mathsf{P}^{\vee}\backslash\mathsf{G}^{\vee}/\mathsf{B}^{\vee})

(97)

with the induced action of
\Gamma
. As explained in Section

3.4.7
all components of the fixed locus
\mathsf{Fix}\subset\mathscr{T}
are copies
of
T^{*}(\mathsf{B}^{\sqbullet}\backslash\mathsf{G}^{\sqbullet}/\mathsf{B}^{\sqbullet})
. Therefore,
the L-genus in the following theorem defines a distribution on

\mathsf{A}^{\sqbullet}\times\mathsf{A}^{\sqbullet}
.

Theorem 1
.

[
28
]
We have

\Psi_{\textup{Eis}}=\widehat{\mathsf{L}}_{\frac{1}{2}}(T^{*}(\mathsf{P}^{\vee}\backslash\mathsf{G}^{\vee}/\mathsf{P}^{\vee}))\,.

(98)

We recall from Section

3.4.7
that the components of
\mathsf{Fix}
that are
\Fr
-stable,
and hence contribute to
L-genus, are indexed by
\pi_{0}(\mathsf{G}^{\diamond})
,
where the group
\mathsf{G}^{\diamond}
is defined in (
90
).

As before, the fixed locus
\mathsf{Fix}
maps to nilpotent
elements in
\Lie\mathsf{G}^{\sqbullet}
and one can decompose its genus
into the corresponding pieces. When the
the representation
\phi
is
tempered
, this is
entirely parallel to what we did in
[
27
]
, see
[
28
]
.

4.2.4

Note that complex Eisenstein series mesh seamlessly with
\ell
-adic
representation in Theorem
98
because K-theoretic
computations that are involved in the definition of the RHS in
(
98
) are really defined over integers, and not over the field
of the definition of the varieties in questions. If one wants a more
direct interaction between
\ell
-adic and complex manifolds, one
may note that the L-genera are certain tensor functors of the

\Gamma^{\prime}
-isotypic components of the normal bundle to the fixed locus

\mathsf{Fix}
. Both the fixed locus and the isotypic component
depend only on the Zariski closure of

\phi(\Gamma^{\prime})
. Since we assume this closure to be reductive, it can
be uniquely transferred from the
\ell
-adic world to the complex
world.

4.3
L-genera in enumerative problems

4.3.1

A large portion of modern enumerative geometry studies various
moduli spaces of maps
\Maps(C\xrightarrow{\,f\,}\mathsf{X})
from a curve
C
, which may be fixed or
move in moduli, to some target algebraic variety
\mathsf{X}
. For a number
of reasons, ranging from the needs of applications in theoretical
physics to the desire to work with moduli spaces that enjoy certain
properness, one is lead to allow certain singularities for
the maps
f
, about which we will be more specific below. For our
goals, the ability to change the degree of
f
by creating or
removing singularities will play a very important role.

One
sets up things so that, whatever counts one wants to do, these
counts have a well-defined answer for maps of fixed degree

\deg f=[f(C)]\in H_{2}(\mathsf{X},\mathbb{Z})\,,

after which one forms a generating function over all
degrees. Equivalently, one studies the Fourier transform of
(
100
) as function on the
dual torus

\mathsf{A}^{\sqbullet}=H^{2}(\mathsf{X},\mathbb{Z})\otimes\mathbb{G}_{\mathbf{m}}\,.

(99)

The reason we denote this torus by
\mathsf{A}^{\sqbullet}
is the following.
In the examples we have in mind,
\mathsf{X}
is obtained as a quotient
by a parabolic subgroup
\mathsf{P}
and the resulting map

\Cochars(\mathsf{A}^{\sqbullet})=\Chars(\mathsf{P})\to H^{2}(\mathsf{X},\mathbb{Z})\,,

turns out to be an isomorphism.

4.3.2

In the traditional approach, see Part 4 in
[
Mirror
]
for an excellent
introduction, the counts are defined by some variation
on the formula

\left\langle\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\dots\right\rangle_{d}=\int_{\deg f=d}[\Maps]_{\textup{vir}}\cap\bigcup\lambda_{i}\,,

(100)

where the integral denotes
pushforward to a point,

[\Maps]_{\textup{vir}}
denotes a distinguished homology
class called the virtual fundamental cycle, and

\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\dots
are some cohomology classes on
the moduli of maps, including those pulled back from
\mathsf{X}
via
the evaluation maps

\ev_{x}:f\mapsto f(x)\in\mathsf{X}\,,\quad x\in C\,.

(101)

Parallel constructions exist in other
cohomology theories, notably equivariant K-theory.

Because of the
direct link between K-theory and indices of the Dirac operator and other
elliptic operators, computations in equivariant K-theory are particularly close
to the indices which our colleagues in modern high energy physics are computing
in supersymmetric quantum mechanics on
the space of maps
3
3

3

If
\mathbb{X}
represents a
component of the moduli
spaces of vacua in some supersymmetric quantum field theory on
C\times\{\textup{time}\}
, the dynamics of the corresponding modulated vacua may
be described by supersymmetric quantum mechanics on
\Maps(C\to\mathbb{X})
. This is a
natural low-energy
approximation to the full dynamics of the theory, which captures
deformation-invariant quantities like indices.
.

4.3.3

One may compare and contrast counts (
100
) with
counts defined as
traces
, or more precisely supertraces, of certain operators
acting in the critical cohomology
{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\textup{crit}}(\Maps)

or its
extraordinary analogs. These are defined when
\Maps
can be
described, globally or locally, as a critical
locus of a certain function

\mathscr{W}
, see for example
[
Ionuts
]
and the many references therein. The counts (
100
) are typically richer and, in
particular, allow richer equivariance
4
4

4

For a function
\mathscr{W}
,
the construction of the
equivariant critical cohomology requires

\mathscr{W}
to be invariant (as opposed to scaled by
group action), which represents a very noticeable loss of equivariance in enumerative applications. A
promising approach to restoring full equivariance is provided by
the interpretation of the fully equivariant counts as
Nekrasov-style

qq
-characters, for which one hopes to find a purely
representation-theoretic definition.
. Traces over
{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\textup{crit}}(\Maps)
have the advantage
of being easily categorified.

In some situations,
{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\textup{crit}}(\Maps)

may be equated with ordinary cohomology of related moduli spaces
by the mechanism of dimensional
reduction, see e.g.
[
DeformRed
]
and references therein for the current state of
technology in this area. In what follows, we will assume, for simplicity
of notation, that dimensional reduction has been already applied to
pass from critical cohomology to ordinary cohomology. In the
concrete situation considered in Section
5
, this
means replacing critical cohomology of quasimaps to
\mathbb{X}=T^{*}\mathbb{P}^{1}
with the ordinary cohomology of quasimaps to

\mathsf{X}=\mathbb{P}^{1}
.

4.3.4

For the computation of traces, it is beneficial to study

{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(\Maps)
as a module over some algebra
\mathscr{A}

which includes operators of cup product like
\cup\ev_{x}^{*}(\alpha)
,

\alpha\in{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(\mathsf{X})
, but
may also include raising and lowering
correspondences which change the degree of
f
by
creating or destroying singularities at points

x\in C
.
Various correspondences associated with
x\in C

can be assembled into a single correspondence

\mathscr{E}\in{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(I\times C\times\Maps)\to{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(\Maps)\,,

(102)

where
I
is a discrete indexing set for the generators of
\mathscr{A}
.
Composing
\mathscr{E}
along the
\Maps
-factors, one
gets an algebra of correspondences between
(I\times C)^{n}\times\Maps
and
\Maps
. It can be studied locally, since
modifications at different points of
C
supercommute.

By definition, the algebra
\mathscr{A}_{\mathscr{E}}(C)
has generators

\mathsf{e}_{i}\,\langle\gamma\rangle
labelled by an index
i\in I
and a cohomology class
\gamma\in{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(C)
. These act
by the
corresponding components

\mathsf{e}_{i}\,\langle\gamma\rangle\mapsto\mathscr{E}(\{i\}\otimes\gamma\otimes\,\textup{---}\,)\in\End{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(\Maps)

of the Künneth decomposition of
\mathscr{E}
along the

I\times C
-factor. The generators
\mathsf{e}_{i}\,\langle\gamma\rangle
satisfy two
sets of relations, discussed in (
105
) and
(
106
) below. Since the
correspondence
\mathscr{E}
will be fixed in what follows, we will drop it
from the notation
\mathscr{A}(C)
.

It is convenient
to use the term
descendants
of
\gamma
to refer to all
generators labelled by
\gamma
. The change
in degree provides a natural grading of

\mathscr{A}(C)
by the lattice
H_{2}(\mathsf{X},\mathbb{Z})
. There is also a
\mathbb{Z}
-grading
by the cohomological degree.

4.3.5

At the expense of making the indexing set
I
infinite, we may
assume that the linear span of the correspondences
\mathscr{E}
is
closed under two following operations.
First, consider the commutator of
\mathscr{E}
with itself
along the
\Maps
-factor. This is a
correspondence between
(I\times C)^{2}\times\Maps
and
\Maps
,
supported on the diagonal in
C^{2}
. To say that this diagonal
correspondence is in the linear
span of the correspondences
\mathscr{E}
is to say that

\left[\mathscr{E}_{iC_{1}},\mathscr{E}_{jC_{2}}\right]=\sum_{k}c^{k}_{ij}\,\mathscr{E}_{k,C}\circ\Delta^{*}\,,

(103)

for some
C
-independent coefficients
c^{k}_{ij}
in the ground ring, where
i,j,k\in I
. In (
103
), all correspondences act in the

\Maps
-factor,
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
refer to the two
factors in
C^{2}
, and

\Delta:C\to C_{1}\times C_{2}

is the diagonal map. Second, we can restrict the composition of the correspondences from

(I\times C)^{2}
to the diagonal in
C
, resulting in a relation of
the form

\mathscr{E}_{iC_{1}}\mathscr{E}_{jC_{2}}\circ\Delta_{*}=\sum_{k}m^{k}_{ij}\,\mathscr{E}_{k,C}\,,

(104)

with
C
-independent coefficients
m^{k}_{ij}
in the ground ring.
In terms of the generators, this means the Lie superalgebra relations

\left[\mathsf{e}_{i}\,\langle\gamma_{1}\rangle,\mathsf{e}_{j}\,\langle\gamma_{2}\rangle\right]=\sum c^{k}_{ij}\,\mathsf{e}_{k}\,\langle\gamma_{1}\cup\gamma_{2}\rangle\,,

(105)

and the relations

\mathsf{e}_{i}\mathsf{e}_{j}\,\langle\Delta_{*}\gamma\rangle=\sum_{k}m^{k}_{ij}\,\mathsf{e}_{k}\,\langle\gamma\rangle\,.

(106)

In (
106
), we use the convention that
\mathsf{e}_{i}\mathsf{e}_{j}\,\langle\gamma_{1}\otimes\gamma_{2}\rangle=\mathsf{e}_{i}\,\langle\gamma_{1}\rangle\,\mathsf{e}_{j}\,\langle\gamma_{2}\rangle
.
We have

\displaystyle\Delta_{*}\mathbf{p}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{p}\otimes\mathbf{p}

\displaystyle\Delta_{*}\alpha

\displaystyle=\alpha\otimes\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{p}\otimes\alpha

\displaystyle\Delta_{*}1

\displaystyle=1\otimes\mathbf{p}+\textstyle{\sum}\,\alpha_{i}\otimes\alpha_{i}^{\vee}+\mathbf{p}\otimes 1\,,

where
\alpha\in H^{1}(C)
and
\alpha_{i}
and
\alpha^{\vee}_{i}
form a
dual set of bases of
H^{1}(C)
with respect to the intersection
form. From this we conclude that:

(1)

assuming we work
nonequivariantly, the descendants of
\mathbf{p}\in H^{2}(C)
generate a commutative
subalgebra
\mathscr{A}^{2}\subset\mathscr{A}(C)
which does not depend on

C
;

(2)

the subalgebra
\mathscr{A}^{\geq 1}(C)\subset\mathscr{A}(C)

generated by the descendants of
H^{\geq 1}(C)
is the Clifford algebra
of
\Omega^{1}\mathscr{A}^{2}\otimes H^{1}(C)
, where
\Omega^{1}

denotes the module of Kähler differentials. The symmetric pairing on
the generators of the Clifford algebra comes from the skew-symmetric
Poisson
structure
c^{k}_{ij}
and the skew-symmetric multiplication in
H^{1}(C)
.

One can relate the descendents of
1\in H^{0}(C)
, which will not receive a
significant attention in this paper, to
Hamiltonian vector fields on the spectrum of
\mathscr{A}^{2}
, with their
natural action on functions and
1
-forms.
For us, it is the descendant of classes
\alpha\in H^{1}(C)
that are of
maximal interest. They form the part of
{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(C)
that
is sensitive to the geometry of the curve
C
, via the Galois action
that they carry.

4.3.6

While in the discussion above we explicitly avoided equivariant
cohomology, the key to understanding
\mathscr{A}^{2}
lies in the study of the
algebra
\mathscr{A}_{\mathscr{E}}(\mathbb{A}^{1}/\mathbb{G}_{\mathbf{m}})

generated by
\mathbb{G}_{\mathbf{m}}
-equivariant correspondences for

C=\mathbb{A}^{1}
. Since
\mathbf{p}^{2}=\tau\mathbf{p}
, where
\tau\in H^{2}(\pt/\mathbb{G}_{\mathbf{m}})

is the generator, this is a noncommutative algebra over

\mathbb{Z}[\tau]
. It becomes

\mathscr{A}^{2}
modulo
\tau
. Computations modulo

\tau^{2}
give
\mathscr{A}^{2}
its Poisson structure, see an example in Section

5.4.10
.

4.3.7

Enumerative geometry has experienced an
enormous influx of ideas from modern high energy
physics and, in particular, in recent years there has been a lot of
progress in making so-called Coulomb branches of
supersymmetric gauge theories act by correspondences
as above

5
5

5

At the risk of a gross oversimplification, this phenomenon
may be explained as follows. A supersymmetric gauge theory has
gauge fields, matter fields, and their supersymmetric
partners. In a Higgs vacuum, the gauge fields vanish, the matter
fields take a constant value at the bottom of their potential
\mathscr{W}_{\textup{mat}}
, while
the remaining
constant gauge transformation act on them with a finite stabilizer.
The moduli space
\mathbb{X}_{\textup{Higgs}}
of such vacua is the quotient
of the critical locus
\partial\mathscr{W}_{\textup{mat}}=0
by the gauge
group. The dynamics of the modulated Higgs
vacua is described by the supersymmetric quantum mechanics of the
moduli spaces of maps, or more precisely quasimaps, from
C
to

\mathbb{X}_{\textup{Higgs}}
.
There is an opposite kind of phase, called Coulomb phase, in which
the matter fields vanish, or take some constant value fixed by the whole
gauge group, while the superpartners of the gauge fields are
allowed constant commuting values. Nonabelian and
nonperturbative effects in the Coulomb phase are due to the
appearance of monopoles, which fill the space-time like a gas of
particles of different species. The moduli space

\mathbb{X}_{\textup{Coulomb}}
of Coulomb vacua, which should be a Poisson algebraic
variety because of the supersymmetry constraints, may be interpreted as describing
the equations
of state for this monopole gas. Inserted in
the expectation, each monopole species gives a global
function on
\mathbb{X}_{\textup{Coulomb}}
.
The correspondence (
102
) may be interpreted as
the insertion
of a monopole of species
i\in I
at the spatial location
x\in C
in
the spacetime
C\times\mathbb{R}
.
Thus, descendants of the point class
\mathbf{p}\in H^{2}(C)
give global
function on
\mathbb{X}_{\textup{Coulomb}}
. This logic can be inverted to
analyze
\mathbb{X}_{\textup{Coulomb}}
as in
[
NakCoul
]
, thus
circumventing the formidable difficulties
present in the direct analysis of the monopole gas.
.
Among the
really vast literature on the subject, we should mention

[
BFN1
,
BFN2
,
BFN3
,
Vermas
,
NakCoul
]
as the references that particularly influenced our thinking
about the subject.

From such considerations, one expects a Poisson map

\sigma:\mathbb{X}^{\vee}\to\Spec\mathscr{A}^{2}\,,

where
\mathbb{X}^{\vee}
is a certain nicer Poisson algebraic variety, such that

\sigma^{*}:\mathscr{A}^{\geq 1}(C)\to\Cliff\,(\Omega^{1}\mathbb{X}^{\vee}\otimes H^{1}(C))\,,

and similarly for the whole algebra
\mathscr{A}(C)
. Here
\Cliff
denotes
the corresponding sheaf of Clifford algebras. It is reasonable to
expect that this sheaf of Clifford algebras is easier to study than
\mathscr{A}^{\geq 1}(C)
and
that one can construct important
\mathscr{A}(C)
-modules as pushforwards
under
\sigma
.

4.3.8

As an important additional degree of freedom, one may have a
constructible sheaf

{\bm{\varphi}}_{\Maps}
on

\Maps
and a matching local system
{\bm{\varphi}}
on
C
with
a correspondence

\mathscr{E}:{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(C\times\Maps,{\bm{\varphi}}\boxtimes{\bm{\varphi}}_{\Maps})\to{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(\Maps,{\bm{\varphi}}_{\Maps})\,.

(107)

The local system
{\bm{\varphi}}
has rank
|I|
, which
absorbs the
I
-factor from earlier formulas. As before, it is
convenient to assume that this rank is countable. The coefficients

c^{k}_{ij}
and
m^{k}_{ij}
from (
103
) and (
104
)
amount to bilinear operations

\{\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,\},m:{\bm{\varphi}}\otimes{\bm{\varphi}}\to{\bm{\varphi}}\,,

which make
{\bm{\varphi}}
a local system of Poisson algebras. Fixing a
base point
x\in C
, we may interpret
{\bm{\varphi}}
as an algebraic action of the group

\Gamma=\pi_{1,\textup{alg}}(C)
on a certain
\mathbb{X}^{\vee}
, which
depends on
{\bm{\varphi}}
. To match the
generality of our discussion to the simplifying assumptions made in
our treatment of L-genera,
we will assume that the image of
\Gamma
is Zariski dense
in a reductive subgroup
\overline{\Gamma}\subset\Aut\mathbb{X}^{\vee}
.

It is important to stress that while
\mathbb{X}^{\vee}
depends on

{\bm{\varphi}}
, this dependence is only through the reductive group
\overline{\Gamma}

that is required to act on
\mathbb{X}^{\vee}
. We will write

\mathbb{X}^{\vee}_{\overline{\Gamma}}
to indicate such

It is natural to require this assignment to be functorial in the sense of the diagram

(108)

The vertical arrows in this diagram are maps in the category of
algebraic groups and the category of schemes with group action,
respectively.

The grading by the degree of the map and by the cohomological degree
leads to the action of
\mathsf{A}^{\sqbullet}\times\mathbb{G}_{\mathbf{m}}
on
\mathbb{X}^{\vee}
which
commutes with the action of
\overline{\Gamma}
.

4.3.9

We denote by
\mathscr{A}_{{\bm{\varphi}}}
the algebra generated by the Künneth
components of (
107
), which we denote by

\mathsf{e}\,\langle\gamma\rangle\in\mathscr{A}_{{\bm{\varphi}}}\,,\quad\gamma\in{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(C,{\bm{\varphi}})={H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(\Gamma^{\prime},{\bm{\varphi}})\,.

Here
\Gamma^{\prime}=\pi_{1,\textup{geom}}(C)
. Since the groups
H^{0}
and

H^{2}
pick out the trivial component in
{\bm{\varphi}}
, we have

\mathscr{A}_{{\bm{\varphi}}}^{2}=\textup{$\overline{\Gamma}$-invariants}\big/(\textup{noninvariants})\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt\sigma^{*}\hskip 8.19447pt}\mathscr{O}_{\mathsf{Fix}}\,,

where we mod out the invariants by its intersection with the
ideal generated by noninvariant functions, and
\mathsf{Fix}\subset\mathbb{X}^{\vee}

denotes the scheme-theoretic fixed locus. Similarly,

\sigma^{*}:\mathscr{A}_{{\bm{\varphi}}}^{\geq 1}\to\Cliff_{\mathbb{X}^{\vee},\Gamma}\,.

(109)

where Clifford algebra in the right-hand side is generated by
the sheaf

H\Omega_{\mathbb{X}^{\vee}}=H^{1}(\Gamma^{\prime},\Omega^{1}\mathbb{X}^{\vee}\big|_{\mathsf{Fix}})\left|\!\left[2\right]\!\right|\,.

Here we pay attention to cohomological degree and the Galois
action, as in Section
3.4.4
. See Section
5.6.2
below for
the details on how this works in a concrete situation.

4.3.10

It is logical to
construct
\mathscr{A}_{{\bm{\varphi}}}^{\geq 1}
-modules or
\mathscr{A}_{{\bm{\varphi}}}
-modules
as pushforwards of the Clifford modules, or Clifford modules with a
certain extra
\mathscr{D}
-module structure, under
\sigma
. If
\mathsf{Fix}
is smooth and has a spinor bundle

\Spin
then
\Spin
is clearly the main building block for all
constructions of this kind. We recall from Section
3.4

that
\Spin
is a natural categorification of the L-genus

\widehat{\mathsf{L}}_{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbb{X}^{\vee})
.

In the pushforward
\sigma_{*}
, one can in principle specify an
arbitrary support. We will see that modules of the form
{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(\Maps,{\bm{\varphi}}_{\Maps})

will indeed be constructed as pushforwards with support. What we do
in Section
5
in an example, and what we think is the right recipe in
general, is to take the pushforward with support in the total attracting manifold
for the
\mathsf{A}^{\sqbullet}
-action. This automatically produces modules whose
grading by degree agrees with the effective cone in
H_{2}(\mathsf{X},\mathbb{Z})
.

4.3.11

In Theorem
1
, L-genera appear as the answer to a
counting problem which is controlled by the Frobenius action on

{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}({\bm{\varphi}}_{\Maps})
for a certain sheaf
{\bm{\varphi}}_{\Maps}
on a
suitable moduli spaces of maps
C\to\mathsf{G}/\mathsf{P}
. More precisely, these
are sections of
\mathsf{G}/\mathsf{P}
-bundles over
C
rather than maps, see
Section
4.3.12
below. The cohomology
groups
{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}({\bm{\varphi}}_{\Maps})
provide
a natural categorification of the left-hand side in
(
98
). Thus, a
natural guess for the categorification of the right-hand side is that

\mathbb{X}^{\vee}=T^{*}(\mathsf{P}^{\vee}\backslash\mathsf{G}^{\vee}/\mathsf{P}^{\vee}),

(110)

with the bundle
\Spin
on its
\Gamma^{\prime}
-fixed locus.

In Section
5
we verify a version of this prediction in the simplest
example
\mathsf{G}=PSL(2)
, with one strange exception which only
happens for curves of genus
2
, see Theorem
2
.

There are
various aspect of this verification that merit comments. The most
immediate one is that the spaces (
110
) are, in general,
not

the same as the
conventional Coulomb branches
6
6

6

With current technology, those
are defined by Nakajima and his collaborators for
\mathsf{G}
of classical type.
for
geometries like
T^{*}\mathsf{G}/\mathsf{P}
. Whence our expectation that more general spaces

\mathbb{X}^{\vee}_{\overline{\Gamma}}
from Section
4.3.8
can be
defined and studied
7
7

7

One may speculate that

\mathbb{X}^{\vee}_{\overline{\Gamma}}
should
become
relevant when the superpartners of the gauge fields take
nongeneric values and leave the remaining gauge fields to vary in

\Bun_{\mathsf{M}}
for some Levi subgroup
\mathsf{M}
.
.

4.3.12

Let
\Bun_{\mathsf{X}}
denote the moduli
stack of
\mathsf{X}
-bundles
\widetilde{\mathsf{X}}\to C
. For any
\widetilde{\mathsf{X}}\in\Bun_{\mathsf{X}}
, we may consider the moduli
space
\Sect(C\to\widetilde{\mathsf{X}})
of sections of

\widetilde{\mathsf{X}}
. These moduli spaces are very similar to
\Maps(C\to\mathsf{X})
and
the corresponding counts are studied in enumerative geometry in parallel
with untwisted counts. Their arithmetic analogs provide very
interesting functions on the stack
\Bun_{\mathsf{X}}
. In the example

\mathsf{X}=\mathbb{P}^{1}
,
\mathsf{G}=PSL(2)
considered in Section
5
, we have

\Bun_{\mathsf{X}}=\Bun_{\mathsf{G}}
.

In the classical enumerative context, all
counts are invariant under equivariant deformation.
Up to such deformation,
we may assume
\widetilde{\mathsf{X}}
is in the image of

\Bun_{\mathsf{A}}\to\Bun_{\mathsf{G}}
, where
\mathsf{A}\subset\mathsf{G}
is a maximal
torus. In the arithmetic context, deformation invariance is no longer valid, but we
can still reduce the counts to
\Bun_{\mathsf{A}}
if we sum or integrate
over all
\mathsf{B}
-bundles that project to a given
\mathsf{A}
-bundle. Such
counts give rise to contant terms, Whittaker coefficients, and
similar functions of the Eisenstein series.

Geometrically, this means we allow the bundle
\widetilde{\mathsf{X}}

to vary in the unipotent directions. Concretely for

\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundles, this means we consider all bundles of the form

\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V})
, where rank 2 vector bundle
\mathscr{V}
fits into a sequence
of the form

0\to\mathscr{M}\to\mathscr{V}\to\mathscr{O}_{C}\to 0\,,

(111)

with fixed
\mathscr{M}\in\Pic(C)
.
Such moduli spaces turn out to be nicer and, in particular, more
amenable to the analysis by
\mathsf{A}
-equivariant localization.
In general, equivariant localization plays an important role in the
geometry of
\mathbb{X}^{\vee}
as, for instance, different components of the
fixed locus give different affine charts on it, see Section

6.2
.

4.3.13

Another technical point about Theorem
2
is that we work with
quasimaps, or rather quasisections, in place of the regular
maps. Quasimaps to
\mathbb{P}^{1}
provide a simple compactification of the
space of maps,
for which the strata look like moduli spaces of maps of lower
degree, with extra marked points. This allows one to, first, construct an
algebra of correspondences from the inclusion of boundary strata
and, second, isolate the compactly supported cohomology of the
moduli spaces of maps via the long exact sequence corresponding
to the inclusion of the boundary.

This structure is reflected in the first correspondence in the
following chain of correspondences

\textstyle{\mathbb{X}^{\vee}_{\QMN}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{X}^{\vee}_{\mathsf{MapsN}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{X}^{\vee}_{\Psi_{\textup{Eis}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{T^{*}\mathbb{P}^{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{T^{*}(\mathsf{N}^{\vee}\backslash\mathbb{P}^{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{T^{*}(\mathsf{B}^{\vee}\backslash\mathbb{P}^{1})\,.}

(112)

Here the correspondences are the conormals to the maps between the
bases of the cotangent bundles,

\QMN
denotes the moduli spaces of quasisections of

\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V})
, where
\mathscr{V}
is allowed to vary as in (
111
),
while
\mathsf{MapsN}\subset\QMN
corresponds to the open set of regular
sections. We expect such structure to be present generally.

4.3.14

The second correspondence in the chain (
112
) appears because we integrate the
constant term, that is, integrate over

\mathscr{M}\in\Pic(C)
, to obtain the distribution
\Psi_{\textup{Eis}}
. The
dependence on
\mathscr{M}
is Theorem
2
is introduced via
a homomorphism

\Pic(C)(\Bbbk)\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt\mathscr{M}\mapsto\mathscr{O}(\mathscr{M})\hskip 8.19447pt}\Pic_{\overline{\Gamma}\times\mathsf{A}^{\sqbullet}}(\mathbb{X}^{\vee})

(113)

such that
\mathscr{O}(\mathscr{M})\cong\mathscr{O}(\deg\mathscr{M})
as
a nonequivariant line bundle.

4.3.15

For
\mathsf{G}=PSL(2)
, there are only Whittaker coefficients beyond the
constant term, and those can be computed in the exact same fashion
in the function field situation. Geometrically equivalent to
Whittaker coefficient are counts of sections of
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V})
according to the order
of their tangency with a distinguished section provided by
\mathscr{M}\subset\mathscr{V}
. Similar counts are well-studied in enumerative geometry
and we are tempted to speculate that the duality interface
[
1
]
, which
is a certain Borel-equivariant elliptic class on
\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{X}^{\vee}
, may provide a general dictionary between insertions not
just for K-theoretic counts but also
in the arithmetic function field situation.

In the number field situations, there is a lack of many basic
building blocks and hence a really vast
space for imagination. It is curious to note, however, that the
Archimedean contributions to Whittaker coefficients of Eisenstein
series for
\mathsf{G}/\mathsf{B}
fully match the
quantum connection for
\mathsf{G}^{\vee}/\mathsf{B}^{\vee}
, see
[
Ger1
,
Ger2
]
.

5
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundles over curves

5.1
Overview

5.1.1

Our goal in this section is to illustrate the mechanism by which
the L-genera, and their natural categorification, appear
in the simplest automorphic and enumerative context, namely in the analysis of the
Eisenstein series for the group
\mathsf{G}=PGL(2,\Bbbk(C))
.
Leaving the detailed discussion of definitions and results to
later sections, our goal in this overview is to explain them in
very broad strokes.

Let
\mathscr{P}\to C
be a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle defined over
\Bbbk
.
By definition, the Eisenstein series

\textup{Eis}(\mathscr{P},\chi)
is the weighted count of

\Bbbk
-rational sections
s
of
\mathscr{P}
. The variable
\chi
is a
character of the group
\Pic(C)(\Bbbk)
which determines
the weight of
s
. It involves
the factor
a^{-\deg(s)}
, where
a
is a variable, and also
a character
\chi_{0}
of the finite group
\Pic_{0}(C)(\Bbbk)
. For

|a|\gg 1
, the series converges to a rational function of
a
.

5.1.2

For a bundle
\mathscr{P}
of the form
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{M}\oplus\mathscr{O}_{C})
, where

\mathscr{M}\in\Pic(C)
, the count of sections contains the
count of sections of
\mathscr{M}
. The latter is a famously delicate function
of

C
and
\mathscr{M}
. This means that
values of
\textup{Eis}(\,\cdot\,,\chi)
at points may be very difficult to
compute directly.
Classically, it has been observed that other linear functionals
of
\textup{Eis}(\,\cdot\,,\chi)
are easier to describe and open the
path to understanding Eisenstein series via Fourier analysis.

One such functional, known as the constant term
8
8

8

See
Appendix
7
for an elementary discussion of the role
played by constant terms in the spectral analysis of Eisenstein
series.
, sums the Eisenstein
series over all
\mathscr{P}=\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V})
such that

0\to\mathscr{M}\to\mathscr{V}\to\mathscr{O}_{C}\to 0\,,

where the line bundle
\mathscr{M}
is fixed. We denote this sum
\CT(\mathscr{M},\chi)
.
One can further refine it by recording the
intersection of
s
with the distinguished section of
\mathscr{P}

defined by
\mathscr{M}
. These refined counts can also be done
following the logic below.

5.1.3

Constant terms are very closely related to the distribution
\Psi_{\textup{Eis}}
, simply because
taking the constant term is the adjoint operator to the operator of
forming the Eisenstein series from
\chi
.
Theorem
1
may be rephrased to give a formula for
the constant term
\CT(\mathscr{M},\chi)
in terms of
\widehat{\mathsf{L}}_{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathsf{X}^{\vee})
, where

\mathsf{X}^{\vee}=T^{*}\mathbb{P}^{1}=T^{*}\left(\mathsf{G}^{\vee}/\mathsf{B}^{\vee}\right)\,,

with the action of the Galois group
\Gamma
that comes from a
homomorphism

\Gamma\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt(\chi,q^{-1})\hskip 8.19447pt}\mathsf{A}^{\vee}\times\mathsf{H}\subset\mathsf{G}^{\vee}\times\mathsf{H}\subset\Aut(\mathsf{X}^{\vee})\,.

Here
\mathsf{G}^{\vee}=SL(2)
,
\mathsf{A}^{\vee}\cong\mathbb{G}_{\mathbf{m}}
is the maximal torus of

SL(2)
, and
\mathsf{H}\cong\mathbb{G}_{\mathbf{m}}
scales the cotangent fibers. The group
\mathsf{B}^{\vee}
is a minimal
parabolic and hence
\mathsf{A}^{\sqbullet}=\mathsf{A}^{\vee}
. We recall that, by
construction,

\mathsf{A}^{\sqbullet}
is the group that records the grading of Eisenstein
series by degree via its character
a^{-\deg(s)}
,
a\in\mathsf{A}^{\sqbullet}
.
The action of
\mathsf{A}^{\sqbullet}
on
\mathsf{X}^{\vee}
commutes with the action of

\Gamma
.

5.1.4

From its construction, the genus
\widehat{\mathsf{L}}_{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathsf{X}^{\vee})
can be interpreted as
the K-theory class of a certain spinor bundle
\Spin
on

\mathsf{Fix}=\left(\mathsf{X}^{\vee}\right)^{\Gamma^{\prime}}=\begin{cases}\mathsf{X}^{\vee}\,,\quad&\chi_{0}^{2}=1\,,\\
\{0,\infty\}\,,\quad&\chi_{0}^{2}\neq 1\,,\end{cases}

(114)

where
\Gamma^{\prime}=\pi_{1,\textup{geom}}(C)\subset\Gamma
is the inertia subgroup.
Our goal in this section is to relate
\Spin
to the constant
term directly and geometrically.

5.1.5

The first steps towards bringing the Eisenstein series and the spinor
bundle
\Spin
closer together are rather
standard. Eisenstein series are counts of
\Bbbk
-rational
points in suitable moduli spaces. Hence, they can be computed and
also categorified using compactly supported cohomology of
these moduli spaces, with their action of Frobenius.
In general, this cohomology is taken with coefficients in a
nontrivial local system determined by
\chi
.

Further, counts of sections are easily
related to counts of quasisections, which is a useful technical
concept recalled in Section
5.3.1
. In the Eisenstein series
context, counts of quasisections are known as the geometric
Eisenstein series, see
[
BravGaits
,
Laumon
]
. We denote by
\QMN
the
moduli spaces that appear in the computation of the constant
term of geometric Eisenstein series. In fact, the formulation in
terms of
\widehat{\mathsf{L}}_{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathsf{X}^{\vee})
, as opposed to

\widehat{\mathsf{L}}_{\frac{1}{2}}(T^{*}(\mathsf{N}^{\vee}\backslash\mathsf{G}^{\vee}/\mathsf{B}^{\vee}))
already assumes passing form
the constant terms of Eisenstein series to the constant terms of
geometric Eisenstein series, see the discussion in Section

4.3.13
.

5.1.6

Quasisections have
two important advantages. First, quasisection of
\mathscr{P}
of
a given degree
d
form a proper moduli
space
\QM_{d}(\mathscr{P})
. Second, there are natural correspondences between quasisections of
different degrees.

These correspondence were introduced in
[
FinkKuz
]
and, specialized to
our situation,
generate an algebra
\mathscr{A}_{\chi}(C)
that may be described as a central
quotient of the universal enveloping
algebra of the Lie superalgebra
{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(C,\mathfrak{sl}_{2}^{\chi})
. Here we compose
the map
\Gamma\xrightarrow{\,\,\chi\,\,}\mathsf{A}^{\vee}
with the adjoint representation
to form the local system
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}^{\chi}
on
C
.

5.1.7

By construction, we have subalgefbras

\mathscr{A}_{\chi}(C)\supset\mathscr{A}^{\geq 1}_{\chi}(C)\supset\mathscr{A}^{2}_{\chi}

generated by the cohomology classes of degree
\geq 1
and
2
,
respectively. For the computation of the constant term, it suffices
to know
{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}_{c}(\QMN)
as a Frobenius-equivariant module over

\mathscr{A}^{2}_{\chi}
, which is central. However, it is even more natural
to keep track of the action of
\mathscr{A}^{\geq 1}_{\chi}(C)
, which is
a Clifford algebra over
\mathscr{A}^{2}_{\chi}
.

5.1.8

There is a Galois-equivariant map

\sigma:\mathsf{X}^{\vee}\to\textup{quadric cone}=\Spec\mathscr{A}^{2}_{\chi=1}\,.

It induces a pullback map

\sigma^{*}:\mathscr{A}^{\geq 1}_{\chi}(C)\to\Cliff_{\mathsf{X}^{\vee},\Gamma}\,,

(115)

where
\Cliff_{\mathsf{X}^{\vee},\Gamma}
is the sheaf of Clifford algebras
acting on
\Spin
. We recall that
\Spin
is distinguished by being
a self-dual bundle such that
\Cliff_{\mathsf{X}^{\vee},\Gamma}=\End(\Spin)
. We can twist it by line bundles on
\mathsf{X}^{\vee}
to obtain other
sheaves of Clifford modules of the same rank.

From (
115
), we get
\mathscr{A}^{\geq 1}_{\chi}(C)
-modules by pushing
forward Clifford modules under
\sigma
. In this pushforward, we can additionally
specify support to create various kinds of modules.

5.1.9

Our main result in this section, Theorem
2
below, says that

\textup{suitable centering of ${H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}_{c}(\QMN)$}=\sigma_{*,\mathbf{L}}\Spin\otimes\mathscr{O}(\mathscr{M})

(116)

as a
\mathscr{A}^{\geq 1}_{\chi}(C)
-module. Here centering means a
combination of cohomological shifts, Tate twists, et cetera,

\sigma_{*,\mathbf{L}}
is the pushforward with support in a certain
Lagrangian
\mathbf{L}\subset\mathsf{X}^{\vee}
, and
\mathscr{O}(\mathscr{M})
is as in
(
113
).

The line bundle
\mathscr{M}\in\Pic(C)
appears in the left-hand side of
(
116
) as the point at which the constant term, and its
categorification
{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}_{c}(\QMN)
, are computed. It appears in the
righ-hand side via the map (
113
) that takes line bundles
on
C
to equivariant line bundles on
\mathsf{X}^{\vee}
.

The identification in (
116
) is
\Fr\times\mathsf{A}^{\sqbullet}
-equivariant and preserves cohomological degree, thus giving a categorial lift to the relation between the
constant term and L-genera.

5.2
Eisenstein series

5.2.1

A global function field is a field of the form
\Bbbk(C)
, where

C
is curve over a finite field

\Bbbk
. The curve
C
can be assumed smooth,
projective, and irreducible over the algebraic closure

\overline{\Bbbk}
. Eisenstein
series for the group
PGL(2,\Bbbk(C))
are
functions on the countable set

\Bun_{PGL(2)}=\{\textup{$\mathbb{P}^{1}$-bundles $\mathscr{P}\to C$
defined over $\Bbbk$}\}/\cong

defined by counting all
\Bbbk
-rational sections
s:C\to\mathscr{P}
with a certain
weight. Namely, for each
section
s
, we may consider the isomorphism class of the
normal bundle

\NN_{s}=s^{*}(N_{\mathscr{P}/s(C)})\in\Pic(C)(\Bbbk)\,.

(117)

Here

0\to\underbrace{\Pic_{0}(C)(\Bbbk)}_{\textup{finite}}\to\Pic(C)(\Bbbk)\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt\deg\hskip 8.19447pt}\mathbb{Z}\to 0

(118)

is the group of
\Bbbk
-points of
\Pic(C)
. Given a character

\chi:\Pic(C)(\Bbbk)\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}\,,

we form the series

\textup{Eis}(\mathscr{P},\chi)=\sum_{s:C\to\mathscr{P}}\chi(\NN_{s})\,.

(119)

This is easily seen to converge to a rational
function of
\chi
if
|\chi(\textup{ample})|\ll 1
.
It is convenient to normalize
\chi
by

\chi(\mathscr{L})=(q^{1/2}a)^{-\deg\mathscr{L}}\,\chi_{0}(\mathscr{L})\,,

(120)

where
\chi_{0}
has finite order and
a\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
is a continuous
variable. For any
\chi_{0}
, the series (
119
) converges to a rational
function of
a
for
|a|\gg 1
.

5.2.2

Two bundles
\mathscr{P}
and
\mathscr{P}^{\prime}
are said to be related by an elementary
modification or a
Hecke modification
if

\textstyle{\Bl_{y}\mathscr{P}=\Bl_{y^{\prime}}\mathscr{P}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathscr{P}}
\textstyle{\mathscr{P}^{\prime}\,,}

(121)

where
y
and
y^{\prime}
are points in the fiber over
x
. In other words,
go from
\mathscr{P}
to
\mathscr{P}^{\prime}
, we first blow up some point
y
in the fiber
over
x
and then blow down the fiber as in (
122
)

(122)

Modification of a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle and its action on sections

5.2.3

A section
s
of
\mathscr{P}
in
(
121
)
determines a unique section
s^{\prime}
of
\mathscr{P}^{\prime}
and

\NN_{s^{\prime}}=\begin{cases}\NN_{s}(-x)\,,&y\in s\,,\\
\NN_{s}(x)\,,&y\notin s\,.\end{cases}

(123)

The fiber of
\mathscr{P}
over
x\in\mathbb{C}
is
\mathbb{P}^{1}(\Bbbk_{x})
, where

[\Bbbk_{x}:\Bbbk]=\deg(\mathscr{O}(x))\overset{\textup{\tiny def}}{=}\deg x\,.

Therefore, there are

q^{\deg(x)}+1
choices of
y
. Since only one is contained in
s
, we
infer that

\sum_{\mathscr{P}^{\prime}=\textup{modification of $\mathscr{P}$ at $x$}}\textup{Eis}(\mathscr{P}^{\prime},\chi)=\lambda(\chi,x)\,\textup{Eis}(\mathscr{P},\chi)\,,

(124)

where

\lambda(\chi,x)=q^{\deg(x)/2}\,\tr\begin{pmatrix}\chi_{\rho}(x)\\
&\chi_{\rho}(x)^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\,,\quad\chi_{\rho}(x)=\chi(x)\,q^{\deg(x)/2}\,.

(125)

The relation (
124
) and (
125
) are the simplest
examples of the very general fact that Eisenstein series are
eigenfunctions of Hecke operators and that the corresponding
eigenvalues have something to do with Galois representations.
More precisely, since the
geometric
Frobenius at the
point
x
acts on
\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}(1)
with weight
q^{-\deg(x)}
, we
can write

\chi_{\rho}(x)=\chi_{0}(x)a^{-\deg(x)}=\chi(x)\bm{(}-\tfrac{1}{2}\bm{)}\,,

(126)

where the final bold parentheses denote minus half a of tensor product
with
\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}(1)
, that is, minus half of the Tate shift. The number

\rho=1/2
is the half sum of the positive roots for
PGL(2)
, which
is reflected in the notation in (
126
). Such shift is a very
general feature in theory of Eisenstein series.

5.3
Moduli of sections and quasisections

5.3.1

Recall that our goal in this section is to explain the relation between Eisenstein
series and L-genera on the
categorical
level. On the Eisenstein
series side, the categorical lift is the obvious one. Namely,

\Bbbk
-rational sections
s
are
\Bbbk
-rational points of a certain
moduli space of sections. Their counts are captured by the Frobenius
action on the compactly supported cohomology of the corresponding
moduli space (in general, with coefficients in a certain local
system). These cohomology groups, with the action of Frobenius,
provide the natural categorification of the Eisenstein series.

5.3.2

Fix a rank 2 bundle
\mathscr{V}
over
C
such that
\mathscr{P}=\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V})
.
A section
s
gives a rank
1
subbundle
\mathscr{L}\subset\mathscr{V}
, that is,
an exact sequence of the form

0\to\mathscr{L}\to\mathscr{V}\to\textup{torsion free}\to 0\,.

(127)

The requirement that the quotient in (
127
) is
torsion-free defines an open set in the larger moduli
space of quasimaps or quasisections. By definition,

\QM(s:C\to\mathscr{P})=\{(\mathscr{L},s),\mathscr{L}\in\Pic(C),s\in\mathbb{P}(H^{0}(\mathscr{V}\otimes\mathscr{L}^{-1}))\}\,.

(128)

5.3.3

Clearly, for every quasisection
s
there exists a unique
effective divisor
D

such that

\textstyle{\mathscr{L}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathscr{L}(D)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s(-D)\,\,\,\,}
\textstyle{\mathscr{V}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
torsion-free
\textstyle{0\,,}

(129)

where the vertical arrow is the natural inclusion and the
bottom row is exact. In other words, a quasisection is the same as
a usual section and a collection of points of
C
. Thus, there
is a very simple relation between counting sections and
quasisections.

Counts of quasisections are known as
geometric
Eisenstein
series
[
BravGaits
]
. Their categorification, and its relation to
categorification of L-genera, is easier to explore. We
will focus on counts of quasisections and, correspondingly, on the cohomology of
\QM
.

5.3.4

By construction,

\textstyle{\mathbb{P}(H^{0}(\mathscr{V}\otimes\mathscr{L}^{-1}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\QM\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\{\mathscr{L}\}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Pic_{\deg\mathscr{L}}(C)}

(130)

is a projective space
fibration over
\Pic(C)
. This fibration is empty for
\deg\mathscr{L}\gg 0

, smooth for
\deg\mathscr{L}\ll 0
, and reflects interesting
features of
C
in the intermediate range. In the smooth
range, we have

\dim\QM(s:C\to\mathscr{P})=\deg\mathscr{V}-2\deg\mathscr{L}+1-g=\deg\NN_{s}+1-g\,,\quad\deg\mathscr{L}\ll 0\,,

(131)

where
g
is the genus of
C
and

\NN_{s}=\det\mathscr{V}/\mathscr{L}

(132)

is the normal bundle to a quasisection
s
. Whenever nonempty, we
define

\virdim\QM(s:C\to\mathscr{P})=\deg\mathscr{V}-2\deg\mathscr{L}+1-g\,.

(133)

The diagram (
130
) equips
\QM
and
\QMN
with the
tautological line bundes
\mathscr{O}_{\QM}(1)
and
\mathscr{O}_{\QMN}(1)

5.3.5

As already stressed in Section
5.1.2
, Eisenstein series,
and hence also the cohomology groups
{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(\QM)
, depend
delicately on
C
and
\mathscr{V}
. There exist more robust moduli
spaces
\QMN
which come up in the analysis of the constant term
and will be discussed presently.

In what follows, it will be convenient to normalize
\mathscr{V}
by requiring
that it fits into an exact sequence of the form

0\to\mathscr{M}\to\mathscr{V}\to\mathscr{O}_{C}\to 0\,.

(134)

We define

\displaystyle\QMN=\{(\mathscr{V},\mathscr{L},s),

\displaystyle\textup{$\mathscr{V}$ as in \eqref{eq:226}}\,,

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}\in\Pic(C),s\in H^{0}(\mathscr{V}\otimes\mathscr{L}^{-1}),s\neq 0\}\Big/\textup{iso fixing $\mathscr{M}$ and $\mathscr{O}_{C}$}\,,

(135)

where the line bundle
\mathscr{M}
is fixed and the isomorphisms by which
one is modding out in (
135
) are required to be identity on

\mathscr{M}
and
\mathscr{O}_{C}
. The automorphisms of
(\mathscr{L},\mathscr{V})
are
thus
\Aut(\mathscr{L})\times H^{0}(\mathscr{M})
, which act as a scalar and as
unipotent transformations on the section
s
, respectively.
There is a natural action of
\Aut(\mathscr{M})=\mathbb{G}_{\mathbf{m}}

on
\QMN
.

Possible choices of
\mathscr{V}
are parametrized
by
\Ext^{1}(\mathscr{O}_{C},\mathscr{M})=H^{1}(\mathscr{M})
and from
(
133
) we conclude

\virdim\QMN=-2\deg\mathscr{L}\,.

(136)

The last letter in the abbreviation
\QMN
stand for the
the unipotent freedom in choosing
\mathscr{V}
in (
134
). This is the
geometric analog of the integration over the maximal
unipotent subgroup in the automorphic setting.

5.3.6

To have a more concrete description of (
135
), one may
note that the extension (
134
) splits in the complement of
any point
y\in C
. Thus,
\mathscr{V}
is described by a
transition function of the form

T(t)=\begin{pmatrix}1&T_{12}(t)\\
&1\end{pmatrix}\,,\quad T_{12}(t)\in\Bbbk_{y}(\!(t)\!)\,,

between the formal neighborhood of
y
and
C\setminus y
.
Here
t
is a formal coordinate at
y
. The section
s
is
described by

s=\begin{pmatrix}s_{1}(t)\\
s_{2}(t)\end{pmatrix}\,,\quad s_{1}\in H^{0}(C\setminus\{y\},\mathscr{M}\otimes\mathscr{L}^{-1})\,,s_{2}\in H^{0}(C,\mathscr{L}^{-1})\,,

(137)

and the condition on
T
and
s
is that
Ts
is regular at
t=0
.
Different choices of trivialization lead to the identification

(T,s)\cong(ATB^{-1},Bs)\,,

where

A\in\left\{\begin{pmatrix}1&\Bbbk_{y}[\![t]\!]\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\right\}\,,\quad B\in\left\{\begin{pmatrix}1&H^{0}(C\setminus\{y\},\mathscr{M})\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\right\}\,.

Clearly, for
s_{2}(0)\neq 0
, the regularity of
Ts
implies

T_{12}=-\frac{s_{1}}{s_{2}}\bmod\Bbbk_{y}[\![t]\!]\,,

(138)

which is well-defined modulo the action of
B
and unique modulo the
action of
A
. Since the choice of
y
is arbitrary, we see that
the locus
s_{2}\neq 0
in
\QMN
is a smooth stack. The locus

s_{2}=0
is also smooth, but
\QMN
may not be smooth
along that locus.

This nice feature of
\QMN
can be observed for maps to more
general targets
X
. Suppose that the attracting manifolds of a torus
fixed point in
X
ia an orbits of a certain unipotent group. If one allows
the corresponding unipotent bundle to vary, the attracting manifold in the spaces
of maps to
X
becomes a smooth stacks with unipotent stabilizers.

5.4
Action of
{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(C,\mathfrak{sl}_{2})

5.4.1

Consider the Lie superalgebra

\mathfrak{sl}_{2}\otimes{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(C)={H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(C,\mathfrak{sl}_{2})\,,

(139)

where in the right-hand side we consider the cohomology of the
trivial local system with fiber
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
. Nontrivial local system will
appear in Section
5.6
below.

This Lie superalgebra acts by correspondences on

{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(\QM)
and
{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(\QMN)
. These correspondences
modify the line bundle
\mathscr{L}
and the section

s
. They do not affect the bundle
\mathscr{V}
.

The construction of
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-correspondences goes back to the classical work of
Finkelberg and Kuznetsov
[
FinkKuz
]
and forms the basic special case of
many more advanced constructions in geometric representation theory,
including the action of
Coulomb branches
[
BFN1
,
BFN2
,
BFN3
,
Vermas
,
NakCoul
]
(as well as quantum groups of which Coulomb
branches tend to be quotients) on ordinary or extraordinary cohomology
of the moduli spaces of quasimaps. We start by recalling the basic construction.

5.4.2

Consider the value
s(x)
of the section
s
at a point
x\in C
. By
construction, this is a section of

\mathscr{N}=\mathscr{V}\otimes\mathscr{L}^{-1}\otimes\mathscr{O}_{\QM}(1)\,,

(140)

which is a rank 2 vector bundle over
C\times\QM
.
Here we interpret
\mathscr{L}
as the pullback to
C\times\QM
of the
universal line bundle over
C\times\Pic(C)
. The
bundles
\mathscr{V}
and
\mathscr{O}_{\QM}(1)
are pulled back to
C\times\QM
from the
first factor and the second factor, respectively.

The locus
s(x)=0
is the image of the embedding

\iota:C\times\QM\hookrightarrow C\times\QM

(141)

defined by

\iota((x,\mathscr{L},s))=(x,\mathscr{L}(-x),s\otimes 1)\,.

(142)

Here
\mathscr{O}_{C}\xrightarrow{\,\,1\,\,}\mathscr{O}_{C}(x)
is the canonical
section. We may call
\iota^{*}\mathscr{N}

the virtual normal bundle to (
141
). It is the actual normal
bundle for
\mathscr{L}\ll 0
.

The inclusion (
141
) defines the functorial pullback map
\iota^{*}
on
cohomology. The definition of the pushforward
\iota_{*}
will require some
preparations.

5.4.3

Consider the following abstract situation. Let
\iota:Y\to X

be a closed inclusion with open complement
U
. Consider a relative
cohomology class
\varepsilon\in{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(X,U)
and
\bar{\varepsilon}
denote
its image in
{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(X)
. There is a factorization

\textstyle{{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\cup\bar{\varepsilon}}
\textstyle{{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(X)}
\textstyle{{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cup\varepsilon}
\textstyle{{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(X,U)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

(143)

of the operator
\cup\bar{\varepsilon}
of cup product by
\bar{\varepsilon}
,
which can be constructed as follows.

We denote by
\Lambda=\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}

the constant sheaf of coefficients. Other sheaves of coefficients may
also be considered. By construction, we have

\displaystyle{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(Y)

\displaystyle={H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(\iota^{*}\Lambda)

\displaystyle={H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(\iota_{*}\iota^{*}\Lambda)

(144)

\displaystyle{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(X,U)

\displaystyle={H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(\iota^{!}\Lambda)

\displaystyle={H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(\iota_{!}\iota^{!}\Lambda)\,,

(145)

where all maps are considered in the derived category of constructible
sheaves and
\iota_{*}=\iota_{!}
since the map
\iota
is proper. The
canonical maps
{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(X)\to{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(Y)
and
{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(X,U)\to{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(X)
come
from the adjunction maps
\Lambda\to\iota_{*}\iota^{*}\Lambda
and

\iota_{!}\iota^{!}\Lambda\to\Lambda
.

We have the following chain of maps

\Lambda\otimes\iota_{!}\iota^{!}\Lambda=\iota_{!}(\iota^{*}\Lambda\otimes\iota^{!}\Lambda)\to\iota_{!}\iota^{!}\Lambda\to\Lambda\,,

(146)

in which the first equality is the projection formula, see formula (2.6.19)
in
[
KS
]
or Theorem 2.3.29 in
[
Dimca
]
, while the
multiplication map
\iota^{*}\Lambda\otimes\iota^{!}\Lambda\to\iota^{!}\Lambda

is constructed in Proposition 3.1.11 in
[
KS
]
,
see also Theorem 3.2.13 in
[
Dimca
]
. The sequence (
146
)
yields the cup product map

{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(X)\otimes{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(X,U)\to{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(X)

together with its factorization as in (
143
).

5.4.4

For our specific inclusion (
142
), we will construct the map

\iota_{*}
as the diagonal map in (
143
) for the refined, also known
as localized, Euler class

\varepsilon=c_{2}(\mathscr{N})\in H^{4}(C\times\QM,U)\,.

(147)

Here
U
is the complement of the image of
\iota
. The Euler class
(
147
) is constructed as follows.

The bundle
\mathscr{N}
is a rank 2 vector bundle over
C\times\QM
with a
section
s(x)
. The data of a bundle with a section defines a map

\textup{clsf}:C\times\QM\to\mathbb{A}^{2}/GL(2)

(148)

to the classifying stack for vector bundles with
a section. By definition,

\varepsilon=\textup{clsf}^{*}(c_{2}(\textup{Universal bundle}))=\textup{clsf}^{*}([0/GL(2)])\,.

Here

[0/GL(2)]\in H^{4}(\mathbb{A}^{2}/GL(2),U_{\textup{universal}})\,,\quad U_{\textup{universal}}=(\mathbb{A}^{2}\setminus 0)/GL(2)\,,

is the class of the origin in the target of (
148
). Since
U=\textup{clsf}^{-1}(U_{\textup{universal}})
, this gives the desired
class (
147
) and completes the construction of
\iota_{*}
.

An alternative definition of
\iota_{*}
may be given as follows. From
the computations in Section
5.3.6
, it is clear that
allowing the bundle
\mathscr{V}
to vary gives a smooth total space of
expected dimension. In
this total space,
\iota
is a regular embedding, hence defines a
pushforward in cohomology. The correspondence
\iota
does not
change
\mathscr{V}
, therefore it defines an action on the cohomology of
\QM
for
fixed
\mathscr{V}
. Since the image of
\iota
is still the zero locus of
the section
s(x)
on the ambient space, the pushforward
along a regular embedding may be equivalently defined in terms of the
refined Euler class. As a result, the two definitions agree.

5.4.5

We define the maps

\mathsf{e},\mathsf{f}:{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(C\times\QM)\xrightarrow{\hskip 7.97224pt}{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(\QM)

(149)

by

\displaystyle\mathsf{e}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\textstyle{\int_{C}}\otimes 1)\,\iota_{*}\,,

\displaystyle\mathsf{f}

\displaystyle=-

\displaystyle(\textstyle{\int_{C}}\otimes 1)\,\iota^{*}\,,

(150)

where
\textstyle{\int_{C}}
denotes the pushforward along
C
. The
operators (
150
) increase the cohomological degree by
\pm 2
, and the degree of
\mathscr{L}
by
\mp 1
, respectively.

As operators in étale cohomology, the operators
\mathsf{e}
and

\mathsf{f}
also acquire a Galois action. We postpone this
discussion to Section
5.6
.

5.4.6

Modifications at different points
x\neq x^{\prime}
commute, which means
the commutator correspondence

\textstyle{{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(C\times C\times\QM)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{[\mathsf{e}_{13},\mathsf{f}_{23}]}
\scriptstyle{\Delta^{*}_{C}}
\textstyle{{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(\QM)}
\textstyle{{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(C\times\QM)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{h}}

(151)

factors through the restriction
\Delta^{*}_{C}
to the diagonal in

C\times C
.
The subscripts in
[\mathsf{e}_{13},\mathsf{f}_{23}]
mean that the operator

\mathsf{e}
is applied in the 1st and the 3d factor of the product, and
similarly for
\mathsf{f}
.
At this point, the diagram (
151
) is the definition of the
operator
\mathsf{h}
.

5.4.7

It is clear that
\mathsf{h}
preserves cohomological degree and is given by a
correspondence that is supported over the diagonal in
\QM
.
Therefore has the form

\mathsf{h}=(\textstyle{\int_{C}}\otimes 1)\circ\textup{cup product by a class in $\mathsf{H}^{2}(C\times\QM)$}\,.

(152)

The divisor class in (
152
) will be computed presently. For
future reference, it will be convenient to list the tautological
divisor classes. We define

\lambda=c_{1}(\mathscr{L})\,,\quad\mu=c_{1}(\mathscr{M})\,,\quad\tau=c_{1}(TC)\,,\quad\eta=c_{1}(\mathscr{O}_{\QM}(1))\,.

(153)

The exact sequence (
134
) relates Chern classes of
\mathscr{V}
to

c_{1}(\mathscr{M})
. In particular, we have

c_{1}(\mathscr{N})=\mu-2\lambda+2\eta\,.

(154)

Being pulled back from
C
, the classes
\mu
and
\tau

represent scalar operators in formulas like (
152
).
While in
a nonequivariant situation we have
\mu^{2}=\tau^{2}=0
, in an equivariant
situation both of these classes are nonzerodivisors. The ability to
divide by
\tau
in a equivariant situation will come in handy in the
proof of Proposition
5.1
below.

5.4.8

Proposition 5.1
.

We have

\mathsf{h}=(\textstyle{\int_{C}}\otimes 1)\circ(\tau+c_{1}(\mathscr{N}))\,.

(155)

The operators
\mathsf{e},\mathsf{f},\mathsf{h}
generate the Lie algebra

\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
in the sense that

[\mathsf{e},\mathsf{f}]=\mathsf{h}\,\Delta_{C}^{*}\,,\quad[\mathsf{h},\mathsf{e}]=2\mathsf{e}\,\Delta_{C}^{*}\,,\quad[\mathsf{h},\mathsf{f}]=-2\mathsf{f}\,\Delta_{C}^{*}\,.

(156)

Proof.

Consider the pushforward map

\Delta_{C,*}:{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(C\times\QM)\to{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(C\times C\times\QM)\,.

Since the composition of the pushforward and pullback is the multiplication
by the Euler class of the normal bundle, we have

\Delta_{C}^{*}\circ\Delta_{C,*}=c_{1}(N_{C^{2}/\Delta C})=c_{1}(TC)=\tau\,.

Therefore

\tau\mathsf{h}=[\mathsf{e},\mathsf{f}]\circ\Delta_{C,*}\,.

(157)

By construction of
\iota_{*}
, we have

\iota^{*}\iota_{*}\circ\Delta_{C,*}=\iota^{*}(\bar{\varepsilon})\cup\,.

where the refined Euler class
\varepsilon
was defined in
(
147
) and
\bar{\varepsilon}
is the usual unrefined Euler class. To
compute the composition in the other direction, we use the factorization
(
143
), which gives

\iota_{*}\iota^{*}\circ\Delta_{C,*}=\bar{\varepsilon}\cup\,.

Therefore,

[\iota^{*},\iota_{*}]\circ\Delta_{C,*}=(\iota^{*}(\bar{\varepsilon})-\bar{\varepsilon})\cup\,,

(158)

We have

\iota^{*}(\mathscr{N})=\mathscr{N}\otimes TC\,,

(159)

whence

\displaystyle[\iota^{*},\iota_{*}]\circ\Delta_{C,*}

\displaystyle=c_{2}(\mathscr{N}\otimes TC)-c_{2}(\mathscr{N})

\displaystyle=\tau(c_{1}(\mathscr{N})+\tau)\,.

(160)

To complete the proof of (
155
), it
remains to argue that we can divide by
\tau
in
(
157
) and (
160
). Clearly, the divisor in
(
152
) is a linear combination of all possible divisors
(
153
) involved, which is universal, that is, independent of the curve
C
.
Since our computations are fully equivariant,
the formula should also be true for
C=\mathbb{A}^{1}
with the defining action
of
\mathbb{G}_{\mathbf{m}}
. In this case,
\tau
is the
generator of
{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\mathbb{G}_{\mathbf{m}}}(C,R)\cong R[\tau]
, where
R
is the ring of
coefficients, and is not a zerodivisor. This completes the proof of
(
155
).

The other
commutators in (
156
) follow from (
159
).

∎

5.4.9

To convert the relations (
156
) into a more familiar form, we
consider the map

\mathfrak{sl}_{2}\otimes{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(C)={H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(C,\mathfrak{sl}_{2})\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt}\End({H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}({\textsf{QM}}))

(161)

such that

\begin{pmatrix}0&\gamma\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}\mapsto\mathsf{e}\,\langle\gamma\rangle\overset{\textup{\tiny def}}{=}\mathsf{e}(\gamma\boxtimes\,\cdot\,)\,,\quad\gamma\in{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(C)\,,

(162)

and similarly for
\mathsf{f}
and
\mathsf{h}
. Both sides in (
161
) are
Lie superalgebras
with the
\mathbb{Z}/2
-grading determined by
the cohomological grading. From (
156
) we deduce

Proposition 5.2
.

The map (
162
) is a homomorphism of Lie superalgebras.

5.4.10

Since the computations in Proposition
5.1
are fully
equivariant, they determine the algebra
\mathscr{A}(\mathbb{A}^{1}/\mathbb{G}_{\mathbf{m}})
mentioned in Section

4.3.6
as the following quadratic extension of the
graded version of
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}\otimes R[\tau]
, where
R
is the ring of
coefficients.

To have a well-defined pushforward along
C
, we need to either
localize or work with compactly supported classes. Choosing the second
options, we fix the generator
\mathbf{p}\in H^{2}_{c}(\mathbb{A}^{1}/\mathbb{G}_{\mathbf{m}})
such
that
\int_{C}\mathbf{p}=1
. Since

\mathbf{p}^{2}=\tau\mathbf{p}\,,

we see that
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}\otimes H^{2}_{c}(\mathbb{A}^{1}/\mathbb{G}_{\mathbf{m}})
is a graded version of

\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
over
R[\tau]
in the sense that

[\mathsf{e}\,\langle\mathbf{p}\rangle,\mathsf{f}\,\langle\mathbf{p}\rangle]=\tau\mathsf{h}\,\langle\mathbf{p}\rangle\,,

et cetera. Adding the element
\mu
, we see from Proposition

5.3
below that

\mathscr{A}(\mathbb{A}^{1}/\mathbb{G}_{\mathbf{m}})=\mathscr{U}_{\textup{graded}}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2})[\mu]\Big/\left(\mu^{2}=\textup{Casimir element}+\tau^{2}\right)\,.

(163)

5.4.11

We note that Propositions
5.1
and
5.2
apply verbatim
to the moduli spaces
\QMN
in place of
\QM
. Indeed, the
correspondences defining the
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-action act over the space

H^{1}(\mathscr{M})
of possible extensions
\mathscr{V}
and commute with the action of

H^{0}(\mathscr{M})
. From now on, we will focus on the compactly
supported cohomology
{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}_{c}(\QMN)
.

5.5
Casimir elements and the mirror
\mathsf{X}^{\vee}

5.5.1

As an immediate corollary of the computation done in (
160
),
we obtain the following

Proposition 5.3
.

We have the equality

\big(2\,\mathsf{e}_{13}\,\mathsf{f}_{23}+2\,\mathsf{f}_{23}\,\mathsf{e}_{13}+\mathsf{h}_{13}\mathsf{h}_{23}\big)\circ\Delta_{C,*}=\mu^{2}-\tau^{2}

(164)

of operators from
{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}_{c}(C\times\QMN)
to
{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}_{c}(\QMN)
.

To convert this to a more familiar form, let
\mathbf{p}\in H^{2}(C)
be the generator that comes from the pushforward of
1\in H^{0}(\pt)
under

\pt\to C
. We may use the relations

\displaystyle\Delta_{C,*}(\mathbf{p})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{p}\otimes\mathbf{p}\,,

\displaystyle\Delta_{C,*}(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\alpha\otimes\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{p}\otimes\alpha\,,\quad\alpha\in H^{1}\,,

to deduce the following

Corollary 5.4
.

We have

\displaystyle 4\,\mathsf{e}\,\langle\mathbf{p}\rangle\,\mathsf{f}\,\langle\mathbf{p}\rangle+\mathsf{h}\,\langle\mathbf{p}\rangle^{2}

\displaystyle=0\,,

(165)

\displaystyle 2\,\mathsf{e}\,\langle\mathbf{p}\rangle\,\mathsf{f}\,\langle\alpha\rangle+2\,\mathsf{f}\,\langle\mathbf{p}\rangle\,\mathsf{e}\,\langle\alpha\rangle+\mathsf{h}\,\langle\mathbf{p}\rangle\,\mathsf{h}\,\langle\alpha\rangle

\displaystyle=0\,,

(166)

where
\alpha\in H^{1}(C)
.

In (
166
), we have divided by
2
since for the point counts
we may assume that
2
is invertible in the ring of coefficients
R
.

5.5.2

It will be useful to have a formula for the action of the operators

\mathsf{h}
.

Proposition 5.5
.

We have

\mathsf{h}\,\langle\mathbf{p}\rangle=2\eta\,,\quad\mathsf{h}\,\langle\alpha\rangle=2\alpha\,,

(167)

viewed as operators of cup product by
\eta=c_{1}(\mathscr{O}(1))
and
the pullback of
\alpha\in H^{1}(C)=H^{1}(\Pic C)
to
\QMN
.

Proof.

This follows from (
155
), (
154
) and the following
formula

\lambda=(\deg\mathscr{L})\cdot\mathbf{p}\otimes 1+\sum\gamma_{i}\otimes\gamma_{i}^{\vee}\,,

(168)

for the class of
\lambda\in H^{2}(C\times\QMN)
. Here

\{\gamma_{i}^{\vee}\}
is the dual basis to a
basis
\{\gamma_{i}\}
of

H^{1}(C)
in the sense that
\gamma_{i}\cup\gamma^{\vee}_{j}=\delta_{ij}\mathbf{p}
. Indeed, for
\deg\mathscr{L}=0
, the universal bundle
\mathscr{L}
is the pullback
via
C\hookrightarrow\Pic_{0}(C)
of the Poincaré line bundle, whose first Chern
class is

\sum\gamma_{i}\otimes\gamma_{i}^{\vee}=\Delta\Theta-\Theta\otimes 1-1\otimes\Theta\,.

Here
\Theta
is the theta divisor and
\Delta
is the comultiplication in the cohomology of the
group
\Pic_{0}(C)
. To obtain other values of
\deg\mathscr{L}
, we may
multiply by line bundles pulled back from
C
.
∎

5.5.3

Formulas (
165
) and (
166
) may be given the
following geometric interpretation. The algebra

\mathscr{A}(C)=\mathscr{U}({H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(C,\mathfrak{sl}_{2}))/\textup{equations
\eqref{eq:36}}\,,

(169)

acts in
{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(\QMN)
. Inside this algebra we have subalgebras

\mathscr{A}(C)\supset\mathscr{A}^{\geq 1}(C)\supset\mathscr{A}^{2}=\textup{center of
$\mathscr{A}^{\geq 1}(C)$}\,,

(170)

generated by elements of cohomological degree
\geq 1
and
2
,
respectively. The notation here reflects the fact that the
subalgebra
\mathscr{A}^{2}
does not depend on the curve

C
. Equation (
165
) says that

\displaystyle\mathsf{X}^{\vee}_{0}

\displaystyle\overset{\textup{\tiny def}}{=}\Spec\mathscr{A}^{2}

\displaystyle=\textup{quadric cone}\,,

(171)

while (
166
) says that

\textup{$\mathscr{A}^{2}$-span of $H^{1}(C,\mathfrak{sl}_{2})$}\cong H^{1}(C,\Omega^{1}(\mathsf{X}^{\vee}_{0})))\,,

(172)

where
\Omega^{1}(\mathsf{X}^{\vee}_{0})
denotes the module of Kähler
differentials over the base field. For the time being,
\Omega^{1}(\mathsf{X}^{\vee}_{0})
forms a trivial
local system on
C
. Nontrivial local system will appear in Section

5.6
.

5.5.4

A shift is required to make the isomorphism (
172
) preserve
cohomological dimension and the action of Frobenius. Indeed, we have

\deg\mathsf{e}\,\langle\alpha\rangle=\deg\mathsf{e}\,\langle\mathbf{p}\rangle-1\,,\quad\textup{weight}\,\mathsf{e}\,\langle\alpha\rangle=\textup{weight}\,\mathsf{e}\,\langle\mathbf{p}\rangle-\tfrac{1}{2}\,,

where the degree is the cohomological degree and weight refers to the
absolute values of the Frobenius eigenvalues. So, if we want to get
these operators as
H^{1}
, we need both a shift in cohomology and a
Tate twist. We define

H\Omega_{0}=H^{1}(C,\Omega^{1}(\mathsf{X}^{\vee}_{0})))\left|\!\left[2\right]\!\right|\,,

(173)

where

\left|\!\left[2\right]\!\right|=[2]\bm{(}1\bm{)}

denotes the pure combination of the cohomological shift and
Tate twist, and
where
\Omega^{1}
was originally placed in degree
0
, compare with
(
83
).
From the Poisson structure on the cone and multiplication in cohomology, we get a symmetric
pairing

S^{2}H\Omega_{0}\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt\cup\otimes\{\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,\}\hskip 8.19447pt}H^{2}(C,\mathscr{O}_{\mathsf{X}^{\vee}_{0}})\left|\!\left[2\right]\!\right|=\mathscr{O}_{\mathsf{X}^{\vee}_{0}}=\mathscr{A}^{2}\,.

(174)

We define

\Cliff_{\mathsf{X}^{\vee}_{0}}=\textup{Clifford}(H\Omega_{0})\,.

Formulas (
165
) and (
166
), and the naturality of
all operations, give the following

Proposition 5.6
.

We have a degree-preserving
isomorphism
\mathscr{A}^{\geq 1}(C)\cong\Cliff_{\mathsf{X}^{\vee}_{0}}
. Moreover,
this isomorphism is Frobenius-equivariant.

5.5.5

We define

\sigma:\mathsf{X}^{\vee}\cong T^{*}\mathbb{P}^{1}\to\mathsf{X}^{\vee}_{0}

(175)

as the blow-up at the vertex of the cone. This is the world’s most
basic equivariant symplectic resolution. As above,
\mathsf{X}^{\vee}

gets its own sheaf of Clifford algebras
\Cliff_{\mathsf{X}^{\vee}}
with
a homomorphism

\sigma^{*}:\Cliff_{\mathsf{X}^{\vee}_{0}}\to\Cliff_{\mathsf{X}^{\vee}}\,.

There is thus a well-defined pushforward
\sigma_{*}
of Clifford modules.

In modern representation theory, understanding the representation of
quantizations of Poisson varieties via (derived) pullbacks and
pushforwards under maps like
\sigma
is a very important technical
tool known as
localization
. Our situation is simpler in that
we deal with sheaves of Clifford algebras instead of really
noncommutative algebras like the full
\mathscr{A}(C)
. In the end,
however, the full structure of the
\mathscr{A}(C)
-module is uniquely
determined.

We will show, and this will be the main result of this section, that
the module
{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(\QMN)
is the pushforward of a suitable twist of the spinor bundle

\Spin_{\mathsf{X}^{\vee}}
. This will be, however, not the
ordinary pushforward, but rather pushforward with support in
a certain Lagrangian
\mathbf{L}\subset\mathsf{X}^{\vee}
.

5.5.6

It will be convenient to visualize various geometric notions
using the toric diagram of
\mathsf{X}^{\vee}=T^{*}\mathbb{P}^{1}
as seen on the right in Figure
(
176
) below. It is also
known as the Newton or moment polyhedron of
T^{*}\mathbb{P}^{1}
. Its two vertices
correspond to the fixed points
0,\infty\in\mathbb{P}^{1}
. Edges
correspond to the 1-dimensional torus orbits.

Canonically, diagrams like (
176
) lie in the
dual of the Lie algebra of a maximal torus

\{(a,q)\}\subset\Aut(T^{*}\mathbb{P}^{1})

where
a
acts by
\diag(a^{-1},a)\in SL(2)
and
q
scales the
cotangent fibers with weight
q^{-1}
. Lattice points in toric
diagram are the weights of sections of line
bundles. Specifically, in (
176
) we see the
weights
H^{0}(\mathscr{O}_{\mathsf{X}^{\vee}})
and

H^{0}(\mathscr{O}_{\mathsf{X}^{\vee}}(8))
. Here
\mathscr{O}_{\mathsf{X}^{\vee}}(8)
is a random ample
line bundle. For the natural linearization, the weights of the corner
vertices for
\mathscr{O}_{\mathsf{X}^{\vee}}(8)
are
a^{8}
and
a^{-8}
,
respectively. In Figure
176
we indicated the weights of
neighboring vertices relative to the weights of the corner vertices.

5.5.7

We define

\displaystyle\mathbf{L}

\displaystyle=\{\mathsf{f}\,\langle\mathbf{p}\rangle=0\}

(177)

\displaystyle=\{x\in\mathsf{X}^{\vee},\exists\lim_{a\to 0}a\cdot x\}\,.

(178)

This is the union of two attracting manifolds, namely

\displaystyle\mathbf{L}_{0}

\displaystyle=\{x\in\mathsf{X}^{\vee},\lim_{a\to 0}a\cdot x=0\}

\displaystyle=\textup{zero section without $\infty$}\,,

(179)

\displaystyle\mathbf{L}_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\{x\in\mathsf{X}^{\vee},\lim_{a\to 0}a\cdot x=\infty\}

\displaystyle=\textup{the fiber over $\infty$}\,,

(180)

see Figure (
182
).

5.5.8

To get a feeling for what the pushforward with support in
\mathbf{L}
looks

\mathscr{O}_{\mathsf{X}^{\vee}}(8)
, which means we

{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\mathbf{L}}(\mathscr{O}_{\mathsf{X}^{\vee}}(8))
.

\mathbf{L}_{\infty}\subset\mathbf{L}

{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\mathbf{L}}
in terms of
{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\mathbf{L}_{\infty}}
and

{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}_{\mathbf{L}_{0}}
. Both of these can be computed in a

H^{i}_{\mathbf{L}_{\infty}}(\mathscr{O}(8))=H^{i}_{\mathbf{L}_{0}}(\mathscr{O}(8))=0\,,\quad i\neq 1\,,

and

\textstyle{H^{1}_{\mathbf{L}}(\mathscr{O}(8))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{1}_{\mathbf{L}_{0}}(\mathscr{O}(8))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\,,}
\textstyle{a^{8}(\mathsf{e}\mathsf{h}^{-1})^{8}\,R[\mathsf{e},(\mathsf{f}\mathsf{h}^{-1})^{\pm 1}]/R[\mathsf{e},\mathsf{f}\mathsf{h}^{-1}]}
\textstyle{a^{8}R[\mathsf{e}\mathsf{h}^{-1},\mathsf{f}^{\pm 1}]/R[\mathsf{e}\mathsf{h}^{-1},\mathsf{f}]}

and
