H^{1}_{\mathbf{L}}(\mathscr{O}(8))=H^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}(\mathscr{O}(8))[\mathsf{f}^{-1}]\big/H^{0}_{\mathbf{L}}(\mathscr{O}(8))\,.

5.5.9

As a result of taking the cohomology with support in the
attracting manifold
\mathbf{L}
, the
a
-grading of all resulting modules
is bounded from above. In the Eisenstein series context, the variable
a
weights
a quasisection
s
by
a^{-\deg(s)}
. Since
\deg(s)
is bounded below
for fixed
\mathscr{M}
, it makes sense to look for a description of the

\mathscr{A}^{\geq 1}(C)
-module
{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}_{c}(\QMN)
as a
pushforward with support in
\mathbf{L}
.

5.5.10

In what follows, we will always include a cohomological shift by the
number
\delta=\frac{1}{2}\dim\mathsf{Fix}
in the
definition of
\sigma_{*,\mathbf{L}}
. With this definition, a line bundle
on
\mathsf{Fix}
pushes forward to object in zero cohomological degree.

5.6
Galois action

5.6.1

For applications to Eisenstein series, we will need cohomology of
\QMN

with coefficients in local systems. Via the natural map

\QMN(s:C\to\mathscr{P})\xrightarrow{\hskip 7.97224pt\NN_{s}\hskip 7.97224pt}\Pic(C)

(183)

we can pull back a local system
\chi
from
\Pic(C)
. We associate a
local systems on
\Pic(C)
to characters
\chi
of
\Pic(C)
by requiring
that the geometric
Frobenius acts on the fiber over
\mathscr{O}_{C}(x)\in\Pic_{1}(C)
with eigenvalue

\chi(x)
.

5.6.2

We have

\NN_{s(x)}=\NN_{s}\otimes\mathscr{O}_{C}(x)^{2}\,.

(184)

Therefore, we have a well-defined degree-preserving
Galois-equivariant correspondences

\displaystyle{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}_{c}(C\times\QMN,

\displaystyle\chi^{2}\,

\displaystyle\boxtimes\chi)\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt\mathsf{e}\hskip 8.19447pt}{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}_{c}(\QMN,\chi)\left|\!\left[2\right]\!\right|\,,

\displaystyle{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}_{c}(C\times\QMN,

\displaystyle\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}

\displaystyle\boxtimes\chi)\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt\mathsf{h}\hskip 8.19447pt}{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}_{c}(\QMN,\chi)\,,

(185)

\displaystyle{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}_{c}(C\times\QMN,

\displaystyle\chi^{-2}

\displaystyle\boxtimes\chi)\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt\mathsf{f}\hskip 8.19447pt}{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}_{c}(\QMN,\chi)\left|\!\left[-2\right]\!\right|\,.

The cohomological shifts and Tate twists in these formulas appear
because pushforwards in the construction of
\mathsf{e}
and

\mathsf{f}
introduce a shift by
\left|\!\left[2\delta\right]\!\right|
, where

\delta
is the difference of
(virtual) dimensions between their target and domain.

5.6.3

To get rid of the cohomological shifts in the right-hand side of
(
185
), we will center the cohomology of
\QMN
around the
middle degree

\virdim\QMN=-2\deg\mathscr{L}\,.

(186)

For the eventual comparison with the spinor bundles, it is
a good idea to also symmetrize the module
{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}_{c}(\QMN)
with
respect to its grading by the degree
\deg\mathscr{L}
and also
with respect to the Galois action. Neither of these shifts
affects the
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-action.

We define

\widehat{H}(\QMN,\chi)={H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}_{c}(\QMN,\chi)\left|\!\left[-2\deg\mathscr{L}+g-1\right]\!\right|\otimes\chi(\mathscr{K}_{C})^{-1}\,.

(187)

where the additional twist comes from

\left(\Lambda^{\textup{top}}{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(C,\chi^{2})\right)^{-1/2}=\chi(\mathscr{K}_{C})^{-1}\left|\!\left[g-1\right]\!\right|\,.

(188)

This is a character of the group
\Fr\times\mathsf{A}^{\sqbullet}
via the
action of Frobenius in the fiber of the local system
\chi
on
\Pic(C)
over
the canonical bundle
\mathscr{K}_{C}
. As a function of
a
, it is a multiple of
a^{2g-2}
in
accordance with (
126
). See
(
212
) below for more on the equality in (
188
).

Note that the implicit dependence of
\chi(\mathscr{M})
on
a\in\mathsf{A}^{\sqbullet}
will
also have a centering effect for the grading by
\mathsf{A}^{\sqbullet}
, equivalently,
the grading by
\deg\mathscr{L}
, which one can see in Lemma
6.11

below.

5.6.4

Consider the Galois representation

\begin{pmatrix}\chi\\
&\chi^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\in SL(2)\,,

(189)

and the corresponding local system
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}^{\chi}
obtained by
composing (
189
) with the adjoint representation.
Künneth decomposition in (
185
) results in the following

Proposition 5.7
.

We have a Lie superalgebra action

{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(C,\mathfrak{sl}_{2}^{\chi})\otimes\widehat{H}(\QMN)\to\widehat{H}(\QMN)\,,

(190)

which is Frobenius-equivariant and degree-preserving.

Note the small, but important difference between (
189
) and
(
125
). With the symmetric normalization
(
187
), the shift by
\rho
as in
\chi_{\rho}=\chi\bm{(}-\tfrac{1}{2}\bm{)}
is not required. Of course, the shift will reappear in
any computation with actual Eisenstein series, because point counts
are given by Frobenius eigenvalues in
{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}_{c}
, not in
\widehat{H}
.
One can also say that centering as in (
187
) provides a more geometric way
to introduce the shift by
\rho
.

More generally, for moduli spaces of maps
f:C\to\mathsf{Y}
to any
target
\mathsf{Y}
, we have

\virdim=\deg f^{*}(T\mathsf{Y})+\const=\left\langle c_{1}(\mathsf{Y}),\deg f\right\rangle+\const\,,

with the same constant
(1-g)\dim\mathsf{Y}
in both instances. Thus
the coefficient
2
in (
187
) should, in general, be replaced by

c_{1}(\mathsf{Y})
.

5.6.5

We denote by
\mathscr{A}_{\chi}(C)
the algebra generated by
{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(C,\mathfrak{sl}_{2}^{\chi})
.
As before, we have subalgebras

\mathscr{A}_{\chi}(C)\supset\mathscr{A}^{\geq 1}_{\chi}(C)\supset\mathscr{A}^{2}_{\chi}

(191)

generated by elements of cohomological degree
\geq 1
and
2
,
respectively. Proposition
5.6
generalizes as follows

Proposition 5.8
.

We have a Frobenius-equivariant, degree-preserving
isomorphism

\mathscr{A}^{\geq 1}_{\chi}(C)\cong\Cliff_{\mathsf{X}^{\vee}_{0},\Gamma}\,.

(192)

5.6.6

When
\chi_{0}^{2}\neq 1
, there is a dramatic simplification in the
structure of these algebras. Indeed, the raising operators are
limited to

\mathsf{e}\,\langle\gamma\rangle\,=\mathsf{e}(\gamma\boxtimes\,\cdot\,)\,,\quad\gamma\in H^{1}(C,\chi^{2}_{\rho})\,,

(193)

and similarly for the lowering operators

\mathsf{f}\,\langle\gamma\rangle
. In principle, the commutators of these operators
may involve
\mathsf{h}\,\langle\mathbf{p}\rangle
, which satisfies
\mathsf{h}\,\langle\mathbf{p}\rangle^{2}=0
as
a corollary of (
166
). We will see that, in fact,

\mathsf{h}\,\langle\mathbf{p}\rangle
acts by
0
in (
190
), except for one
exceptional case.

5.6.7

The vanishing
\mathsf{h}\,\langle\mathbf{p}\rangle
of admits the following geometric
interpretation and generalization. If
\chi^{2}_{0}\neq 0
then scheme-theoretic fixed locus

\mathsf{Fix}_{0}=\left(\mathsf{X}^{\vee}_{0}\right)^{\Gamma^{\prime}}
is a length two scheme with
coordinate ring generated by
\mathsf{h}\,\langle\mathbf{p}\rangle
modulo
\mathsf{h}\,\langle\mathbf{p}\rangle^{2}
.
The
\Gamma^{\prime}
-fixed locus
\mathsf{Fix}=\left(\mathsf{X}^{\vee}\right)^{\Gamma^{\prime}}
is,
however, reduced. Therefore,
\mathsf{h}\,\langle\mathbf{p}\rangle
acts by zero in any
module in the image of
\sigma_{*}
, where

\sigma^{*}:\Cliff_{\mathsf{X}^{\vee}_{0},\Gamma}\to\Cliff_{\mathsf{X}^{\vee},\Gamma}

(194)

is the pullback map. More generally, we have

\Big(\chi^{2}_{0}\neq 0\Big)\quad\Rightarrow\quad\mathsf{e}\mathsf{f},\mathsf{f}\mathsf{e}\in\Ker\sigma^{*}\,.

(195)

In particular,
\mathsf{h}\,\langle\mathbf{p}\rangle\in\Ker\sigma^{*}
as a
commutator of classes in
H^{1}(C,\mathfrak{sl}_{2}^{\chi})
.

5.7
Categorification of the L-genus

5.7.1

The Clifford algebra
\Cliff_{\mathsf{X},\Gamma}
has a natural spinor bundle

\Spin
that may be constructed as follows. There is a canonical
extension

0\to\Omega^{1}_{\textup{hor}}\mathsf{X}^{\vee}\to\Omega^{1}\mathsf{X}^{\vee}\to\Omega^{1}_{\textup{ver}}\mathsf{X}^{\vee}\to 0

where

\Omega^{1}_{\textup{hor}}\mathsf{X}^{\vee}\cong p^{*}\left(T^{*}\mathbb{P}^{1}\right)\subset\Omega^{1}\mathsf{X}^{\vee}

is the Lagrangian subbundle
formed by
1
-forms that vanish on the fibers of the projection
p:\mathsf{X}^{\vee}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
. It gives rise to an isotropic subbundle

H\Omega_{\mathsf{X}^{\vee},{\textup{hor}}}=H^{1}\left(C,\Omega^{1}_{\textup{hor}}\mathsf{X}^{\vee}\right)\left|\!\left[2\right]\!\right|\subset H\Omega_{\mathsf{X}^{\vee}}\,,

(196)

and hence to the spinor bundle

\Spin=\sqrt{\det}\otimes\Cliff_{\mathsf{X}^{\vee},\Gamma}\Big/\left(H\Omega_{\mathsf{X}^{\vee},{\textup{hor}}}\right)\,,

(197)

where we mod out by the left ideal generated by (
196
) and

\det=\Lambda^{\textup{top}}H\Omega_{\mathsf{X}^{\vee},{\textup{hor}}}=\left(\Lambda^{\textup{top}}H\Omega_{\mathsf{X}^{\vee},{\textup{ver}}}\right)^{-1}\,.

(198)

The existence of an equivariant square root is clear both when
\chi_{0}^{2}=1
and
when
\chi_{0}^{2}\neq 1
. In the former case, we have the cohomology with
coefficients in a trivial local system, while in the latter case

\dim\mathsf{Fix}=0
. See Section
3.3.7

for further discussion.

5.7.2

Following (
81
), we define

\widehat{\mathscr{O}}_{\mathsf{Fix},\textup{vir}}=\Spin\left|\!\left[-\delta\right]\!\right|\,,

(199)

where

\delta=\tfrac{1}{2}\dim\mathsf{Fix}=\begin{cases}1\,,\quad&\chi_{0}^{2}=1\,,\\
0\,,\quad&\chi_{0}^{2}\neq 1\,.\end{cases}

(200)

By construction, we have

\left[\widehat{\mathscr{O}}_{\mathsf{Fix},\textup{vir}}\right]=\widehat{\mathsf{L}}(\mathbb{P}^{1})=\widehat{\mathsf{L}}_{\tfrac{1}{2}}(T^{*}\mathbb{P}^{1})\,,

which may be interpreted either as equality of classes in equivariant
K-theory of
\mathsf{X}^{\vee}
, or as equality of distributions after pushing
forward to point modulo group. Our goal in this section is to lift
the relation between L-genera and constant terms to a relation between

\widehat{H}(\QMN)
and (
199
).

5.7.3

Constant term is a function of
\mathscr{M}\in\Pic(C)
and in order to
introduce an
\mathscr{M}
-dependent twist of
\Spin
we need a homomorphism

\Pic(C)(\Bbbk)\owns\mathscr{M}\mapsto\mathscr{O}(\mathscr{M})\in\Pic_{\Fr\times\mathsf{A}^{\sqbullet}}(\mathsf{X}^{\vee})\,,

(201)

such that the equivariant weights
\Fr\times\mathsf{A}^{\sqbullet}
-weights of the
restriction of
\mathscr{O}(\mathscr{M})
to the fixed points
\{0,\infty\}
are

\chi(\mathscr{M})^{-1}
and
\chi(\mathscr{M})
, respectively.

In general,
\Gamma^{\prime}
, or any other group, will act nontrivially on the
restriction of the natural line bundles to the
\Gamma^{\prime}
-fixed
locus. In particular, consider the line bundle
\mathscr{O}(1)
with
its natural linearization. This means that the group
\mathsf{A}^{\sqbullet}=\{\diag(a^{-1},a)\}
acts with the weights
a^{\pm 1}
over
over the
fixed points
\{0,\infty\}
,
respectively. It follows from (
189
) that an element

\gamma\in\Gamma
will act by
\chi(\gamma)^{\mp 1}

on the same fibers. Unless
\chi_{0}=1
, this does not factor through

\Gamma/\Gamma^{\prime}
.

Therefore, if we want
\Fr
to act on
\mathscr{O}(1)
, we need
to pick its lift to
\Gamma/[\Gamma,\Gamma]
. We get such a lift if we fix a
point
x\in C
of degree
1
or, more generally, a divisor
\mathscr{M}

of degree
1
. We denote
\mathscr{O}(\mathscr{M})
the bundle
\mathscr{O}(1)
with the
corresponding action of
\Fr
. This factors through
\Pic_{1}(C)(\Bbbk)

because
\chi_{0}
is a character of
\Pic_{0}(C)(\Bbbk)
. By linearity,
this assignment extends to (
201
).

5.7.4

Our main result in this section is the following

Theorem 2
.

For any
\mathscr{M}
we have

\widehat{H}(\QMN)=\sigma_{*,\mathbf{L}}\left(\widehat{\mathscr{O}}_{\mathsf{Fix},\textup{vir}}\otimes\mathscr{O}(\mathscr{M})\right)\,,

(202)

as a
\mathscr{A}^{\geq 1}_{\chi}(C)
-module, unless

(g,\mathscr{M},\chi_{0}^{2})=(2,\mathscr{O}_{C},\textup{nontrivial})\,.

(203)

The proof of this theorem will occupy Section
6
. The
exceptional case is analyzed in section
6.5
.
Among classical results to which Theorem
2
may be
directly comparable we mention
[
Semiinf2
,
Semiinf1
]
.

6
Proof of Theorem 2

6.1
Attracting manifolds and fixed loci

6.1.1

Consider the action of the group
\Aut(\mathscr{M})\cong\mathbb{G}_{\mathbf{m}}

on the space
\QMN
. It is clear, for instance from the description in section

5.3.6
, that it has two
basins of attraction as
z\in\mathbb{G}_{\mathbf{m}}
tends to
0\in\overline{\mathbb{G}_{\mathbf{m}}}
.
Namely,

\mathsf{Attr}(0)=\{s_{2}=0\}\,,\quad\mathsf{Attr}(\infty)=\{s_{2}\neq 0\}\,.

(204)

We denote the corresponding fix loci by

\QMN^{\mathbb{G}_{\mathbf{m}}}=\QM(0)\sqcup\QM(\infty)\,.

On these fixed loci, we have
\mathscr{V}=\mathscr{M}\oplus\mathscr{O}

and
s
is a quasisection of
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{M})
or
\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{O})
respectively.
These are both
\mathbb{P}^{0}
-bundles over
C
.

We observe that both
\QM(0)
and
\QM(\infty)
are isomorphic
to the symmetric product
{\mathsf{S}}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}=\bigsqcup\mathsf{S}^{d}C
or, equivalently,
the Hilbert scheme of points of
C
. Indeed, the isomorphism

{\mathsf{S}}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}C\to\QM(\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{M}))=\{\mathscr{L},s\in\mathbb{P}(H^{0}(\mathscr{M}\otimes\mathscr{L}^{-1}))\}

(205)

takes a divisor
D\in{\mathsf{S}}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}C
to the quasimap

\mathscr{L}=\mathscr{M}(-D)\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt}\mathscr{M}\,.

6.1.2

The following table

\NN_{s}

N_{\mathsf{Attr}/\textup{{fixed}}}

\rk N_{\mathsf{Attr}/\textup{{fixed}}}

\QM(0)\phantom{\Big|}

\mathscr{M}^{-1}(2D)

-{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(\mathscr{M})

-\deg\mathscr{M}+g-1

\QM(\infty)

\mathscr{M}(2D)

{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(\mathscr{M}(D))-{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(\mathscr{M})

\deg D

(206)

collects the information about the normal bundle (
132
)
to the fixed sections and about the normal bundle to the fixed
locus inside the attracting manifold. In particular,

\displaystyle{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}_{c}(\mathsf{Attr}(\infty))

\displaystyle={H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(\QM(\infty))

\displaystyle\left|\!\left[-2\deg D\right]\!\right|\,,

\displaystyle{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}_{c}(\mathsf{Attr}(0))

\displaystyle={H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(\QM(0))

\displaystyle\left|\!\left[2\deg\mathscr{M}+2-2g\right]\!\right|\,.

(207)

6.1.3

Consider the
long exact sequence of a pair

\textstyle{{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}_{c}(\QMN)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}_{c}(\mathsf{Attr}(\infty))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}_{c}(\mathsf{Attr}(0))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{[1]}

(208)

for cohomology with compact supports. Since both
cohomology groups in (
207
) are pure, we
conclude the following

Lemma 6.1
.

The connecting dotted map in (
208
) vanishes.

6.1.4

The fix loci have their own correspondences as in (
137
),
which we denote by
\mathsf{e}^{(0)}
and
\mathsf{e}^{(\infty)}
, respectively.
The main difference between quasisections of
\mathbb{P}^{k}
-bundles
for different
k
is the rank of normal bundle
\mathscr{N}
as in
(
140
). This rank equals
k+1
and hence
c_{k+1}(\mathscr{N})
is
used
in the definition of the correspondences. In particular, the
analog of (
160
) for
\mathbb{P}^{0}
-bundles reads

[\iota^{*},\iota_{*}]\circ\Delta_{C,*}=c_{1}(\mathscr{N}\otimes TC)-c_{1}(\mathscr{N})=\tau\,.

(209)

Thus

\left[\mathsf{e}^{(0)},\mathsf{f}^{(0)}\right]=(\textstyle{\int_{C}}\otimes 1)\,\Delta_{C}^{*}\,,

(210)

meaning that these correspondences generate a Heisenberg–Clifford
algebra
\mathfrak{Heis}
in place of
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
.

6.1.5

As before, a local system
\chi
defines a local system
\mathfrak{Heis}_{\chi}
, in which

\mathsf{e}
and
\mathsf{f}
are twisted by
\chi
and
\chi^{-1}
,
respectively. Note that, in accordance with the first column in
(
206
) and the Tate shift that
is present in (
207
), we need the local system

\mathfrak{Heis}_{\chi^{2}}
on
\QM(0)
and the local system
\mathfrak{Heis}_{q\chi^{2}}

on
\QM(\infty)
to make the triangle
(
208
) Galois-equivariant.

6.1.6

The following result is well-known and constitutes a
1
-dimensional analog
of Nakajima’s action on the Hilbert schemes of points of surfaces

[
NakHilb
]
.

Proposition 6.2
.

{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}({\mathsf{S}}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}C,\chi)
is a Verma module for
{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(C,\mathfrak{Heis}_{\chi})
.

For the trivial local system, this goes at least as far back as the
work of Macdonald
[
Macd
]
. For a nontrivial local, this is a
consequence of representation theory of Clifford algebras and the
following

Lemma 6.3
.

If
\chi
is nontrivial then
{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(\mathsf{S}^{d}C,\chi)=0
for
d>2g-2
.

Proof.

The map
S^{d}C\to\Pic_{d}(C)
is projective bundle for
d>2g-2
and
the pushforward of the constant sheaf under this map forms a trivial
local system on the base. Since the local system
\chi
is pulled
back from the base, and the cohomology of an abelian variety with
nontrivial coefficients vanishes, the claim follows.
∎

As a corollary of the proof, we note that
{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(\mathsf{S}^{2g-2}C,\chi)

vanishes except for

H^{2g-2}(\mathsf{S}^{2g-2}C,\chi)=\chi(\mathscr{K}_{C})\otimes H^{2g-2}(\mathbb{P}^{g-1})\,,

(211)

where
\mathscr{K}_{C}
is the canonical bundle of
C
and

\mathbb{P}^{g-1}\subset\mathsf{S}^{2g-2}C
is the canonical linear system. From
this and Proposition
6.2
one may observe the
classical fact that

\Lambda^{\textup{top}}{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(C,\chi)=\chi(\mathscr{K}_{C})\left|\!\left[2-2g\right]\!\right|

(212)

for any character
\chi
, trivial or not.

6.2
Localization is localization

6.2.1

Equivariant localization in topology
relates equivariant cohomology of a space, ordinary and
extraordinary, with the cohomology of the fixed loci.
In our case, this comparison is provided by exact
sequence (
208
) and the isomorphisms
(
207
).

Using these isomorphisms, we may express the

\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-action in terms of the Heisenberg operators.
Our goal in this subsection is to identify this topological
equivariant localization with localization in
representation theory as discussed in section

5.6
.

6.2.2

The map
{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}_{c}(\QMN)\to{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}_{c}(\mathsf{Attr}(0))
is pullback under
the embedding and thus commutes with the pullback
\iota^{*}
.
For the pushforward, we need to look at the normal bundle
\mathscr{N}
,
which fits into the sequence

0\to\underbrace{\mathscr{M}\otimes\mathscr{L}^{-1}\otimes\mathscr{O}_{\QM}(1)}_{\mathscr{N}^{(0)}}\to\mathscr{N}\to\underbrace{\mathscr{L}^{-1}\otimes\mathscr{O}_{\QM}(1)}_{\mathscr{N}^{(\infty)}}\to 0\,.

(213)

The map
\iota^{(0)}_{*}
lacks the quotient term in (
213
),
whence
the following

Proposition 6.4
.

The diagrams

\textstyle{{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}_{c}(C\times\QMN)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}_{c}(C\times\mathsf{Attr}(0))}
\textstyle{{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}_{c}(C\times\QMN)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota^{*}}
\textstyle{{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}_{c}(C\times\mathsf{Attr}(0))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota^{(0),*}}

\textstyle{{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}_{c}(C\times\QMN)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{*}}
\textstyle{{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}_{c}(C\times\mathsf{Attr}(0))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\square_{*}}
\textstyle{{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}_{c}(C\times\QMN)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}_{c}(C\times\mathsf{Attr}(0))}

(214)

where

\square_{*}=c_{1}(\mathscr{N}^{(\infty)})\,\iota^{(0)}_{*}=\iota^{(0)}_{*}c_{1}(\mathscr{N}^{(\infty)}\otimes TC)\,,

commute.

Note these formulas are consistent with interpreting the
virtual bundle

N_{\QMN/\mathsf{Attr}(0)}={H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(C,\mathscr{L}^{-1})\otimes\mathscr{O}_{\QM}(1)\,.

as the virtual normal bundle to
\mathsf{Attr}(0)
in
\QMN
. The
correspondence
\iota^{(0)}
changes it by

\iota^{(0),*}N_{\QMN/\mathsf{Attr}(0)}-N_{\QMN/\mathsf{Attr}(0)}=\mathscr{N}^{(\infty)}\otimes TC\,.

6.2.3

The normal bundle

N_{\QMN/\QM(\infty)}=\left({H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(\mathscr{M}\otimes\mathscr{L}^{-1})-{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}(\mathscr{M})\right)\otimes\mathscr{O}_{\QM}(1)

(215)

is a vector bundle of rank
-\deg\mathscr{L}
over

\QM(\infty)
.
The map
{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}_{c}(\mathsf{Attr}(\infty))\to{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}_{c}(\QMN)
commutes
with
\iota_{*}
, since both maps are pushforwards. For pullbacks, we
need to look at the effect of
\iota^{\infty}
on the normal bundle
(
215
). We deduce

Proposition 6.5
.

The diagrams

\textstyle{{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}_{c}(C\times\mathsf{Attr}(\infty))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}_{c}(C\times\QMN)}
\textstyle{{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}_{c}(C\times\mathsf{Attr}(\infty))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\square^{*}}
\textstyle{{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}_{c}(C\times\QMN)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota^{*}}

\textstyle{{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}_{c}(C\times\mathsf{Attr}(\infty))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota^{(\infty)}_{*}}
\textstyle{{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}_{c}(C\times\QMN)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{*}}
\textstyle{{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}_{c}(C\times\mathsf{Attr}(\infty))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{H}^{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}_{c}(C\times\QMN)}

(216)

where

\square^{*}=c_{1}(\mathscr{N}^{(0)}\otimes TC)\,\iota^{(\infty),*}=\iota^{(\infty),*}c_{1}(\mathscr{N}^{(0)})\,,

commute.

Note that the nontrivial squares in (
214
) and (
216
)
also follow from the trivial ones and the relations (
220
)
below.

6.2.4

One has the following analog of the Casimir element equation
(
164
)

\mathsf{f}^{(0)}_{13}\,\mathsf{e}^{(0)}_{23}\circ\Delta_{C,*}=\mathsf{h}^{(0)}\overset{\textup{\tiny def}}{=}(\textstyle{\int_{C}}\otimes 1)\circ c_{1}(\mathscr{N}^{(0)}\otimes TC)\,.

(217)

Indeed, (
217
) is just the composition of pushforward and
pullback by the same map. We can use (
168
) to expand out (
217
) along the
lines of (
165
).

Corollary 6.6
.

We have

\displaystyle\mathsf{e}^{(0)}\,\langle\mathbf{p}\rangle\,\mathsf{f}^{(0)}\,\langle\mathbf{p}\rangle

\displaystyle=\eta\,,

\displaystyle\mathsf{e}^{(0)}\,\langle\mathbf{p}\rangle\,\mathsf{f}^{(0)}\,\langle\alpha\rangle+\mathsf{e}^{(0)}\,\langle\alpha\rangle\,\mathsf{f}^{(0)}\,\langle\mathbf{p}\rangle

\displaystyle=\alpha

(218)

where
\alpha\in H^{1}(C)=H^{1}(\Pic(C))
acts on
\QMN
by
multiplication.

For brevity, let us denote by
FE
the following composition

FE=\mathsf{f}_{13}\,\mathsf{e}_{23}\circ\Delta_{C,*}=c_{2}(\mathscr{N}\otimes TC)

(219)

of pushforward
\mathsf{e}
and the pullback
\mathsf{f}
. Let

FE^{(0)}
denote the analogous expression appearing in (
217
). From (
213
),
we conclude

\displaystyle FE

\displaystyle=FE^{(0)}\,c_{1}(\mathscr{N}^{(\infty)}\otimes TC)

\displaystyle=c_{1}(\mathscr{N}^{(\infty)}\otimes TC)\,FE^{(0)}

(220)

\displaystyle=FE^{(\infty)}\,c_{1}(\mathscr{N}^{(0)}\otimes TC)

\displaystyle=c_{1}(\mathscr{N}^{(0)}\otimes TC)\,FE^{(\infty)}\,,

which provides a different computations of the nontrivial squares in
(
214
) and (
216
).

6.2.5

Writing out the equations (
214
) using
(
218
), we obtain

\displaystyle\mathsf{f}\,\langle\mathbf{p}\rangle

\displaystyle=\mathsf{f}^{(0)}\,\langle\mathbf{p}\rangle=Y

\displaystyle\mathsf{e}\,\langle\mathbf{p}\rangle

\displaystyle=\eta\,\mathsf{e}^{(0)}\,\langle\mathbf{p}\rangle=X^{2}Y

\displaystyle\mathsf{f}\,\langle\alpha\rangle

\displaystyle=\mathsf{f}^{(0)}\,\langle\alpha\rangle=dY

\displaystyle\mathsf{e}\,\langle\alpha\rangle

\displaystyle=\eta\,\mathsf{e}^{(0)}\,\langle\alpha\rangle+\alpha\,\mathsf{e}^{(0)}\,\langle\mathbf{p}\rangle=2XYdX+X^{2}dY\,,

(221)

where we use the shorthand

X=\mathsf{e}^{(0)}\,\langle\mathbf{p}\rangle\,,\quad Y=\mathsf{f}^{(0)}\,\langle\mathbf{p}\rangle\,,\quad dX=\mathsf{e}^{(0)}\,\langle\alpha\rangle\,,\quad dY=\mathsf{f}^{(0)}\,\langle\alpha\rangle\,.

These formulas have a geometric interpretation which may be
illustrated using Figure (
176
). Let
X
and
Y
be the
coordinates around
0\in\mathsf{X}^{\vee}
with weights
a^{-2}
and
qa^{2}
,
respectively. Clearly, the first two lines in (
221
) describe
the map
\sigma
in the coordinates
X
and
Y
, while the second two
lines describe the action of
\sigma^{*}
on
\Omega^{1}
.
Let

\textup{Chart}_{0}=\mathsf{X}^{\vee}\setminus T^{*}_{\infty}\,,\quad\textup{Chart}_{\infty}=\mathsf{X}^{\vee}\setminus T^{*}_{0}\,,

be two affine charts covering
\mathsf{X}^{\vee}
, where
T^{*}_{0}
and
T^{*}_{\infty}
denote the cotangent fibers over
0,\infty\in\mathbb{P}^{1}
. We rephrase (
221
) as follows

Proposition 6.7
.

The equivariant localization homomorphism

\mathscr{A}^{\geq 1}(C)\to\mathscr{A}^{(0),\geq 1}(C)\oplus\mathscr{A}^{(\infty),\geq 1}(C)

given in (
214
) and (
216
) coincide with the
natural maps of the Clifford algebras

\Cliff_{\mathsf{X}^{\vee}_{0}}\xrightarrow{\hskip 7.97224pt\textup{restriction}\,\circ\,\sigma^{*}\hskip 7.97224pt}\Cliff_{\textup{Chart}_{0}}\oplus\Cliff_{\textup{Chart}_{\infty}}\,.

This forms a direct link between equivariant localization and
localization in representation theory.

6.3
The stable range

6.3.1

Our next goal is to prove Theorem
2
outside the vertex of the
cone, that is, one the set where
\sigma
is an isomorphism.
This set is covered by two affine charts

\mathsf{X}_{0}^{\vee}\setminus\{0\}=\{\textup{$\mathsf{e}\,\langle\mathbf{p}\rangle$ is invertible}\}\cup\{\textup{$\mathsf{f}\,\langle\mathbf{p}\rangle$ is invertible}\}\,.

Since the degree of a quasimap is always bounded below, the lowering
operators
\mathsf{f}
are locally nilpotent. Thus both sides of equality
in Theorem
2
restrict to zero on the chart where

\mathsf{f}\,\langle\mathbf{p}\rangle
is invertible.

Since
\mathsf{e}\,\langle\mathbf{p}\rangle
is a raising operator, inverting it involves
understanding of the
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-action in the stable range
\deg\mathscr{L}\ll 0
. We will see
in Section
6.4
that
all cohomology vanish in this range if
\chi_{0}^{2}\neq 1
. Therefore, our
focus in this section is on cohomology with trivial local system
coefficients.

6.3.2

If
\mathsf{e}\,\langle\mathbf{p}\rangle
is invertible then the Casimir relation (
164
)
determine the action of
\mathsf{f}
from the action of
\mathsf{e}
and
\mathsf{h}
,
that is, from the action of the Borel subalgebra
\mathfrak{b}\subset\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
.
We recall from Proposition
5.5
that the operators
\mathsf{h}\,\langle\mathbf{p}\rangle

and
\mathsf{h}\,\langle\alpha\rangle
act by a multiple of
\eta
and
\alpha
,
respectively, where
\alpha\in H^{1}(C)=H^{1}(\Pic C)
acts by cup
product with the pullback of
\alpha
to
\QMN
. The commutator

\left[\alpha_{1},\mathsf{e}\,\langle\alpha_{2}\rangle\right]=\mathsf{e}\,\langle\alpha_{1}\cup\alpha_{2}\rangle

(222)

is the only nontrivial commutator in
\mathfrak{b}\otimes H^{\geq 1}(C)
.

6.3.3

Let
\mathscr{F}
be any locally free Clifford module on
\mathsf{X}_{0}^{\vee}
. It is
clear from the discussion in Section
5.5.8
that

H^{1}_{\mathbf{L}}(\mathsf{X}_{0}^{\vee}\setminus\{0\},\mathscr{F})\cong\left(\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}[\eta^{\pm 1}]\big/\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}[\eta]\right)\otimes\underbrace{\mathscr{F}\Big|_{\mathbf{L}_{\infty}\setminus\{0\}}}_{\Ker\eta}\,,

(223)

as
\mathscr{A}^{\geq 1}
-modules, while
H^{i}_{\mathbf{L}}=0
for
i\neq 1
. One may
call vectors annihilated
by
\eta
primitive. We say that a module of the form (
223
) is
freely
\eta
-cogenerated by its primitive elements.

Proposition 6.8
.

The
\mathscr{A}^{\geq 1}
-module
\widehat{H}(\QMN)
with
\mathsf{e}\,\langle\mathbf{p}\rangle
inverted
is freely
\eta
-cogenerated by its primitive elements. The primitive
elements are freely generated under the action of the operators

\mathsf{e}\,\langle\mathbf{p}\rangle^{\pm 1}
and
\mathsf{e}\,\langle\alpha\rangle
,

\alpha\in H^{1}(C)
,
by any
1
-dimensional subspace of the form

H^{\textup{top}}_{c}(\QMN_{d})
, where
d=\deg\mathscr{L}\ll 0
.

Evidently,
H^{\textup{top}}_{c}(\QMN_{d})
is annihilated by cup products
by
\alpha\in H^{1}(C)
. Thus, the above proposition and (
222
)
completely determines the structure of this module. The shifts and
twists in definition of both sides of (
202
) are
arranged so that primitive elements equal the
restriction of
\Spin\otimes\mathscr{O}(\mathscr{M})\bm{(}\tfrac{\deg\mathscr{M}+\delta}{2}\bm{)}
to
\mathbf{L}_{\infty}\setminus\{0\}
. Therefore,
we have the following

Corollary 6.9
.

Theorem
2
is true outside the vertex of the
cone.

The proof of Proposition
6.8
will occupy the rest of this
subsection.

6.3.4

Let
\QM_{d}\subset\QM
denote the component with
\deg\mathscr{L}=d
,
and similarly for
\QMN_{d}
. We recall from Section
5.3.4

that
\QM_{d}\to\Pic_{d}(C)
is a projective bundle for
d\ll 0
. Similarly,
\QMN_{d}
is
the quotient of a projective bundle over
\Pic_{d}(C)\times H^{1}(\mathscr{M})
by the action of the unipotent group
H^{0}(\mathscr{M})
.
By the projective bundle theorem, we have

H_{c}(\QMN_{d})\cong\underbrace{{\Lambda}^{\!\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}\!\,H^{1}(C)}_{\textup{coefficients}}\,\big[\eta\big]\Big/\mathsf{C}_{\mathscr{O}\oplus\mathscr{M}}(\eta)\,,\quad d\ll 0\,,

(224)

as a module over
\eta
and
H^{1}(C)
, where

\mathsf{C}_{\mathscr{V}}(\eta)=\textup{Chern
polynomial}(H^{0}(C,\mathscr{V}\otimes\mathscr{L}^{-1}),\eta)\,.

While we can argue with just abstract properties of this Chern
polynomial, it is easy to describe it concretely.

6.3.5

Since
H^{0}(C,\mathscr{V}\otimes\mathscr{L}^{-1})
is obtained by pushforward
along
C
, which is
1
-dimensional, we conclude that

\textup{ch}_{i}\left(H^{0}(\mathscr{V}\otimes\mathscr{L}^{-1})\right)=0\,,\quad i>1\,.

On the other hand, by the definition and symmetry of the theta divisor

\Theta
, we have

c_{1}\left(H^{0}(\mathscr{V}\otimes\mathscr{L}^{-1})\right)=\rk(\mathscr{V})\,\Theta\,,\quad\Theta=\sum_{i=1}^{g}\gamma_{i}\gamma^{\vee}_{i}\,.

(225)

Therefore,

\mathsf{C}_{\mathscr{V}}(\eta)=\eta^{\rk H^{0}(\mathscr{V}\otimes\mathscr{L}^{-1})}e^{-\textup{ch}_{1}/\eta}=\eta^{-rd+\const}\prod_{i=1}^{g}(\eta-r\gamma_{i}\gamma^{\vee}_{i})\,,\quad r=\rk(\mathscr{V})\,,

(226)

where the constant equals
\deg\mathscr{V}+r-(r+1)g
. Importantly, this
stabilizes in that it depends on
d
only through the
\eta^{-rd}

factor.

6.3.6

It is easy to describe the action of the operators
\mathsf{e}
and
\mathsf{f}
in
the stable range. Let
P=P(\eta,\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\dots)
be an
element of the ring in the right-hand side of (
224
).

Lemma 6.10
.

We have

\displaystyle\mathsf{f}\,\langle\mathbf{p}\rangle\,P

\displaystyle=-P\,,

\displaystyle\qquad\mathsf{f}\,\langle\alpha\rangle\,P

\displaystyle=-\frac{\partial}{\partial\alpha^{\vee}}P\,,

(227)

\displaystyle\mathsf{e}\,\langle\mathbf{p}\rangle\,P

\displaystyle=\eta^{2}P\,,

\displaystyle\qquad\mathsf{e}\,\langle\alpha\rangle\,P

\displaystyle=\eta^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial\alpha^{\vee}}P-2\eta\alpha P\,.

(228)

Proof.

The pullback
\iota^{*}
is an algebra homomorphism which acts by

\displaystyle\iota^{*}(1\otimes\alpha)

\displaystyle=\alpha\otimes 1+1\otimes\alpha\,,

\displaystyle\alpha\in H^{1}(C)\,,

(229)

\displaystyle\iota^{*}(1\otimes\eta)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{p}\otimes 1+1\otimes\eta\,,

(230)

where
\mathbf{p}\in H^{2}(C)
is the generator. The relation (
229
)
was already discussed in the proof of Proposition
5.5
. To see (
230
),
note that
\eta
is represented by the divisor where a linear function of

s(x)
vanishes for some fixed
x\in C
.

The equations (
227
) follows from the definition of
\mathsf{f}
and
the above description of
\iota^{*}
. Formulas (
228
)
follow from the Casimir relations (
164
).
∎

6.3.7

The line
H^{\textup{top}}_{c}(\QMN_{d})
is spanned by
\eta^{\max}\Lambda^{\textup{top}}H^{1}(C)
, where the maximal power of
\eta
is
one less than the degree of
\mathsf{C}(\eta)
. It is clear that the action
of
\mathsf{e}\,\langle\mathbf{p}\rangle^{\pm 1}
takes all these elements to each other.

By
looking at the lowest degree term in the odd variables, it is clear
that the action
\mathsf{e}\,\langle\alpha\rangle
generates a
2^{2g}
-dimensional
space of primitive vectors from
H^{\textup{top}}_{c}(\QMN_{d})
.
These have to exhaust all primitive vectors and this completes
the proof of Proposition
6.8
.

In fact, it is not difficult
to see directly that the product of all
\mathsf{e}\,\langle\alpha\rangle
, applied to
H^{\textup{top}}_{c}
reproduces
precisely the relation
\mathsf{C}(\eta)
.

6.4
Completion of the proof

6.4.1

By Proposition
6.2
, the exact sequence (
208
)
translates into the exact sequence

0\to\textup{Verma}\to\widehat{H}(\QMN)\to\textup{dual Verma}\to 0\,,

(231)

where we call a
\mathscr{A}_{\chi}(C)
-module a Verma module if it is free as a module over
the commuting operators
\mathsf{e}
, while a dual Verma
module is dual to a free module as a module over the commuting
operators
\mathsf{f}
. Note this definition also involves operators coming
from
H^{0}(C)
.

Our plan is to compare this sequence with the exact sequence
of the pair

0\to\sigma_{\mathbf{L}_{\infty},*}\to\sigma_{\mathbf{L},*}\to\sigma_{\mathbf{L}_{0},*}\to 0\,,

(232)

as in (
181
). We will first compare the submodules and
quotient modules, and then compare the extensions.

6.4.2

It is clear from Proposition
6.7
that the terms of the
filtration (
232
) for
\sigma_{\mathbf{L},*}\widehat{\mathscr{O}}_{\mathsf{Fix},\textup{vir}}\otimes\mathscr{O}(M)

are similarly
a Verma
module and a dual Verma module. Therefore, it suffices to compare
the weights of the generators. This is done in the following

Lemma 6.11
.

For both
\widehat{H}(\QMN)
and
\sigma_{\mathbf{L},*}\widehat{\mathscr{O}}_{\mathsf{Fix},\textup{vir}}\otimes\mathscr{O}(M)
, we have

generator of the submodule

\displaystyle=\chi(\mathscr{M}\otimes\mathscr{K}_{C}^{-1})

\displaystyle\left|\!\left[g-1\right]\!\right|\,,

generator of the quotient

\displaystyle=\chi(\mathscr{M}^{-1}\otimes\mathscr{K}_{C}^{-1})

\displaystyle\left|\!\left[1-g\right]\!\right|\,.

(233)

Proof.

For
\widehat{H}(\QMN)
, this is an immediate consequence of
the definition (
187
) and table (
206
). In the
computations with the spinor bundle, we will need the number

\delta=\delta_{\{\chi_{0}^{2}=1\}}\in\{0,1\}
, where
\delta_{\{\chi_{0}^{2}=1\}}
denotes the indicator function. From
(
212
)
we deduce

\left(\Lambda^{\textup{top}}H^{1}(C,\chi^{2})\right)^{1/2}=\chi(\mathscr{K}_{C})\chi^{2\delta}\left|\!\left[1-g-\delta\right]\!\right|\,.

(234)

Consider the bundle
\Spin
. The generator of the submodule
and the quotient modules come from the elements
1\in\Cliff
and
the product of all
\mathsf{f}\,\langle\alpha_{i}\rangle
,
respectively. With the square root twists, these have the weight

\left(\Lambda^{\textup{top}}H^{1}(C,\chi^{2})\right)^{-1/2}

and
\left(\Lambda^{\textup{top}}H^{1}(C,\chi^{-2})\right)^{1/2}
,
respectively. These weights form the first row in the following
table

contributions from

quotient

sub

\Spin

\chi(\mathscr{K}_{C})^{-1}\chi^{-2\delta}\left|\!\left[1-g-\delta\right]\!\right|

\chi(\mathscr{K}_{C})^{-1}\chi^{-2\delta}\left|\!\left[\delta+g-1\right]\!\right|

\widehat{\mathscr{O}}_{\mathsf{Fix},\textup{vir}}

\left|\!\left[-\delta\right]\!\right|

\left|\!\left[-\delta\right]\!\right|

\mathscr{O}(\mathscr{M})

\chi(\mathscr{M})^{-1}

\chi(\mathscr{M})

local cohomology

\chi^{2\delta}\left|\!\left[2\delta\right]\!\right|

\chi^{2\delta}

(235)

where the shifts in local cohomology appear as in the computation done
in section
5.5.8
. Collecting terms in the above table
concludes
the proof.
∎

6.4.3

To finish the proof, we need to identify the classes of the
extension. If
\chi^{2}_{0}=1
, then the submodule is free as
a module over
\mathsf{e}\,\langle\mathbf{p}\rangle
. This means the extension class
is uniquely determined by its localization with respect to

\mathsf{e}\,\langle\mathbf{p}\rangle
. By Proposition
6.8
, the extension
classes coinside after such localization, completing the
proof in the
\chi^{2}_{0}=1
case.

6.4.4

Consider the case
\chi^{2}_{0}\neq 1
, in which case the
statement of the theorem is equivalent to the splitting of the
module
\widehat{H}(\QMN,\chi)
. The submodule
is a free module over
\mathsf{e}\,\langle\alpha_{i}\rangle
, with the operators

\mathsf{f}\,\langle\alpha_{j}\rangle
acting by zero. For the quotient module,
the situation is reversed. Thus
\mathsf{f}\,\langle\alpha_{j}\rangle\,\mathsf{e}\,\langle\alpha_{i}\rangle=0
for any
\alpha_{i}
and
\alpha_{j}
, and the module splits
if and only if the product in the other order also vanishes. This is
equivalent to the vanishing of the commutator
\mathsf{h}\,\langle\mathbf{p}\rangle
.

6.4.5

Both the sub and the quotient module are nonzero for a single
cohomological degree for given degree of the quasimap.
From the considerations of degrees, we see that

\rk\mathsf{h}\,\langle\mathbf{p}\rangle\leq 1
. Since
\mathsf{h}\,\langle\mathbf{p}\rangle
is central, we conclude

\Image\mathsf{f}\,\langle\alpha_{i}\rangle\subset\Ker\mathsf{h}\,\langle\mathbf{p}\rangle\,,\quad\Image\mathsf{h}\,\langle\mathbf{p}\rangle\subset\Ker\mathsf{e}\,\langle\alpha_{i}\rangle\,,

for any
\alpha_{i}
.
Therefore, if nonzero,
\mathsf{h}\,\langle\mathbf{p}\rangle
has to map the generator of the
quotient to the cogenerator of the submodule. Comparing the
degrees, we see that this is only possible if

g=2\,,\quad\deg\mathscr{M}=0\,.

This case will be analyzed explicitly below and, indeed,

\mathsf{h}\,\langle\mathbf{p}\rangle\neq 0
when
\mathscr{M}=\mathscr{O}_{C}
.

6.5
The exceptional case

6.5.1

Consider the case
g=2
,
\mathscr{M}=\mathscr{O}_{C}
. In this case, the generator of
the quotient module and the cogenerator of the submodule both
correspond to the class of
H^{2}(\mathsf{S}^{2}C,\chi)
represented by
the canonical linear system
\mathbb{P}^{1}\subset\mathsf{S}^{2}C
. This cycle
lies in the fiber

\textstyle{\QMN_{\mathscr{K}^{-1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\QMN_{-2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathscr{K}^{-1}_{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Pic_{-2}(C)\,,}

over the point
\mathscr{L}=\mathscr{K}^{-1}_{C}
. The moduli space
\QMN_{\mathscr{K}^{-1}}
is formed by all possible extensions

0\to\mathscr{O}\to\mathscr{V}\to\mathscr{O}\to 0

together with a section

s\in H^{0}(\mathscr{K}\otimes\mathscr{V})

taken modulo the action of
\mathbb{G}_{\mathbf{m}}\times H^{0}(\mathscr{O})\subset\Aut(\mathscr{V})
.
The operator
\mathsf{h}\,\langle\mathbf{p}\rangle
acts by a multiple of
\eta=c_{1}(\mathscr{O}(1))
.
It descends from the same action on the space
\widetilde{\QMN}_{\mathscr{K}^{-1}}
, where we divide
by the action of
\mathbb{G}_{\mathbf{m}}
only.

6.5.2

Consider the exact sequence

0\to H^{0}(\mathscr{K})\to H^{0}(\mathscr{K}\otimes\mathscr{V})\to H^{0}(\mathscr{K})\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt\beta_{\mathscr{V}}\hskip 8.19447pt}H^{1}(\mathscr{K})

where the map
\beta
is the pairing with the class of
the extension

\beta_{\mathscr{V}}\in\Ext^{1}(\mathscr{O},\mathscr{O})=H^{0}(\mathscr{K})^{*}\,.

Possible bundles
\mathscr{V}
are precisely parameterized by
their classes
\beta_{\mathscr{V}}
. Denoting
K=H^{0}(\mathscr{K})
, we obtain
the following description of
\widetilde{\QMN}_{\mathscr{K}^{-1}}

as a subset of
\mathbb{P}(K^{\oplus 2})\times K^{*}

\textstyle{\mathbb{P}(K\oplus\beta^{\perp})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widetilde{\QMN}_{\mathscr{K}^{-1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\beta\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K^{*}\,.}

(236)

The action of
\mathbb{G}_{\mathbf{m}}=\Aut\mathscr{M}
corresponds to scaling the first copy
of
K
in
K^{\oplus 2}
.

6.5.3

In terms of the diagram (
236
), we generator of the quotient
module and the cogenerator of the submodule are dual to
the fundamental cycle in
H_{8}
and
the cycle
\left[\mathbb{P}(K)\right]\in H_{6}
, respectively. Similarly,

\eta
is dual to
\left[\mathbb{P}(\textup{line}\oplus\beta^{\perp})\right]\in H_{6}
. One can move the line off the origin and then send it to
infinity by the action of
\mathbb{G}_{\mathbf{m}}=\Aut\mathscr{M}
. Thus

\left[\mathbb{P}(K)\right]=\left[\mathbb{P}(\textup{line}\oplus\beta^{\perp})\right]

and so
\mathsf{h}\,\langle\mathbf{p}\rangle\neq 0
in this instance.

6.5.4

If
\mathscr{M}\not\cong\mathscr{O}
then the classes corresponding to
H^{2}(\mathsf{S}^{2}C,\chi)

lie over different fibers of the projection to
\Pic_{-2}(C)
, thus
cannot possible be related via multiplication by
\eta
. This
concludes
the proof of Theorem
2
.

7
Appendix. Rank 2 bundles over
\mathbb{P}^{1}

7.1
\Bun_{\mathsf{G}}
and
\mathsf{Gr}_{\mathsf{G}}

7.1.1

This appendix is elementary and written for readers with no background in
automorphic forms. Our main objective
here is to explain by means of the simplest example how spectral
decompositions are encoded by certain additive decompositions of
distributions. We also explain what these distributions have to do
with constant terms of Eisenstein series. Finally, we point out
how this elementary example fits into the general story told in our
paper.

7.1.2

Let
C=\mathbb{P}^{1}
be the projective line over a finite field
\Bbbk
with

q
elements.
More generally, one may replace
\mathbb{P}^{1}
by a smooth
projective curve over

\Bbbk
which remains irreducible over the algebraic
closure
\overline{\Bbbk}
, and that would be setting of
Section
5
. We denote by
\mathbb{F}=\Bbbk(C)
the field of rational functions on
C
. For

C=\mathbb{P}^{1}
, this simply means
\mathbb{F}=\Bbbk(t)
, where

t
is an indeterminate.

A closed point
x\in\mathbb{P}^{1}
other than
\infty\in\mathbb{P}^{1}
corresponds
to an irreducible monic polynomials in
t
. The degree
\deg_{x}
is
the degree of this polynomial. We denote by
\Bbbk_{x}
the
corresponding finite extension of
\Bbbk
. Consider
the completion
\mathbb{F}_{x}
of
\mathbb{F}
at
x
and its subring

\Bbbk_{x}(\!(t)\!)\cong\mathbb{F}_{x}\supset\mathbb{O}_{x}\cong\Bbbk_{x}[\![t]\!]

(237)

formed
by functions regular at
x
. Here
\Bbbk_{x}(\!(t)\!)
and
\Bbbk_{x}[\![t]\!]

denote formal Laurent and Taylor series, respectively.
One defines integral adeles and all adeles of
\mathbb{F}
,
respectively, by

\mathbb{O}=\prod_{x\in C}\mathbb{O}_{x}\subset{\prod_{x\in C}}^{\prime}\,\mathbb{F}_{x}=\mathbb{A}

(238)

where the prime in (
238
) means that any adele is integral for
all but finitely many
x
.

7.1.3

A vector bundle
\mathscr{V}
over
C
may be trivialized in the formal
neighborhood of each closed point
x
and also at the generic point.
The corresponding transition function identifies isomorphism classes
of vector bundles with
double cosets

\Bun_{\mathsf{G}}=\mathsf{G}(\mathbb{F})\backslash\mathsf{G}(\mathbb{A})/\mathsf{G}(\mathbb{O})\,,

(239)

where
\mathsf{G}=GL(n)
,
n=\rk\mathscr{V}
.

For any reductive
\mathsf{G}
, the
double cosets (
239
) describe the
\Bbbk
-points of the stack
\cBun_{\mathsf{G}}
of

\mathsf{G}
-bundles on
C
. For

\mathsf{G}=SL(n),PGL(n)

this is the same as vector bundles with trivial
determinant, and vector bundles up to tensoring with a line bundle,
respectively. The latter can be also identified with

\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
-bundles
\mathscr{P}
over the curve
C
.

7.1.4

A very special property of
C=\mathbb{P}^{1}
is that every vector bundle is a
direct sum of line bundles. As a result, the
structure of
\Bun_{\mathsf{G}}
, and the corresponding theory of automorphic
forms, becomes almost trivial. Concretely,

\Bun_{SL(2)}=\left\{\mathscr{O}(m)\oplus\mathscr{O}(-m)\right\}_{m\geq 0}\hookrightarrow\Bun_{PGL(2)}=\left\{\mathscr{P}_{k}\right\}_{k\geq 0}\,,\quad\mathscr{P}_{k}=\mathbb{P}\left(\mathscr{O}(k)\oplus\mathscr{O}\right)\,,

(240)

where the map takes a rank 2 bundle to associated

\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle and its image consist of
\mathscr{P}_{k}
with
k
even.

For
C=\mathbb{P}^{1}
, there are no cusp forms, no Galois
representations, Eisenstein series are equal to their constant term,
etc. Luckily, the structures that we want to illustrate survive even
in this simplest case and can give an idea about how things work in
general.

7.1.5

Let
x\in C
be a point of degree 1.
The bundles in (
240
) are trivial in the complement of
x
,
which means that

\displaystyle\Bun_{\mathsf{G}}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{G}(\mathscr{O}_{C\setminus x})\backslash\mathsf{G}(\mathbb{F}_{x})/\mathsf{G}(\mathbb{O}_{x})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{G}(\Bbbk[t^{-1}])\backslash\mathsf{G}(\Bbbk(\!(t)\!))/\mathsf{G}(\Bbbk[\![t]\!])\,,

(241)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{G}(\Bbbk[t^{-1}])\backslash\mathsf{Gr}_{\mathsf{G}}

where

\displaystyle\mathsf{Gr}_{\mathsf{G}}=\mathsf{G}(\Bbbk(\!(t)\!))/\mathsf{G}(\Bbbk[\![t]\!])\,,

(242)

are the
\Bbbk
-points of the affine Grassmannian.
Among other
things,
\mathsf{Gr}_{\mathsf{G}}
describes
\mathsf{G}
-bundles over
\mathbb{P}^{1}
trivialized
in the complement of a
\Bbbk
-point
x
.

7.1.6

For
\mathsf{G}=GL(n)
, we have

\mathsf{Gr}_{GL(n)}=\{\textup{$\Bbbk[\![t]\!]$-lattices $L\subset\Bbbk(\!(t)\!)^{\oplus n}$}\}\,.

The lattice
L(\mathscr{V})
corresponding to a vector bundle
\mathscr{V}
is
described as follows

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 25.79514pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-25.79514pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{H^{0}(\mathbb{D}_{x},\mathscr{V})\,\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 25.79515pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@hook{1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 49.79514pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}{\hbox{\kern-14.875pt\raise-32.55113pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{L(\mathscr{V})\,\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.87502pt\raise-32.55113pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@hook{1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 56.07343pt\raise-32.55113pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 56.07343pt\raise-32.55113pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\Bbbk(\!(t)\!)^{\oplus n}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\,,

(243)

where

\mathbb{D}_{x}=\Spec\Bbbk[\![t]\!]\,,\quad\mathbb{D}^{\times}_{x}=\Spec\Bbbk(\!(t)\!)\,,

are the formal neighborhood of
x
and
the punctured formal neighborhood of
x
.
For
\mathsf{G}=SL(n)
and
\mathsf{G}=PGL(n)
one considers lattices
L

such that
\Lambda^{n}L=\Bbbk[\![t]\!]
and lattices up to scale,
respectively.

7.1.7

For
\mathsf{G}=PGL(2)
, the points of (
242
) correspond to vertices
of a regular
(q+1)
-valent tree, see
[
Serre
]
. This is the rank 1 case
of the general Bruhat-Tits building. Edges
L\textup{---}L^{\prime}
of this tree correspond to
pairs of lattices such that

L\supsetneq L^{\prime}\supsetneq tL\,.

Note this is a symmetric relation.
For
q=2
, the tree is plotted in
(
244
).

(244)

Orbits of
\mathsf{G}(\Bbbk[\![t]\!])
on
\mathsf{Gr}_{\mathsf{G}}
for
q=2

In (
244
), vertices of the tree are represented by circles and their color
reflects the distance from the root vertex (
=
the trivial bundle).

The stabilizer of the root vertex in

\mathsf{G}(\Bbbk(\!(t)\!))
is
\mathsf{G}(\Bbbk[\![t]\!])
,
which acts transitively on the sphere of each radius around the
root. In other words, any path without backtracking
from the root to infinity gives a fundamental domain for
\mathsf{G}(\Bbbk[\![t]\!])
.
Three such paths are plotted in (
244
). The tree in
(
244
) is the nonarchimedian analog of the unit disk,
equivalently,
upper half-plane, and the action of
\mathsf{G}(\Bbbk[\![t]\!])
should be compared
to the action of the maximal compact subgroup
SO(2,\mathbb{R})\subset SL(2,\mathbb{R})
.

The subset

\mathsf{Gr}_{SL(2)}\subset\mathsf{Gr}_{PGL(2)}

is formed by vertices at even distance from the root.

7.2
Hecke operators

7.2.1

The spherical Hecke algebra

\displaystyle\SH_{x}=\,\,

compactly supported functions

on
\mathsf{G}(\Bbbk[\![t]\!])\backslash\mathsf{G}(\Bbbk(\!(t)\!))/\mathsf{G}(\Bbbk[\![t]\!])

(245)

acts by right convolution on function on
\mathsf{Gr}_{\mathsf{G}}
. It is
clear from the description of orbits in (
244
) that
\SH_{x}

for
\mathsf{G}=PGL(2)

is an algebra of polynomials in one generator
\Delta
which may be
chosen as the graph Laplacian

\left[\Delta f\right](L)=\sum_{L\textup{---}L^{\prime}}f(L^{\prime})\,.

(246)

The spherical Hecke algebra for
\mathsf{G}=SL(2)
is spanned by even powers of

\Delta
.

7.2.2

In terms of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundles
\mathscr{P}\to C
, the action of
\Delta
is
given by modifications as in (
121
) in Section
5.2.2
.
A section
s
of
\mathscr{P}
in
(
121
)
determines a unique section
s^{\prime}
of
\mathscr{P}^{\prime}
. Self-intersections of

s
and
s^{\prime}
are related by

(s^{\prime},s^{\prime})-(s,s)=\begin{cases}-1\,,&y\in s\,,\\
+1\,,&y\notin s\,,\end{cases}

(247)

where
y
is a point in the fiber of
\mathscr{P}
over
x\in C

that one blows up in the
process of modification. Note there are
(q+1)
choices for
y
since
we assume
\deg x=1
.

The bundles
\mathscr{P}_{k}
for
k>0
are distinguished by having a unique section

s_{\infty}:C=\mathbb{P}\left(\mathscr{O}(k)\right)\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt}\mathbb{P}\left(\mathscr{O}\oplus\mathscr{O}(k)\right)=\mathscr{P}_{k}

(248)

with a negative self-intersection
(s_{\infty},s_{\infty})=-k
.
According to (
247
),
the corresponding
(q+1)
neighbors of
\mathscr{P}_{k}
look
like this:

{{{{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}}}}{}{{{{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 16.13 -20.22 L 28.06 -35.18}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

{{{{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}}}}{}{{{{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -5.76 25.22 L -10.01 43.87}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{}}{}{{}}
{{{{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}}}}{}{{{{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -5.76 -25.22 L -10.01 -43.87}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
