{{{{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}}}}{}{{{{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -23.31 11.22 L -40.54 19.52}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{}}{}{{}}
{{{{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}}}}{}{{{{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -23.31 -11.22 L -40.54 -19.52}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{}}{}{{}}
{{{{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}}}}{}{{{{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -25.87 0 L -45 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\,,\quad k>0\,,

(249)

The neighborhood of
\mathscr{P}_{k}
in
\mathsf{Gr}_{\mathsf{G}}
.

where abbreviate
\mathscr{P}_{k}
to just
k
. Note that
\mathscr{P}_{-k}\cong\mathscr{P}_{k}
; with this in mind one does not need to distinguish between
the two cases
in (
249
).

7.2.3

The bundles
\mathscr{P}_{k}
represents the the
\mathsf{G}(\Bbbk[t^{-1}])
-orbits
on
\mathsf{Gr}_{\mathsf{G}}
for
\mathsf{G}=PGL(2)
. The local rules (
249
)
give a unique reconstruction of those up to an automorphism of the
tree, see Figure (
250
).

(250)

Orbits of
\mathsf{G}(\Bbbk[t^{-1}])
in
\mathsf{Gr}_{\mathsf{G}}
.

The grayscale in (
250
) encodes the isomorphism type as
follows

\mathscr{P}_{0}
\mathscr{P}_{1}
\mathscr{P}_{2}
\mathscr{P}_{3}
\mathscr{P}_{4}
\mathscr{P}_{5}
\dots

(251)

Any chain of the form (
251
) is a
fundamental domain for
PSL(2,\Bbbk[t^{-1}])
. Three such domain
are plotted in (
250
). They differ by shifts by element of the
subgroup

\Bbbk^{\infty}\cong\left\{\begin{pmatrix}1&\Bbbk[t^{-1}]\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\right\}\subset PSL(2,\Bbbk[t^{-1}])\,,

(252)

which fixes the corresponding cusp.

One may compare and
contrast this action with its Archimedean analog, namely, the action
of
PSL(2,\mathbb{Z})
on the hyperbolic plane.
The subgroup (
252
) is the nonarchimedian analog of

\left\{\begin{pmatrix}1&\mathbb{Z}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\right\}\subset PSL(2,\mathbb{Z})\,.

Together with
PSL(2,\Bbbk)
, the subgroup (
252
) generates
PSL(2,\Bbbk[t^{-1}])
.

7.2.4

Summarizing the above discussion, one can say that for
\mathsf{G}=PGL(2)
,
the action of the
spherical Hecke algebra on functions on
\Bun_{\mathsf{G}}
amounts to the action
of one operator
\Delta
on functions
f(k)
of one variable
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
given by the formula

\left[\Delta f\right](k)=qf(k-1)+f(k+1)\,,\quad f(-1)=f(1)\,.

(253)

This operator is self adjoint with respect to the natural Hermitian
inner
product in
L^{2}(\Bun_{\mathsf{G}})
given by

\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Bun_{\mathsf{G}})}=\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{|f(k)|^{2}}{|\Aut(\mathscr{P}_{k})|}\,,

(254)

which is elementary to see directly using

|\Aut(\mathscr{P}_{k})|=\begin{cases}q(q^{2}-1)\,,&k=0\,,\\
q^{k+1}(q-1)\,,&k>0\,.\end{cases}

(255)

7.3
Spectrum of
\Delta

7.3.1

The operator (
253
) is a second-order
difference operators with constant coefficients. Ignoring the
boundary conditions, we find two eigenfunctions

f_{\pm}(k;a)=\left(q^{1/2}a^{\pm 1}\right)^{k},\quad k\in\mathbb{Z},a\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}\,,

with eigenvalue

\Delta f_{\pm}(k;a)=\lambda(a)\,f_{\pm}(k;a)\,,\quad\lambda(a)=q^{1/2}(a+a^{-1})\,.

(256)

The boundary condition in (
253
) singles out one
particular linear combination of those

\textup{Eis}(k,a)=\left(q^{1/2}a\right)^{k}+\frac{qa^{2}-1}{a^{2}-q}\,\left(q^{1/2}a^{-1}\right)^{k}\,,

(257)

which is the Eisenstein series for
\Bun_{PSL(2)}(\mathbb{P}^{1})
, see below.

7.3.2

By construction,
\textup{Eis}(k,a)
and
\textup{Eis}(k,a^{-1})
are proportional. In
fact, the elementary relation

\textup{Eis}(k,a^{-1})=\frac{a^{2}-q}{qa^{2}-1}\,\textup{Eis}(k,a)

(258)

is the simplest instance of the general functional equation for
Eisenstein series.

7.3.3

In the spectral theory of differential and difference operators,
one views (
257
) as a superposition of a wave and its
reflection by the boundary. One reads off properties of the
system, such as its spectrum, from the properties of the
coefficient in (
257
) as a function of
a
, see e.g.
[
Faddeev
,
Leon
]
. This analysis is
elementary in the case at hand.

7.3.4

Note that none of the functions (
257
) lies in
L^{2}
. Indeed,
for large
k
it grows at least as
q^{k/2}
, making the sum
(
254
) diverge. Also note that

\Res_{a=\pm\sqrt{q}}\textup{Eis}(k,a)\,\frac{da}{a}=\frac{q-q^{-1}}{2}\,(\pm 1)^{k}

are
L^{2}
-eigenfunctions with eigenvalues
\pm(q+1)
. As we will
see momentarily

\Spec(\Delta)=\big[-2\sqrt{q},2\sqrt{q}\big]\cup\{\pm(q+1)\}\,,

(259)

with multiplicity one. The spectral measure on the continuos spectrum in (
259
) is in the Lebesgue measure
equivalence class.

7.3.5

One popular starting point for spectral decompositions is the
identity

f=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{|z|\gg 1}dz\,\frac{1}{\Delta-z}\,f

(260)

valid for any function or vector
f
and a bounded linear operator

\Delta
. The operator
(\Delta-z)^{-1}
in (
260
) is called the
resolvent of the operator
\Delta
. It is analytic for
|z|\gg 1
and
its residue at
z=\infty
is the identity operator, whence
(
260
).

If
\Delta
is self-adjoint then by the abstract spectral theorem
one has the following integral representation for the matrix
coefficients of the resolvent

\left((\Delta-z)^{-1}f_{1},f_{2}\right)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{f_{1}(\lambda)\overline{f_{2}(\lambda)}}{\lambda-z}\,\mu_{\textup{spectral}}(d\lambda)\,.

(261)

Here we identify elements
f_{i}
of our Hilbert space with
functions
9
9

9

in principle, vector-valued, but scalar-valued in our case
on the spectrum of
\Delta
and

\mu_{\textup{spectral}}
is the spectral
measure. It is clear that the atoms of
\mu_{\textup{spectral}}

produce poles in (
261
), while the absolutely continuous
part of
\mu_{\textup{spectral}}
produces a jump across the real
axis in (
261
). The magnitude of this jump is proportional to the
density of
\mu_{\textup{spectral}}
. A concrete spectral decomposition can thus
be obtained by moving the contour of integration in (
260
) so
that to make it encircle the singularities of
(\Delta-z)^{-1}
.

7.3.6

A good set of functions
f
to plug into (
260
) is not the
Eisenstein series themselves, but their linear combinations of the
form

\displaystyle\textup{Eis}_{\omega}(k)

\displaystyle=\oint_{|a|\gg 1}\textup{Eis}(k;a)\,\omega(a)\,\frac{da}{2\pi ia}\,,

(262)

\displaystyle=\oint_{|a|=1}\textup{Eis}(k;a)\,\omega(a)\,\frac{da}{2\pi ia}+\frac{q-q^{-1}}{2}\sum_{\pm}\omega(\pm q^{1/2})\,(\pm 1)^{k}\,,

(263)

where
\omega(a)\in\mathbb{C}[a^{\pm 1}]
.
These are
called pseudo-Eisenstein series in the theory of automorphic forms.
In other situations, they may be called wave packets, etc., that is,
linear combinations of eigenfunctions that decay at infinity sufficiently
rapidly.

Since (
262
) picks out the coefficients of the
a\to\infty

expansion of
\textup{Eis}(k;a)
, it is easy to see that each
\textup{Eis}_{\omega}(k)
is
compactly supported and that the map

\mathbb{C}[a^{\pm 1}]\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt\omega\mapsto\textup{Eis}_{\omega}\hskip 8.19447pt}\,\,\begin{matrix}\textup{compactly supported}\\
\textup{functions on $\Bun_{\mathsf{G}}$}\end{matrix}

(264)

is surjective. The right hand side is dense in
L^{2}(\Bun_{\mathsf{G}})
and
a very useful perspective on the spectral decomposition is to describe

L^{2}(\Bun_{\mathsf{G}})
as the completion of
\mathbb{C}[a^{\pm 1}]
with respect to
the seminorm
\|\textup{Eis}_{\omega}\|_{L^{2}(\Bun_{\mathsf{G}})}
(which includes
quotienting out by the seminorm’s kernel). This seminorm
can be computed
abstractly in great generality, see Section
7.7
.

7.3.7

By construction

\frac{1}{\Delta-z}\,\textup{Eis}_{\omega}=\oint_{|z|\gg|a|\gg 1}\frac{da}{2\pi ia}\,\frac{\omega(a)}{\lambda(a)-z}\,\textup{Eis}(a)\,.

(265)

Moving
z
away from
z=\infty
means shrinking the contour of
integration in (
265
) to the unit circle
|a|=1
as in
(
263
).
Note the function
\lambda(a)
is one-to-one for
|a|>1
and
and two-to-one for
|a|=1
, creating the expected discontinuity of the
resolvent along

\lambda(\{|a|=1\})=\big[-2\sqrt{q},2\sqrt{q}\big]\,.

This proves (
259
).

7.3.8

For
\mathsf{G}=SL(2)
, we need to consider only even
k
and the operator

\Delta^{2}
. This means we will have
b=a^{2}
as the new parameter
for Eisenstein series

\textup{Eis}_{SL(2)}(\mathscr{O}(m)\oplus\mathscr{O}(-m),b)=\left(qb\right)^{m}+\frac{qb-1}{b-q}\,\left(qb^{-1}\right)^{m}\,.

(266)

In particular, the eigenfunctions
(\pm 1)^{k}
get identified, while
the continuous spectrum retains its shape.

7.4
Same spectrum, as seen in the dual group

7.4.1

The interpretation of this spectrum from the point of the general theory
is the following. One considers the Langlands dual group
\mathsf{G}^{\vee}

and identifies, following Satake,

\lambda:\SH_{x}(\mathsf{G})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Z}[q^{\pm 1/2}]\xrightarrow{\hskip 7.97224pt\sim\hskip 7.97224pt}K_{\mathsf{G}^{\vee}}(\pt)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Z}[q^{\pm 1/2}]\,,

where the isomorphism is an isomorphism of algebras. In particular,

PGL(2)^{\vee}=SL(2)\,,

and vice versa, and

\lambda(\Delta)=q^{1/2}\otimes\textup{defining representation
of $SL(2)$}\,.

(267)

To find an agreement with (
256
) above, we observe that

\lambda(a)=q^{1/2}\tr\begin{pmatrix}a\\
&a^{-1}\end{pmatrix}

is the trace in the defining representation of
a
, viewed as a
conjugacy class in
SL(2)
. Viewed from this angle,

\Spec(\SH_{x}(\mathsf{G}))\subset\textup{conjugacy classes in
$\mathsf{G}^{\vee}(\mathbb{C})$}\,.

(268)

7.4.2

We hope the reader will enjoy checking that for both
PGL(2)
and

SL(2)
the spectrum computed above can be described as
follows

\Spec(\SH_{x}(\mathsf{G}))=\bigsqcup_{\pi:SL(2,\mathbb{C})\to\mathsf{G}^{\vee}(\mathbb{C})}\pi\left(\begin{pmatrix}q^{1/2}\\
&q^{-1/2}\end{pmatrix}\right)\cdot\mathsf{G}^{\vee}(\mathbb{C})^{\pi}_{\textup{compact}}

(269)

where the union is over all homomorphisms

\pi
, the group
\mathsf{G}^{\vee}(\mathbb{C})^{\pi}
is the centralizer of the image of

\pi
, and
\mathsf{G}^{\vee}(\mathbb{C})^{\pi}_{\textup{compact}}
is its maximal
compact subgroup.

Indeed, in the case at hand, there are only two possibilities for
\pi
. We have the
trivial representation, which gives

\Spec(\SH_{x}(PGL(2)))_{\textup{continous}}=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}a\\
&a^{-1}\end{pmatrix},|a|=1\right\}\,,

(270)

and the defining
representation which gives

\Spec(\SH_{x}(PGL(2)))_{\textup{discrete}}=\left\{\pm\begin{pmatrix}q^{1/2}\\
&q^{-1/2}\end{pmatrix}\right\}\,.

(271)

For
\mathsf{G}=SL(2)
, one takes the image of (
270
) and
(
271
) in
PGL(2,\mathbb{C})
.

7.4.3

Formula (
269
) admits extremely far-reaching generalizations of the
form

\Spec(\SH_{x}(\mathsf{G}))=\bigsqcup_{\pi:\Gamma\times SL(2,\mathbb{C})\to\mathsf{G}^{\vee}(\mathbb{C})}\pi\left(\Fr_{x}\times\begin{pmatrix}\|\varpi_{x}\|^{1/2}\\
&\|\varpi_{x}\|^{-1/2}\end{pmatrix}\right)\cdot\mathsf{G}^{\vee}(\mathbb{C})^{\pi}_{\textup{compact}}\,,

(272)

where the spectrum is the joint spectrum of the spherical Hecke
algebras for all nonarchimedian
places of a global field
\mathbb{F}
,
\Gamma
is some
version of the unramified Galois group of
\mathbb{F}
,
\Fr_{x}
is the
conjugacy class of the Frobenius element at
x
, and

\|\varpi_{x}\|
is the norm of a uniformizing element of
\mathbb{F}_{x}
.

7.5
Eisenstein series

7.5.1

The space
\Bun_{\mathsf{G}}
depends covariantly on the group
\mathsf{G}
, meaning
that a group homomorphism
\mathsf{G}\to\mathsf{G}^{\prime}
induces a map
\Bun_{\mathsf{G}}\to\Bun_{\mathsf{G}^{\prime}}
. In particular, let
\mathsf{P}\to\mathsf{G}
be an inclusion of a
parabolic subgroup and let
\mathsf{P}\to\mathsf{M}
be the quotient by the unipotent
radical. Both Eisenstein and pseudo-Eisenstein series on
\Bun_{\mathsf{G}}

are produced by the
pull-push
\pi_{\mathsf{G},*}\pi_{\mathsf{M}}^{*}
in the diagram

\textstyle{\Bun_{\mathsf{P}}(C)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{\mathsf{M}}}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{\mathsf{G}}}
\textstyle{\Bun_{\mathsf{M}}(C)}
\textstyle{\Bun_{\mathsf{G}}(C)\,,}

(273)

which appears in many places in our narrative.
Here we have discrete sets with the canonical measure
|\Aut|^{-1}
,
which provides a natural bijection between functions and measures. The
pushforward of a functions is really the pushforward of the
corresponding measure, in other words

\big[\pi_{*}f\big](x)=\sum_{x^{\prime}\in\pi^{-1}(x)}\frac{|\Aut(x\phantom{{}^{\prime}})|}{|\Aut(x^{\prime})|}\,f(x^{\prime})\,.

(274)

By construction,
\pi_{*}
and
\pi^{*}
are transpose with respect to the
natural duality between functions and measures.

7.5.2

In our running example, we have

\mathsf{M}\cong GL(1)=\{z\}\xleftarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt}\mathsf{P}=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}z&*\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\right\}\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt}\mathsf{G}=PGL(2)\,.

(275)

A
\mathsf{P}
-bundle over a curve
C
is an extension

0\to\mathscr{M}\to\mathscr{V}\to\mathscr{O}_{C}\to 0\,,\quad\mathscr{M}\in\Bun_{GL(1)}=\Pic(C)(\Bbbk)\,.

(276)

Up to isomorphism, it is uniquely encoded by the
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle

\mathscr{P}=\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{V})
with a section

s_{0}:C=\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{M})\to\mathscr{P}\,.

We have
\mathscr{M}=s_{0}^{*}(\mathscr{O}_{\mathscr{P}}(-1))
and, in particular,

\deg s_{0}=-\deg\mathscr{M}=(s_{0},s_{0})\,.

(277)

7.5.3

The numerator in (
274
) means that, in the
corresponding count, we
do not
identify sections that
differ by the automorphism of
\mathscr{P}
.
In other
words, every one of the the
\frac{|\Aut(\mathscr{P})|}{|\Aut(\mathscr{P},s)|}
many
point in each
\Aut(\mathscr{P})
-orbit in

\left[\pi_{\mathsf{G},*}\pi_{\mathsf{M}}^{*}\,\phi\right](\mathscr{P})=\sum_{\textup{sections $s:C\to\mathscr{P}$}}\phi(s^{*}\mathscr{O}(-1))\,,

(278)

contributes the same value
\phi(s^{*}\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(1))
. Here
\phi
is an
arbitrary function on

\Pic(C)(\Bbbk)=\mathbb{Z}\oplus\Pic_{0}(\mathbb{C})(\Bbbk)\,,

(279)

where the
\mathbb{Z}
-coordinate is given by (
277
).
In our running example
C=\mathbb{P}^{1}
, the second summand in (
279
) is zero.

7.5.4

As we will see momentarily, choosing
\phi(\deg s)
to be compactly
supported gives pseudo-Eisenstein series on

\Bun_{PGL(2)}
. Equivalently, we will check that taking

\phi(\mathscr{M})=\left(q^{1/2}a\right)^{\deg\mathscr{M}}

(280)

recovers (
257
), which means that

\textup{Eis}(\mathscr{P},a)=\sum_{\textup{sections $s:C\to\mathscr{P}$}}\left(q^{1/2}a\right)^{-\deg(s)}\,.

(281)

The shift by
q^{1/2}
is the shift by

q^{\rho}
where
\rho=1/2
is the half-sum of weights of
\mathsf{M}
in
the unipotent radical of
\mathsf{P}
.

7.5.5

It is obvious from (
247
) that the right-hand side of
(
281
) is
an eigenfunctions of
\Delta
with eigenvalue
\lambda(a)
.
Further, the normalization is correct since
\mathscr{P}_{k}
contains
a unique section (
248
) of degree
-k
. Thus, (
281
)
is established.

Nonetheless, it will in instructive to do the actual counting.
Actually, we will do
explicitly a middle-school version of the counting we
did just slightly more abstractly in Section
5
.

7.6
Counting quasisections

7.6.1

We
have
\mathscr{P}_{k}\setminus s_{\infty}\cong\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(k)
and hence
all other sections can be identified with rational sections of

\mathscr{O}(k)
. In other words, they have the form

s(t)=\frac{F_{d+k}(t)}{G_{d}(t)},\quad d=0,1,2,\dots\,,

where
F_{d+k}
and
G_{d}
are polynomials of the indicated
degree. The degree of this section is
k+2d
.

7.6.2

One way to count these, is to first count pairs
(F_{d+k},G_{d})

with
G_{d}\neq 0
up to an overall constant
multiple, ignoring the fact that they may have a common nonconstant
factor. The corresponding pairs
(F_{d+k}(t),G_{d}(t))
describe
quasisections of
\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(k)
, see Section
5
. They
form a complement to a linear subspace in a projective space of dimension

2d+k+1
. Hence we get the count

\sum_{d\geq 0}\left(q^{1/2}a\right)^{-k-2d}\frac{q^{2d+k+2}-q^{d+k+1}}{q-1}=\frac{q}{(1-qa^{-2})(1-a^{-2})}\,\left(q^{1/2}a^{-1}\right)^{k}\,.

(282)

For the common multiple up to a constant factor, we similarly get the
count

\sum_{d\geq 0}\left(q^{1/2}a\right)^{-2d}\frac{q^{d+1}-1}{q-1}=\frac{1}{(1-a^{-2})(1-q^{-1}a^{-2})}\,.

(283)

7.6.3

By definition, counting nonzero polynomials up to a constant factor is
the same as counting closed points of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
. This explains why the
\zeta
-function

\zeta_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(t)=\frac{1}{(1-t)(1-qt)}

(284)

appears in the answer. Introducing the symmetrized version of (
284
),
also known as the completed
\zeta
-function

\xi_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(t)=\frac{q^{1/2}t}{(1-t)(1-qt)}\,,

(285)

we can write the ratio of (
282
) and (
283
) as
follows

\textup{Eis}(\mathscr{P}_{k},a)=\left(q^{1/2}a\right)^{k}+\frac{\xi(a^{-2})}{\xi(a^{2})}\left(q^{1/2}a^{-1}\right)^{k}\,.

(286)

Eisenstein series for
C=\mathbb{P}^{1}
are proportional to the their constant
terms, see (
287
) below. The formula (
286
) is thus
equivalent to the simplest instance of Langlands’ constant term
formula.

7.7
Constant term and
L^{2}
-norms

7.7.1

By definition, the constant term

\CT=\pi_{\mathsf{M},*}\pi_{\mathsf{G}}^{*}

is the transposed operator in the diagram (
273
).
For
C=\mathbb{P}^{1}
and
\deg\mathscr{M}\geq-1
, any extension in (
276
)
is trivial, thus

\CT(\phi)(\mathscr{M})=\frac{1}{q^{\deg\mathscr{M}+1}}\,\phi(\mathscr{M}\oplus\mathscr{O})\,,\quad\deg\mathscr{M}\geq-1\,.

(287)

The prefactor in (
287
) comes from the ratio

\frac{|\Aut(\mathscr{M})|}{|\Aut(\mathscr{M}\subset\mathscr{M}\oplus\mathscr{O})|}=\frac{1}{|H^{0}(\mathscr{M})|}=\frac{1}{q^{\deg\mathscr{M}+1}}\,.

in formula (
274
).
Thus

\CT(\textup{Eis}(a))(\mathscr{O}(k))=\frac{1}{q}\left[\left(q^{-1/2}a\right)^{k}+\frac{\xi(a^{-2})}{\xi(a^{2})}\left(q^{-1/2}a^{-1}\right)^{k}\right]\,.

(288)

Langlands gave a very general formulation and very general proof of
such constant term formulas in
[
L
]
, further investigated by Harder
in
[
Harder
]
and many other authors.

7.7.2

For a general curve
C
and Eisenstein series associated
to
\mathsf{G}/\mathsf{B}
, where
\mathsf{G}
is a split reductive group and
\mathsf{B}\subset\mathsf{G}
is the Borel subgroup, formula (
288
) generalizes as follows. In this case

\mathsf{M}
is the maximal torus of
\mathsf{G}
, hence

\Bun_{\mathsf{M}}=\Cochars(\mathsf{M})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\Pic(C)\xrightarrow{\hskip 7.97224pt\deg\hskip 7.97224pt}\Cochars(\mathsf{M})\,.

The variable
a
in the formula

\phi(\mathscr{M})=(q^{\rho}a)^{\deg\mathscr{M}}

(289)

should thus be interpreted
as an element of the dual torus

a\in\mathsf{M}^{\vee}=\Chars(\mathsf{M})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{C}^{\times}\,.

The Weyl group
W
acts on functions of
a
, such as (
289
)
in the natural way. Consider the operator

\displaystyle\bm{\Sigma}

\displaystyle=\sum_{w\in W}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha>0\\
w^{-1}\alpha<0\end{subarray}}\frac{\xi(a^{-\alpha})}{\xi(a^{\alpha})}\,w\,,

(290)

\displaystyle=\bm{\Pi}^{-1}\left(\sum_{w\in W}w\right)\bm{\Pi}

(291)

where
\bm{\Pi}=\prod_{\alpha>0}\xi(a^{-\alpha})
. It
follows from (
291
) that

\bm{\Sigma}^{2}=|W|\bm{\Sigma}\,,

(292)

which means that
\Sigma
is proportional to a projector when
acting on rational functions of
a
. With these notations,

\CT(\textup{Eis}(\,\cdot\,))\,\propto\,\bm{\Sigma}

(293)

when acting on functions of the form (
289
). For functions

\phi
that involve a nontrivial character of
\Pic_{0}(C)(\Bbbk)
, the function

\xi
in (
290
) should be replaced by the corresponding
Dirichlet L-function.

7.7.3

One immediate application of constant term formulas is a
formula of the norm
\|\textup{Eis}_{\omega}\|_{L^{2}(\Bun_{\mathsf{G}})}
of a
pseudo-Eisenstein series. This is because we can
we can express norms on
\Bun_{\mathsf{G}}
in terms of norms on
\Bun_{\mathsf{M}}

using the relation

\left(\pi_{\mathsf{G},*}\pi_{\mathsf{M}}^{*}\right)^{*}=\CT.

(294)

The space
\Bun_{\mathsf{M}}
for
\mathsf{M}=GL(1)
is just
\mathbb{Z}
, each point of which has
the automorphism group
GL(1,\Bbbk)
of order
q-1
. We can
go between functions

\widehat{\omega}:\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{C}

and functions
\omega(a)
on
\mathbb{C}^{\times}=\mathsf{M}^{\vee}
by means of the Fourier
transform, which we normalize by

\omega(a)=\sum_{k}\widehat{\omega}(k)\,(q^{1/2}a)^{-k}\,.

(295)

The shift in (
295
) is introduced so that there is no shift
in

\textup{Eis}_{\omega}=\pi_{\mathsf{G},*}\pi_{\mathsf{M}}^{*}\,\,\widehat{\omega}(\deg(\,\cdot\,))\,.

(296)

We have

\|\widehat{\omega}\|_{L^{2}(\Bun_{\mathsf{M}})}=\tfrac{1}{q-1}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}|\widehat{\omega}(k)|^{2}\,\,,

(297)

where the prefactor accounts for the automorphisms.

7.7.4

Formulas (
295
) and (
297
) imply

\Big((q^{-1/2}a)^{\deg},\widehat{\omega}\Big)_{L^{2}(\Bun_{\mathsf{M}})}=\tfrac{1}{q-1}\,\bar{\omega}(a^{-1})\,,

(298)

where
\bar{\omega}(a)=\overline{\omega(\overline{a})}
.
From this, (
294
) and (
296
), we conclude

\Big(\textup{Eis}(a),\textup{Eis}_{\omega}\Big)_{L^{2}(\Bun_{\mathsf{G}})}=\Big(\!\CT\textup{Eis}(a),\widehat{\omega}\Big)_{L^{2}(\Bun_{\mathsf{M}})}=\tfrac{1}{q(q-1)}\bm{\Sigma}\,\bar{\omega}(a^{-1})\,,

where

\bm{\Sigma}\,\omega(a)=\omega(a)+\frac{\xi(a^{-2})}{\xi(a^{2})}\omega(a^{-1})\,.

(299)

It follows that

\|\textup{Eis}_{\omega}\|_{L^{2}(\Bun_{\mathsf{G}})}=\tfrac{1}{q(q-1)}\oint_{|a|\gg 1}\frac{da}{2\pi ia}\,\omega(a)\bm{\Sigma}\,\bar{\omega}(a^{-1})\,.

(300)

7.7.5

Moving the contour of integration in (
300
) as in
(
263
), we obtain

\displaystyle\|\textup{Eis}_{\omega}\|_{L^{2}(\Bun_{\mathsf{G}})}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{q(q-1)}\oint_{|a|=1}\frac{da}{2\pi ia}\,\omega(a)\bm{\Sigma}\,\overline{\omega(a)}

\displaystyle+r_{1}|\omega(q^{1/2})|^{2}+r_{2}|\omega(-q^{1/2})|^{2}\,,

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\tfrac{1}{2q(q-1)}\,\,\,\Big\|\overline{\bm{\Sigma}}\,\omega\Big\|^{2}_{L^{2}(|a|=1)}}_{\textup{continuous spectrum}}

\displaystyle+\underbrace{r_{1}|\omega(q^{1/2})|^{2}+r_{2}|\omega(-q^{1/2})|^{2}}_{\textup{discrete spectrum}}\,,

(301)

where the coefficients
r_{1},r_{2}>0
are obtained from the residues of

\bm{\Sigma}
at
a=\pm q^{1/2}
, and where in (
301
)
we used the fact that
\frac{1}{2}\bm{\Sigma}
is a self-adjoint
projector acting on
L^{2}(|a|=1)
.

7.7.6

Let us now go back to the discussion in Section
7.3.6
. Formula
(
301
) describes a seminorm on
\mathbb{C}[a^{\pm 1}]
. Its three terms
involve values of
\omega
at disjoint closed sets, hence the
completion
L^{2}(\Bun_{\mathsf{G}})

splits into a direct sum of two 1-dimensional eigenspaces and
their orthogonal complement. The latter is a direct integral of
Eisenstein series
\textup{Eis}(a)
for
a
on the unit circle
|a|=1
modulo

a\mapsto a^{-1}
. The
a\mapsto a^{-1}
quotient appears as
the quotient by the kernel of the spectral
projector
\frac{1}{2}\overline{\bm{\Sigma}}
. It reflects
the functional equation for
\textup{Eis}(a)
.

7.8
A geometric interpretation

7.8.1

To conclude this Appendix, we want to explain our geometric
point of view on spectral decomposition in the
running example. The relevant geometric object is yet another
projective line

\mathbb{P}^{1}=\mathsf{G}^{\vee}/\mathsf{B}^{\vee}\,,

which is now interpreted as the flag variety for the Langlands
dual group. It has the natural action of

\mathsf{M}^{\vee}=\{\diag(a^{-1},a)\}\subset\mathsf{G}^{\vee}\,.

In fact, the group
\mathsf{M}^{\vee}
will play two roles because it both
acts on
\mathbb{P}^{1}
and is also the Levi subgroup of the group

\mathsf{B}^{\vee}
which appears in the quotient construction of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
.
To account for these two roles, one can consider quotient stacks like

\mathsf{M}^{\vee}\backslash\mathsf{G}^{\vee}/\mathsf{B}^{\vee}
or even
\mathsf{B}^{\vee}\backslash\mathsf{G}^{\vee}/\mathsf{B}^{\vee}
, in which
\mathsf{M}^{\vee}
appears on the left and
on the right on an equal footing.
This results in the
identification

K_{\mathsf{M}^{\vee}}\!(\mathbb{P}^{1})=K_{\mathsf{B}^{\vee}}\!(\mathbb{P}^{1})=K(\mathsf{B}^{\vee}\backslash\mathsf{G}^{\vee}/\mathsf{B}^{\vee})=\dots=\mathbb{Z}[\mathsf{M}^{\vee}]\boxtimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\mathsf{M}^{\vee}]^{W}}\mathbb{Z}[\mathsf{M}^{\vee}]\,,

(302)

where dots denote many other possible geometric realizations of
this algebra, the Weyl group
W\cong S(2)
acts by
a\mapsto a^{\pm 1}
, and the
\boxtimes
symbol is used to distinguish this
tensor product from the tensor product in equivariant K-theory (which
is the algebra product in (
302
)). The
identification may be fixed so that

\displaystyle a\boxtimes 1

\displaystyle=\textup{character $a$}\otimes\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}\,,

\displaystyle 1\boxtimes a

\displaystyle=\mathscr{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(1)\,.

We will write elements of
K_{\mathsf{M}^{\vee}}\!(\mathbb{P}^{1})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{C}
in
the form
\omega_{1}(a)\boxtimes\omega_{2}(a)
.

7.8.2

There is a distinguished linear functional
\int_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}
, given by the horizontal
arrow in the following diagram

\textstyle{K_{\mathsf{M}^{\vee}}\!(\mathbb{P}^{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hskip 8.19447pt\int_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}=(\mathsf{M}^{\vee}\backslash\mathbb{P}^{1}\to\pt)_{*}\hskip 8.19447pt}
\scriptstyle{\chi}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}\,,}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}[\mathsf{M}^{\vee}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
invariants

(303)

where
\chi
is the Euler characteristic, equivalently pushforward to

\pt/\mathsf{M}^{\vee}
. It may be computed by the localization formula in equivariant
K-theory, or the Weyl character formula for
SL(2)
, or the formula
for the sum of a geometric progression. The result is

\int_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}\omega_{1}(a)\boxtimes\omega_{2}(a)=\int_{|a|=1}\frac{da}{2\pi ia}\,\omega_{1}(a)\left(\sum_{w\in W}w\right)\frac{\omega_{2}(a)}{1-a^{-2}}\,.

(304)

Since the symmetrization eliminates the poles in
(
304
), the integration contour
|a|=1

may be moved arbitrarily.

7.8.3

Consider the cotangent bundle

\mathsf{X}^{\vee}=T^{*}\mathbb{P}^{1}=T^{*}(\mathsf{G}^{\vee}/\mathsf{B}^{\vee})\,.

Using the identification

K_{\mathsf{M}^{\vee}}\!(\mathbb{P}^{1})=K_{\mathsf{M}^{\vee}}\!(\mathsf{X}^{\vee})\,,

one may similarly consider pushing forward along
\mathsf{X}^{\vee}\to\pt
.
Since this pushforward is not proper, a certain care needs to be
exercised. Concretely, let
\mathbf{L}\subset\mathsf{X}^{\vee}
be the set of
points that have a limit under the action of
a
as
a\to 0
. This is
the union of the zero section
\mathbb{P}^{1}\subset T^{*}\mathbb{P}^{1}
and the cotangent
fiber over one of the fixed
points. Let
\chi_{\mathbf{L}}
denote Euler characteristic with support in

\mathbf{L}
. It takes values in formal Laurent series in
a^{-1}
.
Consider the diagram

\textstyle{K_{\mathbb{C}^{\times}_{q}\times\mathsf{M}^{\vee}}\!(\mathsf{X}^{\vee})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hskip 8.19447pt\int_{\mathsf{X}^{\!\vee}}\hskip 8.19447pt}
\scriptstyle{\chi_{\mathbf{L}}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}[q^{\pm 1}]\,,}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}[q^{\pm 1}][a,a^{-1}]\!]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
invariants

(305)

in which we enlarged the equivariance by making
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
with coordinate

q
act by scaling the cotangent directions with weight
q^{-1}
.
Again, by localization or geometric series, we compute

\int_{\mathsf{X}^{\!\vee}}\omega_{1}(a)\boxtimes\omega_{2}(a)=\int_{|a|\gg 1}\frac{da}{2\pi ia}\,\omega_{1}(a)\left(\sum_{w\in W}w\right)\frac{\omega_{2}(a)}{(1-a^{-2})(1-qa^{2})}\,.

(306)

Note the similarity between the weight here and the
\zeta
-function
(
284
) of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
.

7.8.4

Now consider the following Hamiltonian reduction of
\mathsf{X}^{\vee}

\mathscr{T}=T^{*}(\mathsf{B}^{\vee}\backslash\mathsf{G}^{\vee}/\mathsf{B}^{\vee})=\mathsf{B}^{\vee}\backslash\mu_{\mathsf{B}^{\vee}}^{\!-1}(0)\,,

where

\mu_{\mathsf{B}^{\vee}}:\mathsf{X}^{\vee}\to\Lie(\mathsf{B}^{\vee})^{*}\,,

is the moment map for the action of
\mathsf{B}^{\vee}
. The
map
\mu_{\mathsf{B}^{\vee}}

is equivariant for the action of
\mathbb{C}^{\times}_{q}\times\mathsf{M}^{\vee}
provided

\mathbb{C}^{\times}_{q}
scales the target with weight
q^{-1}
. Again, we have
the identification

K_{\mathsf{M}^{\vee}}\!(\mathbb{P}^{1})=K(\mathscr{T})\,,

and we would like to define a linear functional
\int_{\mathscr{T}}
as before.
Since the map
\mathscr{T}\to\pt
is not proper, this does require an
explanation, but the explanation is easier than the one for

\int_{\mathsf{X}^{\vee}}
.

We can choose
\mathsf{B}^{\vee}\supset\mathsf{M}^{\vee}
so that
\mathsf{M}^{\vee}
acts
with weight
a^{2}
on its unipotent radical
\mathsf{U}^{\vee}\subset\mathsf{B}^{\vee}
. Importantly, the points
in the unipotent radical have a limit as
a\to 0
. This means that
one of the two equations in
\mu_{\mathsf{B}^{\vee}}=0
defines
the Lagrangian
\mathbf{L}
. Therefore, the Euler characteristic
is well-defined as an element of

\mathbb{Z}[q^{\pm 1}][a,a^{-1}]\!]
.

The equation
\mu_{\mathsf{B}^{\vee}}=0
cuts the Euler class of a trivial
vector
bundle with a nontrivial group action. Similarly, the
Chevalley-Eilenberg complex for
\mathsf{U}^{\vee}
-invariants cuts out
the Euler class of a trivial line bundle with a nontrivial weight.
This gives

\int_{\mathscr{T}}\omega_{1}(a)\boxtimes\omega_{2}(a)=\int_{\mathsf{X}^{\!\vee}}\mathscr{E}\omega_{1}(a)\boxtimes\omega_{2}(a)\,,

(307)

where
\mathscr{E}
is the Euler class

\mathscr{E}=\underbrace{(1-q)(1-qa^{2})}_{\mu_{\mathsf{B}^{\vee}}=0}\underbrace{(1-a^{-2})}_{\textup{$\mathsf{U}^{\vee}$-invariants}}\,.

This means

\int_{\mathscr{T}}\omega_{1}(a)\boxtimes\omega_{2}(a)={\scriptstyle{(1-q)}}\int_{|a|\gg 1}\frac{da}{2\pi ia}\,\left(\omega_{1}(a)\omega_{2}(a)+\frac{\xi(a^{-2})}{\xi(a^{2})}\,\omega_{1}(a)\omega_{2}(a^{-1})\right)\,,

(308)

Comparing this with (
300
), we see that

\left(\textup{Eis}_{\omega_{1}},\textup{Eis}_{\omega_{2}}\right)_{L^{2}(\Bun_{\mathsf{G}})}\propto\int_{\mathscr{T}}\omega_{1}(a)\boxtimes\omega^{*}_{2}(a)\,,

(309)

where

\omega^{*}(a)=\bar{\omega}(a^{-1})\,.

(310)

The proportionality constant in (
309
) is interesting
and has to do with volumes of
\Bun_{\mathsf{G}}
and other things. It is, however, immaterial
for the spectral decomposition.

7.8.5

Now, how does one see the spectral decomposition and specifically
(
269
) from this ? For any reductive group
\mathsf{G}^{\vee}
, the moment
map

\mu_{\mathsf{G}^{\vee}}:T^{*}(\mathsf{G}^{\vee}/\mathsf{B}^{\vee})\to\Lie(\mathsf{G}^{\vee})^{*}\cong\Lie(\mathsf{G}^{\vee})

(311)

is the resolution of the cone of nilpotent elements in

\Lie(\mathsf{G}^{\vee})
. The fibers of this map are called Springer fibers.
The map (
311
) induces a map

\bm{\mu}:\mathscr{T}\to\Lie(\mathsf{G}^{\vee})/\mathsf{G}^{\vee}\,,

(312)

the fibers of which are Cartesian squares of the Springer
fibers.

Consider the diagram

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 16.5347pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-16.5347pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathscr{T}\big/\mathbb{C}^{\times}_{q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 153.73343pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 34.99312pt\raise-21.93748pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.8264pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\bm{\mu}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 61.0037pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern 82.13406pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 153.73343pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\pt\big/\mathbb{C}^{\times}_{q}}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise-33.5pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 40.5347pt\raise-33.5pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\Lie(\mathsf{G}^{\vee})\big/(\mathbb{C}^{\times}_{q}\times\mathsf{G}^{\vee})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 153.73343pt\raise-6.73624pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\,.

(313)

The pushforward
\bm{\mu}_{*}
in (
313
) gives a K-theory class
on the stack of nilpotent conjugacy
classes
e
for the dual group
\mathsf{G}^{\vee}
. These
correspond to representations
\pi
in (
269
)
via

e=\pi\left(\!\left(\begin{matrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{matrix}\right)\!\right)\,.

(314)

We have

\textup{Orbit}(e)\big/(\mathbb{C}^{\times}_{q}\times\mathsf{G}^{\vee})\cong\pt\big/(\mathbb{C}^{\times}_{q}\times\mathsf{C}(e))\,,

(315)

where
\mathsf{C}(e)
is the centralizer of
e
and
q\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}_{q}
acts by

\pi(\diag(q^{1/2},q^{-1/2}))
in the fiber over
e
. We see
this action of
q
explicitly in (
269
). Restricted to
(
315
), the northeast
going map in (
313
) is the integration over the maximal
compact subgroup of the complex group
\mathsf{C}(e)
. This
group coincides with
\mathsf{G}^{\vee}(\mathbb{C})^{\pi}_{\textup{compact}}
in
(
269
). This identifies the spectrum as a set of conjugacy
classes in
\mathsf{G}^{\vee}
.

Further analysis of (
313
)
describes spectral projectors as pushforwards along the Spinger
fibers and computes the spectral measure from the

\mathbb{C}^{\times}_{q}\times\mathsf{C}(e)_{\textup{compact}}
-action on the
unipotent radical of
\mathsf{C}(e)
. See
[
27
]
for many
technical details required for the complete match. One key
property that needs to be checked is that the pieces corresponding
to each
e
are positive in the same way as
(
301
) is a sum of orthogonal seminorms. Here the
Hermitian structure is introduced by (
310
).
It would be
interesting to have an abstract argument showing such positivity.
